**2026-11-10 — copying between files: `os.copy_file_range`, `os.sendfile`, `os.chmod`, `os.fchmod` (C-7-59).** The four are the posix prelude's over three primitives. `copy_file_range` and `sendfile` call the kernel's, again after a signal, with CPython's parsing (a count that is negative is a ValueError before any call; an offset of None means the descriptor's own position, which then moves). `chmod` is `os_chmod_impl`'s three ways: `fchmod` for a descriptor (whatever `dir_fd` or `follow_symlinks` say, as CPython), `fchmodat` under a directory's descriptor or for the link itself, `chmod` otherwise; where the system cannot change a link's own mode it is CPython's NotImplementedError, or its ValueError when `dir_fd` was given too. With them `shutil.copyfile`, `copyfileobj`, `copymode` and `copy` run as CPython's do, through the fast paths. `shutil.copy2` now stops at `os.utime`, which with the extended attributes (`copystat`) is the next piece.

**2026-11-10 — `os.utime` and the extended attributes; `shutil`'s `copystat`, `copy2`, `copytree` and `move` (C-7-60).** `os.utime` is `os_utime_impl`: the two times as seconds, converted as `pytime_object_to_denominator` converts them in 3.15 (what has `__index__` is whole seconds; anything else goes through `PyFloat_AsDouble`, so a Decimal or a Fraction is a time and a str is "must be real number"; a float is rounded down to the nanosecond), or as nanoseconds split by the object's own `divmod` by a billion, or left to be now; its refusals of both forms at once, and of a descriptor beside `dir_fd` or `follow_symlinks=False`; and one system call for every way (`futimens` for a descriptor, `utimensat` otherwise, with `AT_SYMLINK_NOFOLLOW` for the link itself). `listxattr`, `getxattr`, `setxattr` and `removexattr` are the prelude's over one primitive, by path, by descriptor or for a link itself, with CPython's two buffer sizes. So `shutil.copystat`, `copy2`, `copytree` and `move` run as CPython's do, with times, modes and extended attributes carried over. The case keeps its times between 1901 and 2038 and reads none back after a file was opened, since what a file system can hold beyond that, and whether reading moves the time of access, differ from one to the next; the extended attributes are compared in a test, on one file system for both sides. Still absent in `os` and stopping by name: `truncate`, `ftruncate`, `chown` and the rest of the list in `runtime/prelude/posix.py`.

**2026-11-10 — review R20 (RL-424 to RL-428): the confinement of the dry run, third pass.** R20 (gemini-3.8-flash) found three more ways through, and one refusal too many. While the tool wrote its artefact a counter let everything through, so a thread the program had started could retry its write until it landed in that window: the tool's own writing is now recognised by what it is (its own function, on the main thread, opening or renaming one of its own two files). `os.mkfifo` and `os.mknod` raise no audit event, which no hook can refuse: every function of `Modules/posixmodule.c` was read for its event, and those that reach beyond the process without a descriptor opened for writing first (`mkfifo`, `mknod`, `setpriority`, the three `sched_set` functions, `tcsetpgrp`; and `signal.pidfd_send_signal`) are replaced by refusals when the program's code starts. `os.lockf` with `F_TEST` is a question and passes, as does giving a lock up; `fcntl.flock`, `fcntl.lockf`, `syslog.syslog`, `shutil.chown` and `webbrowser.open` joined the list. Not taken: `signal.pthread_kill`, which signals a thread of the dry run's own process, and the loss of buffered output at `os._exit`, which was not reproduced (the streams flushed are the interpreter's own, which `main` puts back on every way out). Added on the way: the dry run's standard input is the null device, so a body that reads descriptor 0 does not take the build's terminal. Three reviews in one day found holes in this hook, each a different kind (when it applies, what it sees, what counts): the document says in so many words that it is a guard for honest programs and no sandbox, and an OS-level one stays the answer if untrusted programs are ever built.

**2026-11-10 — review R20, the other reviewer's probe (RL-429): `os` converts its arguments in the order of its parameters (C-7-61).** The round had two reviewers and the worktree kept one report (the second took the first's file name), but the first reviewer's five probes were still there with the outputs of both sides, and one differed: `os.getxattr` and `os.removexattr` asked `follow_symlinks` for its truth before `attribute` for its path, so a program in which the one changes what the other gives read and removed another attribute than under CPython. Argument Clinic converts in the order of the parameters, and the function's own checks (a descriptor with `follow_symlinks=False`) come after all conversions; the prelude now does the same in the four functions of the extended attributes. That the rest of `os` already did was measured before the fix and is now a case: C-7-61 records the order for `stat`, `lstat`, `mkdir`, `access`, `readlink`, `symlink`, `link`, `rename`, `replace`, `open`, `chmod`, `fchmod`, `copy_file_range`, `sendfile`, `utime`, `unlink`, `remove` and `rmdir`, by position and by name, where a conversion fails, what a function converts itself after the Clinic's (the times of `utime`; the offset of `sendfile` after its count), and an object that is a number and a path at once (a descriptor wherever one is allowed). The attributes themselves stay in a same-machine test, since a file system may lack them. The case gives `dir_fd` only to the functions that have it; `mkdir`, `access`, `readlink`, `symlink`, `link`, `rename` and `replace` stop by name on it and are queued in `TODO.md`. A lesson for the review procedure, recorded in the log: two reviewers in one worktree must write to different files, and a worktree is read through (probes too) before it is removed.

**2026-11-10 — the ResourceWarning of a file dropped open (C-7-62).** `_io.FileIO` now issues it, as `fileio_dealloc_warn` does: for a file that owns an open descriptor, about the object the program dropped (the text or buffered file above it, or itself), charged to the line that dropped it. The three close functions reach it as the C's do, which is what a subclass can and cannot change: `FileIO.close` calls the C function itself (a subclass's `_dealloc_warn` is not asked), a buffered file's close calls its own `_dealloc_warn`, which asks the raw file's by attribute, and a text file's close asks its buffer's by attribute; `TextIOWrapper` has no `_dealloc_warn` of its own in CPython and the prelude's is gone. A warning the filters made an error is reported as unraisable about the raw file ("Exception ignored while finalizing file ..."), and the file is closed all the same. **On the way out** the C needs no import to warn, and an import statement fails by then as CPython's does, so the preludes that finalize something left open (`_io`, and `posix` for a scandir iterator) go through a primitive of the runtime, which asks the `_warnings` module as it stands; one never created has CPython's own filters, which ignore a ResourceWarning, and nothing is done. `_warnings` no longer imports `warnings` once the interpreter is finalizing (`get_warnings_attr`), finds `sys.modules` empty, and prints the warning itself: `show_warning`, the C's own printing, is implemented (a write for each part of the line; the source line it is given, written whole; the line it would read from the file is nothing for a name in angle brackets and nothing on the way out, where its import of `io` fails; elsewhere it stops by name). So a file a module still holds when the program ends gives CPython's `<sys>:0: ResourceWarning: unclosed file ...` on every way out, whatever `showwarning` the program had put in, as CPython does for a module with a function in it. Found on the way: `FileIO`'s close after an error (in `close` and in `__init__`) raised the second error `from None`, suppressing a context that `_PyErr_ChainExceptions1` only sets; it is the plain context now. **Three differences remain, all of the order at exit (D-07, D-04):** a file dropped by a Python `__del__` that itself runs at exit is charged to `<sys>:0`, not to the `__del__`'s line, because the finalizers of what the namespaces reach each run in their own turn; several files open at exit are named in another order; and a namespace that a hook stored in `sys` keeps alive past the standard streams warns here and is silent in CPython, which has no stream left by then. **Open with the author:** beneath a warning that has a source CPython's default hook prints "Enable tracemalloc to get the object allocation traceback" when `tracemalloc` can be imported; it is excluded (E-03) and the manifest leaves it out of `warnings`' imports, so the compiled program takes the hook's other path and has no such line. The tests compare without it and check that it is absent; `docs/deviations.toml` (the omitted modules) does not list it yet. The case passes compiled and under eval2; eval1 runs on the host's `_io`, whose warning is charged to the interpreter's own frame, so the case is not in its baseline.

**2026-11-10 — the two RuntimeWarnings of `io` and `os` (C-7-63).** Three stops by name are warnings now, since the `_warnings` prelude can be reached from another prelude: `open` of a binary file with `buffering=1` warns that line buffering is not supported and gives the file with the default buffer, after its checks of the mode and of the encoding and before anything is opened; and a bool given as a file descriptor warns in `FileIO` (so in `open`), in `os` wherever `_fd_converter` runs (a descriptor where a path may stand: `stat`, `listdir`, `chdir`, `scandir`; and `dir_fd`) and in `PyObject_AsFileDescriptor` (`fsync`), but not where a function takes a plain int (`fstat`, `isatty`, `lseek`, `read`, `write`), nor for a subclass of int, an `__index__` or a `fileno()` that gives a bool. Each is `PyErr_WarnEx(RuntimeWarning, text, 1)`: charged to the calling line, and when the filters make it an error it is raised there, before the descriptor is used or a file made. The case passes compiled and under eval2; under eval1 the host's `_io` charges the interpreter's own frame, as with C-7-62.

**2026-11-10 — `dir_fd` for the functions of `os` that name an entry (C-7-64).** `os.mkdir`, `rename`, `replace`, `readlink`, `symlink` and `link` take a directory's descriptor for each name they are given, and `os.access` takes one with `effective_ids` and `follow_symlinks`; all seven stopped by name on them. Each is the `at` call CPython makes (`mkdirat`, `renameat`, `readlinkat`, `symlinkat`, `linkat`, and `faccessat` when any of access's three is given, `access` otherwise), with `None` for the working directory, so a relative name is looked up under the descriptor and an absolute one is not; the OSError carries the names as they were given, both for the functions of two names. `os.link` follows a link unless told not to, as `linkat` with `AT_SYMLINK_FOLLOW` does on this platform. With this C-7-61 (the order in which `os` converts its arguments) has the rows it had to leave out: `dir_fd` after the mode, the two descriptors of `rename` and `link` after both names, a failing conversion ending it there. The case keeps away from what depends on who runs it: it asks only for what an owner and root are both given or both refused, and makes nothing relative to the root directory.

**2026-11-11 — the functions of `os` that work on a descriptor (C-7-65).** Nineteen that stopped by name are system calls now: `dup` (`F_DUPFD_CLOEXEC`: a new descriptor is not inherited), `dup2` (`dup3` with `O_CLOEXEC` when it is not to be inherited, which is why the same number twice is refused then and allowed otherwise), `pipe` (`pipe2` with `O_CLOEXEC`) and `pipe2`, `get_inheritable` and `set_inheritable` (whose flag is an int, not a truth), `get_blocking` and `set_blocking` (`FIONBIO`, as `_Py_set_blocking`), `closerange` (`close_range`, errors ignored), `fchdir` and `fdatasync` (a descriptor or an object with `fileno`, as `fsync`), `sync`, `truncate` (by name, or by a descriptor as `ftruncate`) and `ftruncate`, `pread` and `pwrite`, `lockf`, `posix_fallocate` and `posix_fadvise` (which give their error as the result, not in `errno`). Each takes by position only what CPython's takes so, converts in the order of its parameters, and raises an OSError that carries a name only where the function was given one. Writing the case showed a trap worth keeping: a descriptor's number that was closed is handed out again by the next open, so "a closed descriptor" must be a number the program is never given (the case uses one far above any it opens). Still stops by name in `os`: the process functions (`fork`, the `exec` and `wait` families, `kill`, `system`), ownership (`chown`, `fchown`, `lchown`), the ids and groups beyond the four the runtime has, `statvfs`, `sysconf`, the vectors (`readv`, `writev`) and the rest of `posix`'s 106 names without a body.

**2026-11-11 — the functions of `os` that only compute (C-7-66).** Twelve more that stopped by name: the eight readings of a wait status (`WIFEXITED`, `WEXITSTATUS`, `WIFSIGNALED`, `WTERMSIG`, `WCOREDUMP`, `WIFSTOPPED`, `WSTOPSIG`, `WIFCONTINUED`), `waitstatus_to_exitcode`, and `major`, `minor` and `makedev`. They are the C library's own macros and functions behind two primitives, not their definitions written out again, so what a status or a device number means stays the platform's. A device number converts as `_Py_Dev_Converter` does (64 bits unsigned, with -1 for `NODEV` going in and coming out, and `major_minor_conv` giving -1 for the unsigned int that is `NODEV`'s). The case compares every status of two bytes, 65,536 of them, by sums, and the messages are compared on one machine with those of C-7-65's functions. `posix` has 94 names without a body left.

**2026-11-11 — ownership and the ids of the process (C-7-67).** Fourteen more of `os`: `chown` (by name, by descriptor, under a directory's descriptor and for a link itself: `chown`, `fchown`, `lchown` or `fchownat` as `os_chown_impl` chooses), `fchown`, `lchown`, `getgroups`, `getpgrp`, `getpgid`, `getsid`, `getresuid`, `getresgid`, `getpriority`, `nice`, `getloadavg`, `sched_yield` and `getrandom`. A user or group id converts as `_Py_Uid_Converter` does, slips included: -1 means "leave it", whatever `__index__` raised is replaced by "uid should be integer", and a value too large for the 32 bits of the type is reported as "less than minimum" when it fits a C long (the C goes to its underflow label there) and "greater than maximum" beyond. An OSError from `chown` by descriptor carries the descriptor as its file name, as CPython's does. `getpriority` and `nice` give any value as a priority, so the error travels beside the value from the primitive. The case prints how the ids relate, since they are the environment's own, and only ever gives a file to the owner it has. `posix` has 80 names without a body left.

**2026-11-11 — what a file system and the system say of their limits (C-7-68).** Six more of `os`: `statvfs` (by name or descriptor) and `fstatvfs`, which give the `os.statvfs_result` the prelude already had its type for, and `sysconf`, `confstr`, `pathconf` and `fpathconf`. A configuration name is looked up as `conv_confname` does: in the module's table as it is when the function is called (a program that adds a name to `os.sysconf_names` can ask by it), or given as the number; a name of the table that is no number is a TypeError, an unknown one a ValueError. `sysconf` and the two `pathconf`s give -1 for "no limit" as well as for an error, so `errno` travels beside the value from the primitive; `pathconf`'s EINVAL carries no file name, since it may be the name's, and its other errors do. The case prints kinds, relations and the few limits Linux fixes; every name of the three tables is compared by value on one machine (`test_posix.py`), where `CS_PATH` alone already differs between two versions of the C library. `posix` has 74 names without a body left.

**2026-11-11 — what an AttributeError carries when an attribute cannot be set or deleted (C-3-54).** CPython sets `name` and `obj` on the error only where `_PyObject_GenericSetAttrWithDict` finds no such attribute and nowhere to put one (`_PyObject_SetAttributeErrorContext`); where a descriptor of the type refuses (a getset with no setter, a read-only member) or the name is a method of a type with no `__dict__`, the error names nothing and holds no object. The runtime set both at every site and now sets them where CPython does. With it, measured over some seventy kinds of object and every attribute of a function: `method.__func__` and `__self__`, `function.__closure__` and `type.__base__` say "readonly attribute", as the members they are in CPython (the names table has the kind, whatever the runtime uses to hold them); deleting a function's `__name__` or `__qualname__` is the TypeError its setter raises for what is no str; a function's `__dict__` can be given any dict and cannot be deleted (`PyObject_GenericSetDict`), where it could not be assigned at all; `del f.__doc__` leaves None; and deleting an attribute from a function's own dict works, where it stopped by name. **Not in it, and a case of its own (C-3-55, which the compiled leg does not pass yet):** `property`, `staticmethod` and `classmethod`. The prelude writes them as plain classes, so `property.fget` and the two `__func__`s can be assigned where CPython's are read-only members, a property has a `__dict__` where CPython's has none, and the types' own namespaces show the prelude's helpers instead of the members; reading `property.fget` on the type already stops by name. That is a rewrite of the three most used descriptors and is queued in `TODO.md`.

**2026-11-11 — `property`, `staticmethod` and `classmethod` as the types they are in CPython (C-3-55).** The three were plain classes of `runtime/prelude/builtins.py`: a getter, a setter or a function could be assigned where CPython's are read-only members, a property had a `__dict__`, the types' namespaces showed the prelude's helpers, the constructors answered as Python functions do, and `property.fget` read on the type stopped by name. They are now written from `Objects/descrobject.c` and `Objects/funcobject.c` function by function. **The storage** is the C struct's: each field is a slot whose member the prelude takes out of the type once the class is made (`_field`), so nothing a program can name holds it; a property has no `__dict__`, a staticmethod and a classmethod have the one C declares, which takes any dict and is never deleted (`PyObject_GenericSetDict`). **The members and getsets** are written in the class body with a private descriptor class, `_GetSet`, by the functions that read, write and delete them; the runtime makes each the descriptor the names table says (`present_methods`, which did this for a prelude's `property` objects and now also calls a deleter where C's setter is given NULL), so `property.fget` is a `member_descriptor` that says "readonly attribute" and `__isabstractmethod__` a `getset_descriptor` that is "not writable". `property` cannot use `property` for its own members, and `__name__` cannot be assigned to a class afterwards (the type's own name is in the way), which is why the helper exists and why it is in the body. `__new__` is given after the three exist, by a staticmethod made by hand. **What changed for a program, measured against CPython (the case's rows, and `pipeline/tests/test_descriptors.py` for the words):** `__set_name__` names a property each time (the last class to hold it, where the first one stayed); a copy made by `getter`, `setter` or `deleter` keeps the name; a subclass's docstring goes into its own `__dict__` or `__doc__` slot and is dropped where it has neither, unless it was the getter's (`property_init_impl`); `property.__doc__` and `__name__` can be set and deleted; `staticmethod(f)` and `classmethod(f)` refuse keywords and a second argument in `__new__`, and `__init__` given the same function again does nothing; their `repr`, `__annotations__` and `__annotate__` (taken into the dict when first asked), `__class_getitem__`; `__get__(None, None)` is the TypeError `wrap_descr_get` raises. A type of `builtins.py` now has `tp_doc` as its `__doc__` (the names table's text, where the prelude's own first line stood) and no `__module__` in its namespace. **The paths a program runs most are as short as before:** `__get__`, `__set__`, `__delete__` and `staticmethod.__call__` keep fixed parameters and read their field with one call; `property.__init__` takes its arguments through the runtime's keyword parser (`_unpack_keywords`, now also given to the startup preludes), and the one-argument constructors and copying methods check theirs in CPython's words. **Found on the way and fixed, with rows in C-3-54:** an instance with no `__dict__` asked to set or delete a name its class has (a class attribute, a method, `__doc__`) is told the attribute "is read-only", and deleting a slot that was never set says the name alone; neither error names anything or holds an object (`_PyObject_GenericSetAttrWithDict`, `PyMember_SetOne`), where both carried the name and the object. The same holds through `object.__setattr__` and `object.__delattr__` called by name, which a class with a `__setattr__` of its own goes through (and with it `list[int].__doc__ = …` and a union's, whose prelude classes have one). A name the runtime keeps on a C type that CPython's type does not have is not found there: a static type's `__module__` is its `tp_name`'s, so `(int | str).__module__ = …` is "no attribute", with name and object (the full local checks caught the first version, which said "read-only": `pipeline/tests/test_union.py`). And "no attribute" ends with "and no __dict__ for setting new attributes" only where `tp_setattro` is the generic one, which a class with a `__setattr__` or `__delattr__` of its own does not have. And `object.__reduce_ex__` of a subclass of a C type with fields of its own (`class Sub(property)`) refuses as CPython does ("cannot pickle 'Sub' object"): the layout was asked of the subclass's name. **Left as it is:** a wrong number of arguments to `__get__`, `__set__` or `__delete__` called by hand is a TypeError whose words are a Python function's beyond the one case above (`p.__get__()`); other types the prelude writes with a `__dict__` of their own (`functools.partial`) still let it be deleted.

**2026-11-11 — several buffers at once, and descriptors made without a file (C-7-69).** Twelve more of `os`: `readv`, `preadv`, `writev`, `pwritev`, `readinto`, `eventfd`, `eventfd_read`, `eventfd_write`, `memfd_create`, `mkfifo`, `mknod` and `splice`. **The buffers** are taken as `iov_setup` takes them: anything `PySequence_Check` accepts (a class with `__len__` and `__getitem__` is one, an iterator and a dict are not), its length asked once, each item fetched in order; a buffer to read into must be a `bytearray` (a `bytes` is a BufferError, "Object is not writable."), one to write from a `bytes` or a `bytearray`, and any other object with a `__buffer__` stops by name, as `memoryview` does everywhere. The system call is made once, on buffers of the sizes the program's have, and the bytes are laid into the program's buffers in the order the kernel filled them, so the same `bytearray` given twice ends as it does in CPython. **The pinned build has no `preadv2`:** no `RWF_*` constant exists, any flags but 0 are a NotImplementedError ("preadv2: flags unavailable on this platform", and `pwritev2` for the other), and an offset of -1 is the kernel's EINVAL; the runtime says the same rather than what a newer C library would allow. **The unsigned arguments** convert as their clinic converters do: `eventfd`'s initial value and `splice`'s flags refuse a negative int with a ValueError and one too large with an OverflowError, `eventfd_write`'s value likewise over 64 bits, and `memfd_create`'s flags are `unsigned_int(bitwise=True)`, which takes the low 32 bits and, in 3.15, warns (DeprecationWarning, "integer value out of range") for a value neither a C int nor an unsigned holds. `mkfifo` and `mknod` raise with no file name (`posix_error`), try again after a signal, and take `dir_fd`; a device number goes through the converter `makedev`'s results come from. `splice` converts its offsets inside the function, after the flags, and an offset given for a pipe is the kernel's ESPIPE. The case prints what each returns, leaves and refuses; the words, the warning and the order of two refusals in one call are compared on one machine (`test_posix.py`, 139 lines). `posix` has 62 names without a body left: the process functions, the setters of ids, scheduling, the terminal functions, `timerfd` and `pidfd_open`.

**2026-11-11 — timers that are descriptors, and the scheduler (C-7-70).** Nineteen more of `os`, and `os.sched_param` made real: `timerfd_create`, `timerfd_settime`, `timerfd_settime_ns`, `timerfd_gettime`, `timerfd_gettime_ns`; `sched_get_priority_max` and `_min`, `sched_getscheduler`, `sched_setscheduler`, `sched_getparam`, `sched_setparam`, `sched_rr_get_interval`, `sched_getaffinity`, `sched_setaffinity`; `pidfd_open`, `ctermid`, `device_encoding`, `getlogin`, `getgrouplist`. With `sched_getaffinity` real, `os.process_cpu_count()` answers, where it stopped by name (`os.py` defines it over that function when the name exists). **A time in seconds** becomes nanoseconds as `pytime_from_double` does it: times a billion, floored, and an OverflowError ("timestamp out of range for C PyTime_t") for what 64 bits do not hold, a NaN among them; what comes back is `seconds + nanoseconds * 1e-9`, the expression `build_itimerspec` uses, so the float is the same one. In nanoseconds the arguments are the `long long` converter's ("int too big to convert"). A negative time is the kernel's EINVAL, not the function's. `timerfd_create` always adds `TFD_CLOEXEC` (PEP 446). **`sched_param`** is a struct sequence whose constructor takes its field by position or name and keeps any object; it is asked for a C int only where it is used (`convert_sched_param`: the type itself, no subclass and no tuple). `_bella_structseq` takes two more arguments for it, `parse` (a `tp_new` of the type's own) and `reduced` (its own `__reduce__`), rather than a second copy of the builder. **The CPU set** is read from any iterable, item by item, with CPython's checks in its order, and grown as CPython grows it; what the kernel allows comes back as a `set`. **`device_encoding`** of a terminal is `utf-8`: 3.15 runs in UTF-8 mode unless told otherwise, and the runtime has no other mode (`sys.flags.utf8_mode` is 1); of anything else it is None. The test gives CPython and the compiled program the same pseudo-terminal. `getlogin` and `getgrouplist` answer from the C library, as CPython's do; their values are compared on one machine only, as are the CPUs and the round-robin quantum. The case sets its timers minutes ahead where it only looks at them, and prints the policy it started under as a relation, so a stalled machine or a run started under `chrt -b` records the same. Not in the case and not tried: a real-time policy (`SCHED_FIFO` needs a privilege, and a test that got it would hold a CPU). `posix` has 43 names without a body left: the process functions, the setters of ids, the terminal functions, `setns`, `unshare`, `chroot`. Left as it is: a struct sequence's member has no `__doc__` (`os.sched_param.sched_priority.__doc__`, as `os.stat_result.st_mode.__doc__`, stops by name).

**2026-11-11 — setting what the process is (C-7-71).** Fourteen more of `os`: `setuid`, `seteuid`, `setgid`, `setegid`, `setreuid`, `setregid`, `setresuid`, `setresgid`, `setgroups`, `initgroups`, `setpgid`, `setpgrp`, `setsid` and `setpriority`. Each is the system call behind CPython's converters: an id is -1 ("leave it") or 32 bits (`_Py_Uid_Converter` and `_Py_Gid_Converter`, with their odd "is less than minimum" for a value too large), `setgroups` takes what `PySequence_Check` accepts, at most `NGROUPS_MAX` items, each an int that is a gid, and every error is `posix_error`'s, with no file name. **What a process may set depends on who runs it**, and CI runs as root where a developer does not, so the case sets each id to what it already is, asks for what nobody may (an id of -1 alone, a group that is not there), and says of the session and the process group what follows from the process being a leader or not: a group's leader cannot start a session, a session's leader cannot change its group. It records the same started by a shell, under `setsid`, and in a pipeline. What privilege decides (`setgroups`, `initgroups`) is compared on one machine, where both sides have the same. Not tried anywhere: setting an id to another user's, or a lower niceness, which a test that got it could not take back. **Found on the way:** `posix.py`'s own keyword parser (`_unpack`, written by hand before the runtime had one) said that an argument was given twice before it said that one was missing, where `_PyArg_UnpackKeywords` says them the other way round (`os.setpriority(0, 0, which=0)`). It now calls the runtime's `_unpack_keywords`, as the rule for preludes has been since 2026-11-09; every `os` function with named parameters goes through it, and the same-machine tests of the earlier batches pass unchanged. `posix` has 29 names without a body left.

**2026-11-11 — pseudo-terminals, and the process moved elsewhere (C-7-72).** Fourteen more of `os`: `openpty`, `posix_openpt`, `grantpt`, `unlockpt`, `ptsname`, `ttyname`, `tcgetpgrp`, `tcsetpgrp`, `login_tty`; `chroot`, `unshare`, `setns`; and `posix._inputhook` with `posix._is_inputhook_installed`, which answer 0 and False (`PyOS_InputHook` is C's to set, and no C sets it in a compiled program). Each is the C library's call as `posixmodule.c` makes it on Linux: `openpty` with both ends made non-inheritable, `posix_openpt` with `O_CLOEXEC` added, `grantpt` with SIGCHLD at its default while a helper may run, `ttyname_r` and `ptsname_r` whose value is the error, `login_tty` the C library's alone (the session, the controlling terminal, the three standard descriptors, the descriptor closed). Which arguments are descriptors by `fileno()` and which are plain ints is CPython's, function by function (`ttyname(fd)` and `tcgetpgrp(fd)` take an int only). **A terminal's name is the machine's**, so the case prints where it is and that two ways of asking agree; it records the same with a controlling terminal and as a session leader without one, and opens every terminal with `O_NOCTTY` so that it never acquires one. **`login_tty` is tested where it succeeds** by a program that is given one end of a pseudo-terminal, makes it its own and reports over it (the test holds the other end): after the call the process leads a session, the terminal is its controlling one, `tcgetpgrp` and `tcsetpgrp` answer for it. `chroot` is tried only as far as a process without the privilege gets (a root that is not there); CI runs as root, and a test that changed its root could not undo it. Of `unshare` and `setns` the case prints only what is refused before the system is asked: CI's container filters the two calls themselves (`unshare(0)` is EPERM there and nothing here), which the first recording did not allow for and CI's `verify` caught; the multiplexer's name differed too (`/dev/pts/ptmx` in the container) and is now a relation. What the calls answer is compared on one machine. Since then a case that asks the system for anything a container or a privilege may answer differently is verified under CPython in the project's image before it is pushed (`docker run --rm -v $PWD:/p:ro bella:task1 sh -c 'cd /p && python3 conformance/harness.py verify --filter …'`). `posix` has 15 names without a body left. **What they wait for** (TODO.md): `fork` and `forkpty` are [P2+] by the specification; `wait3` and `wait4` return a `resource.struct_rusage`, and `resource` is not in the manifest; `kill` and `killpg` need `_signal`, because a signal sent to the process itself runs its handler before the call returns; the rest (`system`, `execv`, `execve`, `wait`, `waitpid`, `waitid`, `posix_spawn`, `posix_spawnp`, `abort`) are next.

**2026-11-11 — a pipe nobody reads: the signals CPython ignores when it starts (C-7-73).** A defect, found while reading what the process functions will need: a compiled program that wrote to a pipe whose reader had gone was killed by SIGPIPE (exit status 141, nothing said), where CPython raises BrokenPipeError, which a program may catch, and otherwise says "Exception ignored while flushing sys.stdout" and exits with 120. CPython ignores SIGPIPE and SIGXFSZ when it starts (`signal_install_handlers`, `Python/pylifecycle.c`); the runtime now does the same as the first thing `run_main` does. Nothing else changed: the errors were already raised from `EPIPE` and `EFBIG` wherever the write returns them, and the way out already reports a flush that fails. The case writes to a pipe of its own with the reading end closed, through `os.write`, a raw file, a buffered one, a text file and a line-buffered one; `pipeline/tests/test_signals.py` takes the reader of the program's standard output away and sets a file size limit from outside, and compares the words and the exit status with CPython's. The two dispositions are inherited by whatever a program starts, as they are from CPython (the well-known reason a shell pipeline started from Python sees `EPIPE` where it would be killed), which is why this comes before `os.system`, `exec` and `posix_spawn`. **Not done here, and the author's to decide:** the third thing CPython does at startup, the handler for SIGINT that raises KeyboardInterrupt. It needs `_signal` ([P1], not written yet) and a place where compiled code looks for a signal that has arrived; until then Ctrl-C ends a compiled program by the signal, with no traceback.

**2026-11-11 — starting a program and waiting for it (C-7-74).** Nine more of `os`: `posix_spawn`, `posix_spawnp`, `wait`, `waitpid`, `waitid`, `system`, `execv`, `execve` and `abort`. `os.fork` is [P2+] by the specification, so a compiled program's children are programs it starts, not copies of itself; the case's are `/bin/sh` with a script, and one that must still be running waits on a pipe the case holds, not on a clock. **`posix_spawn` is read as `py_posix_spawn` reads it, in its order:** the path, `argv` (a list or a tuple, not empty, each item by `PyUnicode_FSConverter`, the first not empty), the environment (None for the program's own, else a mapping whose keys and values are taken as `PyMapping_Keys` and `PyMapping_Values` take them), the file actions, then the flags. A file action is parsed as `PyArg_ParseTuple` parses it, which is where its odd words come from: a tuple of the wrong length and a mode that is no int both say "A open file_action tuple must have 5 elements", an fd too large is "signed integer is greater than maximum", a mode of more than 64 bits is taken with a DeprecationWarning. Each action is added to a set of its own as it is read, so that the C library's refusal of it (a descriptor below zero) comes where CPython's does, before a later tuple is looked at. A signal set takes numbers 1 to 64; one the C library keeps for itself (32, 33) is left out with a RuntimeWarning, as `_Py_Sigset_Converter` does. An error of setting an attribute carries no file name, an error of the spawn itself carries the path. **As the pinned build has it:** `setsid=True` is a NotImplementedError ("setsid unavailable on this platform"), and `POSIX_SPAWN_CLOSEFROM` does not exist. **`execv` and `execve`** replace the program without writing its buffers out or running its exit hooks, as in CPython; `execve` takes a descriptor (`fexecve`); `execv`'s error names no file and `execve`'s does. **`system`** returns what the C library's does, the wait status. **`abort`** ends the program by SIGABRT. **The wait family** tries again after a signal; `waitid` gives None where there is nothing to report. A child inherits the ignored SIGPIPE (C-7-73), which the case shows with a shell that signals itself and goes on, and `setsigdef` gives it back. The tests add what only an outside view shows: what a child writes comes before what the program still holds in its buffer; after `exec` and after `abort` nothing of the buffer is written and no exit hook runs (the abort test sets the core file limit to zero from outside). **Six names of `posix` are left**, and each says what it waits for when a program calls it: `fork` and `forkpty` ([P2+]), `wait3` and `wait4` (their result holds a `resource.struct_rusage`, and `resource` is not in the phase-1 manifest), `kill` and `killpg` (a signal sent to the process itself runs its handler inside the call, which needs `_signal`).

**2026-11-11 — what a member says of itself (C-3-56).** `os.stat_result.st_mode.__doc__` stopped by name, as did the `__doc__` of every member the runtime had not given one by hand. The names table now records it: `tools/cpython_names.py` writes the `__doc__` of each member and getset descriptor of every type it samples (`member:<tp_name>.<name>`, 619 rows, an empty row for None), and `descriptor_doc` answers from it where the runtime's own definition carries none; a name the table lacks still stops. A program's own `__slots__` member has no doc, and the `__dict__` and `__weakref__` every class with them shares say what CPython's say (they belong to `object` there too, which is why they are shared here). **Which of the two a descriptor is, a member or a getset, is the table's as well:** `present_descriptor_kinds` sets it for every C++ type and exception class at start-up and for every prelude class as it is presented, where each file chose by hand before and `function.__closure__`, a method's `__func__` and `__self__`, a built-in's `__module__`, `super`'s three and `SimpleNamespace.__dict__` had it wrong. With it comes the member's refusal ("readonly attribute") where a getset's stood. `BaseException.__dict__` is the type's own getset (`PyObject_GenericGetDict`, `PyObject_GenericSetDict`), as `staticmethod`'s became in C-3-55, by the same rule: a C type the table gives a `__dict__` getset has its own, with itself as `__objclass__`. **Measured** by a sweep of every public member and getset of some ninety types against CPython (kind, `__objclass__`, names, doc, repr): seventy agree entirely and are the test (`pipeline/tests/test_descriptors.py`). **The rest differ for gaps this did not close** (TODO.md): members the types lack (`type.__basicsize__`, `__itemsize__`, `__type_params__`; `generator.gi_code` and `gi_frame`; `module.__annotations__`; most of a code object's; `__text_signature__` and `__vectorcalloffset__` everywhere), `SimpleNamespace` showing a `__weakref__` CPython's does not, the Unicode errors' `__init__`, and the preludes' own slots (`types.GenericAlias._ga_parameters`, a union's), which `vars()` shows and CPython's types do not have. Reading the type's namespace for one of the missing gives a KeyError where an attribute read would stop by name.

**2026-11-11 — a partial without its dict (C-3-57).** Found by the full checks of C-3-56: once `functools.partial`'s `__dict__` was the getset CPython's type has (`PyObject_GenericSetDict`, which never deletes), `partial.__setstate__` given no dict could not drop the one it had, because the prelude did it with `object.__delattr__(self, "__dict__")` (case C-7-25 stopped with "cannot delete __dict__"). What the prelude did before was not CPython's either: it installed a fresh empty dict, so `__reduce__` said `{}` where CPython says None, and another partial did not unwrap this one (`partial_new` looks at `pto->dict == NULL`). The C does `Py_XSETREF(pto->dict, NULL)`; the model has that operation now (`Runtime.drop_dict`, `refmodel/objects.py`: the object has no dict again, as when it was made, and what it held goes as the dict it was), `object.hpp` has it from the model (`drop_dict`), and `_functools` reaches it through a primitive (`_drop_dict`). `del partial_obj.__dict__` is the TypeError CPython raises, which closes the leftover of C-3-55. The operation is for a C type's own dict slot only. `del obj.__dict__` of a class's instance stays as it was (a fresh dict is installed): CPython leaves such an object in a state of its own, measured the same day and not reproduced yet (TODO: its inline values are invalid, the next plain read gives it an ordinary dict, the next write, delete or `__dict__` read one that shares the class's keys, and a method call `o.m()` neither), so the oracle has no random operation for `drop_dict` and the model test is the fixed one (`test_a_dropped_dict_leaves_the_object_as_it_was_made`). A row of `test_names_cpython_has_are_refused` that expected `OSError.errno.__doc__` to stop went with C-3-56, which answers it.

**2026-11-11 — where compiled code looks for a signal, and what a handler is given (the author's decisions).** CPython's C-level handler only sets a flag, and the Python-level handler runs when the evaluation loop tests it: at a function's entry, at every backward jump of a loop, after a call, and wherever a blocking call is interrupted or the program signals itself (`trip_signal`, `_CHECK_PERIODIC` in `Python/bytecodes.c`). **Decided: the same sites.** Generated code tests the flag at every Python function's entry (where the recursion limit is already tested) and at every loop turn, and the runtime runs the handlers at the synchronous points (`signal.raise_signal`, `os.kill` to the process itself, a blocking call that a signal interrupted). A prototype of the two tests cost nothing measurable on three benchmarks (a loop of 30 million turns, 20 million small calls, nested loops over a list), at today's code quality. **With a build switch that leaves the tests out of generated code**, so that handlers run only at the synchronous points and at blocking calls: the author wants it as part of a broader flag for production builds of well-tested programs, which drops such rare compatibility items for speed. What that flag changes is a deviation to register with it; its name and its place (`bella.toml`, the build command) are not chosen yet. **What a handler receives as `frame`:** an opaque object of type `frame` whose attributes stop by name (frame objects are excluded, E-02), so that a handler that ignores it works and one that inspects it stops; under the switch it is None. This unblocks `_signal`, `os.kill`, `os.killpg`, and SIGINT raising KeyboardInterrupt (case C-7-11 compiled also needs `gc`).

**2026-11-11 — an instance without its dict (C-3-58, D-23; the author's decision).** `del obj.__dict__` of a class's instance installed a fresh ordinary dict. CPython leaves the object with none, its inline values invalid (`subtype_setdict`, `_PyObject_SetManagedDict(obj, NULL)`), and what is asked of it next decides what it gets, measured on 3.15.2: a plain read that reaches the instance (`o.x`, a method not called, `getattr`, `hasattr`, a non-data descriptor, `copy.copy`) gives it an ordinary dict (`_PyObject_MaterializeManagedDict` with the values invalid: `PyDict_New`), whose values go in the order they were set; a write, a delete, `o.__dict__`, `vars` or `dir` gives it one that shares the class's keys, its values its own (`ensure_managed_dict`: `new_dict_with_shared_keys`), whose values go in the keys' order; what the class answers alone (a data descriptor, a property, a slot, `__class__`, a special method) gives it none yet. The author chose to reproduce this as a state of the object, calling it a stop-gap: **the model first** (`RefObject.dropped`, `Runtime.del_dict`, `_settle`, `refmodel/objects.py`), with the oracle given the operation as the del, the first thing asked, and a probe that stores two tagged values and deletes the dict again, so that the order of their deaths says which dict the object had (a wrong variant of either rule is caught within some 1,500 operations on each of eight seeds; without the probe one of them survived 10,000); **then the header** (`DICT_DROPPED`, `del_dict`, `settle_dropped`), where the flag shares the test of `DICT_EXPOSED` on the read and the write path, so an object that never lost its dict pays nothing. The object is without its dict before the del releases anything, so a finaliser that runs meanwhile meets the dropped object, as in CPython (the case has the row). The dict a write gives is not the object's inline values again: a delete of a missing name joins no keys there, as measured. **An `int`'s or a `tuple`'s subclass keeps no values in the object** (`tp_itemsize`), so its instance gets the sharing dict whatever comes first: for those the runtime uses `drop_dict` (the object as it was made), which is exact for the case's rows; two finer differences of such instances, older than this, are in TODO.md. **One row stays different, deviation D-23:** a method call `o.m()` as the first thing asked. CPython's call form (`_PyObject_GetMethod`) asks nothing of the instance; the compiler, `eval1` and `eval2` do not tell a call's load from a plain one, so the call is the plain read. It shows only in the order of release, only for a class with neither `__getattr__` nor `__getattribute__`, and is pinned by `pipeline/tests/test_instance_dict.py`, which fails when the two agree. The author wants it reconsidered later; exactness needs the call form threaded through L1, L2, the backend and both evaluators. The specification is v0.16.

**2026-11-11 — the dict an instance has is its class's (C-3-59).** Two defects found with C-3-57 and C-3-58, older than both, fixed as one: the runtime gave every instance the dict of a program's class, its values kept in the object and sharing the class's keys, so the values went in the keys' order whatever the type. CPython has three kinds (measured on 3.15.2 for some seventy types; `type_new_descriptors`, `type_ready_managed_dict`, `ensure_nonmanaged_dict`). **The values in the object** (a managed dict with inline values): a program's class, and a subclass of a built-in type of fixed size (`list`, `dict`, `str`, `float`, `property`, `deque`). **A dict of its own that shares the class's keys** (a managed dict with no inline values, because the object's size varies): the subclass of an `int` or a `tuple`; it is made at the first write, the first delete or when asked for, a delete of a name it has not joins no keys (where the values are in the object the name joins them before it is found missing), a cleared dict is an ordinary one, and `del obj.__dict__` leaves the object as it was made. **An ordinary dict** made the same way: a type written in C with its own dict slot and whatever derives from it (every exception and a program's exception classes, `functools.partial` and an `lru_cache` wrapper, `types.SimpleNamespace`, `staticmethod` and `classmethod`, `collections.OrderedDict`, the `io` classes, `threading.local`): the values go in the order they were set, and a program never deletes that dict. A delete that fails makes the dict in the last two kinds, which `BaseException.__reduce__` and `partial.__reduce__` show (the case has both rows). **The model first** (`RefClass.dict_kind`, inherited from the nearest class in the MRO that is not inline; `Runtime._settle` now gives either kind of own dict), with two classes added to the oracle's world, an exception's subclass and an `int`'s, and a second probe operation, since the first cannot ask an object whose dict is never deleted: fresh instances get two tagged values under two names, are dropped, and get them again in the other order, so an ordinary dict lets them go as stored both times and a sharing one does not; with a failed delete first, the two sharing kinds differ too. Three wrong variants of the rules are caught within some 2,500 operations on each of six seeds; a fourth is not, and is not wrong (whether a failed delete joins the keys of a class whose dicts are ordinary shows nowhere). **Then the header** (`DictKind`, `ClassObject::dict_kind`, `detail::settle`): the kind is asked only where an object has no dict yet, so an object of a program's class pays nothing. **In the runtime the kind is CPython's own answer**, from the rows the names table already had for `__flags__` and `__dictoffset__`: the nearest type written in C along `tp_base` has a dict slot of its own (an ordinary dict, unless its flags say the dict is managed), or has none and a class added a managed one, whose values are inline unless `heapflags:` lacks `Py_TPFLAGS_INLINE_VALUES`. `del obj.__dict__` needs no rule of its own for `int` and `tuple` subclasses any more (C-3-58 had one). The case passes on every leg; `pipeline/tests/test_instance_dict.py` compares forty more types on one machine. A type the table has no row for keeps the first kind, as before (TODO.md).

**2026-11-11 — a class has thirty places for its keys, and its instances take them too (C-3-60).** Found by the reference model's Hypothesis machine an hour after C-3-59 was pushed, in the local run and in CI alike (`main` was red for one commit): nine probes of one name, then two more names, and CPython released an instance's two values in the order they were set where the model said the keys' order. The rule was older than the day's work and no operation had made instances fast enough to meet it; C-3-59's probe makes three per operation. CPython gives a class's shared keys thirty places (`dk_usable`, `_PyDict_NewKeysForClass`: "it will shrink by one before each new object"). A key takes one (`insert_split_key`), and so does every instance made while more than one is left (`_PyObject_InitInlineValues`), so a class that has made some thirty instances takes one more name and then none. An instance that stores a name the keys cannot take gets an ordinary dict from there on, holding what it had in the order it was stored (`store_instance_attr_lock_held`: `make_dict_from_instance_attributes`, then `insertdict`'s resize into a combined table), and its values go in that order. The model and the header had "at most thirty keys" and released such an object's values keys first. Measured on 3.15.2 and in the case: the order flips at the twenty-eighth instance made before three names are stored; names the keys already hold stay with them; the names a class's own code sets are keys from the start (`__static_attributes__`); a subclass has places of its own; an `int`'s or a `tuple`'s subclass, whose instances keep no values in them, takes no place per instance; a delete of a name the keys cannot take changes nothing. **The model first** (`RefClass.keys_left`, `Runtime._unshare`), with a `churn` operation in the oracle (an object dropped and replaced a dozen to thirty-five times) so that random runs cross the limit: the old rule is caught within some 5,000 operations on each of six seeds, within 2,400 on the default one. **Then the header** (`SharedKeys::left`, `instance_made` in every instance kind's constructor, `detail::unshare`). A program sees it only in the order an object's attribute values are released, for a class that stores new attribute names on instances after it has made about thirty.

**2026-11-11 — signals (C-7-75, C-7-76; D-24, D-25, D-26; the author's decisions of the same day).** `_signal` is `runtime/prelude/_signal.py`, from `Modules/signalmodule.c` and its clinic for what a program can tell (the order arguments are converted and refused in, the warnings for the two numbers the C library keeps and the line they are charged to, times rounded up before their sign is tested), over one primitive, `_signals(name, ...)`, in `runtime/src/signals.cpp`. **The core keeps CPython's two notes**: `is_tripped`, which `check_signals()` (PyErr_CheckSignals) reads and clears, and the eval breaker as generated code tests it, one word for CPython's signals bit and its pending-calls bit (a failed wakeup write queues the one pending call there is). The C handler writes, in CPython's order, the signal's flag, `is_tripped`, the breaker, then the wakeup byte, and nothing else. **Three ways in**, as CPython's: `check_signals()` where the C calls PyErr_CheckSignals itself (a blocking call a signal interrupted, `raise_signal`, `os.kill`, `pthread_kill`, `pthread_sigmask`, `signal.signal`); the evaluation loop's look (`look_for_signals()`, behind `BELLA_LOOK()`: the signals bit cleared, the handlers, the bit set again if one raised, then the queued reports); and Py_MakePendingCalls (before and after the program, a lock's wait). The difference shows when a handler raises with another signal tripped: after a look the next look of generated code runs the rest, after a check the next check does. **Generated code looks where CPython's evaluation loop does** (the decision of this morning): `BELLA_LOOK();` after a frame's arguments are bound (RESUME: functions, lambdas, class and module bodies, a generator's first resume and each resume after a plain `yield`), `for (;; BELLA_LOOK())` at a loop's turn, and after a loop that a `break` CPython compiles backward has left. Which breaks those are, CPython's compiler says: the frontend already compiles every tree with the pin, and `mark_looking_breaks` reads its code (the precedent is the set displays of C-6-13), since the rule depends on block layout (a break on a path only an exception reaches jumps backward unless the loop's exit is small enough to be copied in place). No look in prelude units, which stand for CPython's C, nor in `_pydecimal` and `_pydatetime`, which stand for its C accelerators (`LOOK_FREE` in `pipeline/build.py`). **The runtime looks when a call that waits or sends a signal is over**, which covers what the approved note says of CPython's look after every built-in call; measured, CPython never looks before a blocking call, so the looks the core's draft had before each wait are a lean build's alone (`look_lean`). **An untimed wait on a lock is a real wait now**: the decision of 2026-11-05 stopped it by name "since hanging would be right only with signals, which are not there yet". The wait is a futex word nothing wakes, which the kernel restarts after a handler whose signal has SA_RESTART and ends after one without, as it does CPython's park; a timed wait ends after either, as CPython's does. `RLock._acquire_restore` on an RLock another thread owns still stops by name: CPython's wait there takes no signal, so nothing could end it. `_thread.interrupt_main`, `os.kill`, `os.killpg` and `signal.pidfd_send_signal` are real; `pthread_kill` to another thread's id stops by name (there is no other thread, and the C library's answer for an unknown id is undefined). **A handler's `frame`** is an object of type `frame` made once for each pass that runs handlers, whose attributes stop by name (E-02 narrowed in v0.17); None where CPython has no Python frame, and in a lean build. **An uncaught KeyboardInterrupt ends the process by SIGINT** (exit_sigint: a parent sees -2, a shell 130). **The fidelity switch**: `fidelity = "exact" | "lean"`, the name the author confirmed; `pipeline/build.py --fidelity lean` or `BELLA_FIDELITY=lean`, `-DBELLA_LEAN` to the units compiled from the program and the standard library and to the main unit, never to the runtime's or the prelude's, so one runtime library serves both builds; the main unit hands the runtime `BELLA_IS_LEAN`. A lean build does not take SIGINT at start (Ctrl-C ends the program by the signal), and its handlers run at the runtime's looks, the one before a call that may wait included (D-24, approved). **What stays different in an exact build** is D-25: the look after every built-in call (approved as a note), and five places found while writing this, each listed rather than left silent. **D-26**: `sigtimedwait` whose time ran out while handlers ran returns None (approved; CPython's answer is undefined). Also: `_io`'s buffered classes have CPython's reentrancy guard ("reentrant call inside"), which a handler that prints while a print is half done meets, and check signals after a raw write as the C does; a stream's flush retries an interrupted write; `os.fstat`, `os.fsync`, `os.fchmod` and the other calls CPython retries after a signal retry. The names table has the 60 rows of `_signal` and `signal.struct_siginfo`. The cases pass compiled, C-7-75 under eval2 too; C-7-76 stays out of the eval1 and eval2 baselines, since there the handlers run at the looks of the CPython that runs the evaluator, so its rows that depend on where a handler runs pass in some runs only (as C-7-51 stays out of eval1's); the same-machine half (signals from outside, the ways a process dies of one, the words of every refusal, the rows the cases leave out, the lean build's pins) is `pipeline/tests/test_signals.py`, and `pipeline/tests/test_fidelity.py` pins the build's side of the switch.

**2026-11-11 — the lines of a with statement (C-3-61).** Found by a same-machine signal test: a KeyboardInterrupt out of a nested `with` said line 9 of a six-line program. The frontend expands `with` into a template parsed from text, and the template's own nodes kept the line numbers of that text, so every exception raised by what the statement does itself (the lookups of `__enter__` and `__exit__`, the call of `__enter__`, binding the target, the calls of `__exit__`) was charged to a line of the template. CPython charges them to the line where the item's expression starts (`codegen_with_inner`): the with's line for one item on one line, an item's own line when the items take several, and the first line of an expression written over several; binding the target is at the target. The template's nodes now take the item's location before the program's nodes go in. A second defect behind it, in L2: after a `try`, the lowering took the frame's line to be the one its last handler said, although the normal path had run other lines in between, so the `finally` block that calls `__exit__` after the body said no line of its own; a handler, an else block, a finally block and what follows a `try` now say their first line again, which costs a store of an int where the line was the same. The case passes compiled and under eval2; under eval1 a traceback's lines are the evaluator's own, as for every case that reads them.

**2026-11-12 — the cycle collector in the reference model (step 3c, model first; C-9-01, C-9-02, C-9-07).** `refmodel/collector.py` is CPython 3.15.2's `Python/gc.c`, rule for rule, over the model's objects: the three generations (2000, 10, 10; the incremental collector of 3.14 is gone in 3.15), `update_refs`, `subtract_refs` and `move_unreachable` with CPython's resulting order (what stays reachable keeps its place, what is reached again goes to the end and is scanned again), survivors to the next generation, then the 3.15 sequence: weak references with callbacks to unreachable objects cleared and the callbacks of the reachable ones run, each reference held meanwhile; every finaliser once, in the list's order; the reckoning again over the unreachable list, what a finaliser made reachable (and what that reaches) living on in the older generation; the rest losing its weak references without callbacks, then each object's clear function, the object held. The number returned is what was cleared plus the weak references the callbacks' release freed. `objects.py` keeps the lists (`GcState`, on the `Heap`): every counted object of the model's is tracked from its creation, as an instance, a dict and a weak reference are in CPython; a dict that stands for the values an object keeps in itself is not an object and is not tracked; `subtype_dealloc`'s tracking is reproduced (untracked first, tracked again around the finaliser, so a resurrected object is young again); each object has its type's `traverse` (`subtype_traverse`, `dict_traverse`, the weak reference's callback) and `gc_clear` (`subtype_clear`, `PyDict_Clear`, `clear_weakref`). A new object counts toward the first threshold and, past it, schedules a collection that runs at the next look (`_Py_ScheduleGC`, `_Py_RunGC`), as CPython 3.12+ does. A tagged object's finaliser now logs its tag, as the oracle's `Tagged.__del__` does, so a tag the collector finalises and a resurrection then keeps is logged once. The oracle has cycles behind `World(cycles=True)` (the selftest and the Hypothesis machine; `make object-test` keeps it off until the header has the collector): a value may be one of the program's objects, a `ring` operation makes a cycle of new objects with tags, weak references with and without callbacks, sometimes an object whose finaliser resurrects the group, sometimes held by one of the program's objects, and `collect` runs `gc.collect(generation)` on both sides, CPython's automatic collector off meanwhile and both sides starting with a full collection so that every object is in the same generation on both; a collection's deaths compare as a multiset (section 9 leaves a group's order unspecified). On five seeds of 3000 operations, 923 collections freed 652 tagged values, called 100 callbacks and met 72 resurrecting finalisers, the model agreeing with CPython; the model's tests compare C-9-07's sequence with and without resurrection, what `collect()` returns and which generation each survivor ends in with CPython's `gc.get_objects(generation)`. Found on the way, both in the oracle: a ring's temporaries must be let go of last to first, as CPython's list of them is, and a ring's object must not store through one of the world's data descriptors, whose stores are keyed by `id()` and outlive an object the collector frees. **Open with the author before the header:** how the header finds its objects (CPython's lists, a link in each container, recommended; or the design draft's trial deletion over candidate roots), what `gc.collect()` returns where Bella's objects are not CPython's (a closure's cells have no tuple), and when a lean build collects.

**2026-11-12 — the gc module (C-7-77), before the collector.** `weakref.finalize`'s exit function imports `gc` to turn automatic collection off while the finalisers run, so a program with a finaliser still registered at exit (`tempfile.TemporaryDirectory` registers one) stopped there, after its last line. `gc` is `runtime/prelude/gc.py`, from `Modules/gcmodule.c` and its clinic (each function's parsing, in the C's order and words: METH_NOARGS and METH_O with the module in the message, `set_threshold` through `PyArg_ParseTuple`'s "i" with all three parsed before any is set, `collect` and `get_objects` through `_PyArg_UnpackKeywords` and their own range checks), over one primitive, `_gc(name, ...)`, in `runtime/src/gc.cpp`: the interpreter's GCState, which is where the collector will read it, with automatic collection on, the thresholds 2000, 10 and 10, the debugging flags, and the interpreter's `garbage` and `callbacks` lists, made once and only added by the module, as CPython's are. What needs the collector stops by name after its arguments are checked (`collect`, `get_count`, `get_stats`, `is_tracked`, `is_finalized`: "the cycle collector: step 3c"), as does what reads CPython's object graph (`get_objects`, `get_referrers`, `get_referents`, `freeze`, `unfreeze`, `get_freeze_count`: E-05). The names table has gc's docstrings. C-7-77 passes on all three legs; `pipeline/tests/test_system_modules.py::test_gc` compares every refusal's words and the finalisers at exit with CPython's.

**2026-11-12 — try/except* (C-3-16, C-3-62).** The frontend lowers it (`desugar-try-star`, a pass before `desugar-with`; Fact NO_TRY_STAR, which `to-l1` requires) into an ordinary try whose one handler catches everything, as CPython's PUSH_EXC_INFO does, and runs the clauses as `codegen_try_star_except` compiles them: each on what the previous left (`%eg_match`, CHECK_EG_MATCH: `_PyEval_CheckExceptStarTypeValid`'s two refusals, a group split by its own `split` method with the C's two TypeErrors for a wrong answer, a bare exception wrapped in a group whose traceback is one entry for the frame at the except* line, `_PyTraceBack_FromFrame`), the clause run with its match as the exception being handled and what it raises gathered, then `%prep_reraise_star` (`_PyExc_PrepReraiseStar`: what kept orig's traceback, cause and context was re-raised and keeps orig's nesting, by `exception_group_projection` over the leaves' identities through `derive`; the rest join it in a new group) and `%reraise_star`, RERAISE, which takes no context and adds no entry for the frame. L1, L2, their passes and the backend see only try statements and intrinsic calls. Compiled, `%eg_match` replaces the innermost handled exception, as `PyErr_SetHandledException` does, so a refusal of a later clause's type takes the previous match as its context (C-3-62 pins it); `runtime/src/types.cpp` has the C's three matchers for the split (by type, by predicate, by instance ids), and a frame's id moved from `FrameGuard` to `FrameRecord` so that the wrapper's entry is the frame's. The evaluators run on CPython, which cannot be told which exception is being handled: the clause runs inside `try: %raise_handled(match) except: ...`, which makes it so, and what a raise changes (the context, the evaluator's own frames on the traceback) is put back where an evaluator catches it (eval1's `_unwrap`, eval2's `strip`); they cannot reproduce that refusal's context nor the wrapper's frame entry, which only C-3-62 reads. A return, break or continue in a clause is CPython's SyntaxError before the pass runs. Under the three legs C-3-16 passes; C-3-62 passes compiled (it reads traceback lines, which an evaluator's are not). `pipeline/tests/test_system_modules.py::test_except_star` compares the refusals' words, a `split` that answers wrongly, a `__subclasscheck__` and clauses that yield. Still a stop: the report of an uncaught exception group (its nested format).

**2026-11-12 — the report of an exception group and of notes (C-8-12).** The runtime's report of an uncaught exception (which stands for `traceback.TracebackException`'s, as `_PyErr_Display` hands it over; file and line only, D-06) is now a port of its construction and its `format()`, compact, with the defaults (fifteen parts and ten levels): each exception's record built when the queue reaches it (last in, first out), with its `str()`, its `__notes__` (a getattr with a default; another error becomes the note "Ignored error getting __notes__: ..."), its cause and context, and a group's parts, every record counting as seen once built, so that a chain ends at an exception already recorded (RL-226) as CPython's does when groups share exceptions; then the boxes and margins of `_ExceptionPrintContext` (`  + Exception Group Traceback`, `+-+---- 1 ----`, `| ` on every line, empty ones too, the closing rule, "and 2 more exceptions", "... (max_group_depth is 10)"), and the notes after the exception's line: a sequence's items by `str()` (`<note str() failed>`), each line on its own, anything else by `repr()`. Whether `__notes__` is a sequence is `collections.abc.Sequence`'s answer when `_collections_abc` is loaded; when it is not, nothing can have been registered with it, and the runtime's types answer as CPython registers them. The harness masks a group's frames as it masks any (D-06): after the box's margin, the echo lines that follow a frame are dropped (`mask_stderr`); no recording had such lines, and all 358 still reproduce. The two stops this replaces are gone. C-8-12 passes compiled; an evaluator prints its own frames.

**2026-11-12 — logging's call sites (C-7-78) and `[replaced]`.** A program that imported `logging` stopped there: its module body asks `hasattr(sys, "_getframe")`, and the name stopped by name. The specification makes `logging` \[P1\] with one adaptation, `findCaller` taking call-site information from the compiler instead of `sys._getframe` (section 7; E-02 keeps frames out). Done with the mechanism the manifest already described and nothing applied: `[replaced]` names functions of a module's CPython text that its adaptation replaces, and `runtime/replaced/<module>.py` holds their bodies; each def repeats CPython's signature (a class there is only the path to its methods), and the frontend (`replace_functions`, right after parsing, so that CPython's compile check and the look marks see the new body) puts the body in place, keeps CPython's docstring and gives the new statements the line of the body's first, so the code object keeps CPython's names and lines; another signature, or a name CPython's text lacks, is refused. The files are part of what a unit's key holds (`_pipeline_bytes`), and `tools/tests/test_replaced.py` holds the manifest, the files and CPython's signatures together. `logging.Logger.findCaller` walks the call sites the runtime keeps for each frame CPython has (`%call_sites`: file, the line the frame is at, the function's name, this frame's first), with CPython's loop and `_is_internal_frame`'s test on each file; with `stack_info` the stack is `traceback.print_stack`'s, file and line (D-06). `sys._getframe` now exists, as in CPython, so `hasattr` answers True, and calling it stops by name (E-02); `logging.currentframe` stops the same way. The evaluators have no frames of the program's, so their `%call_sites` is empty and a record names "(unknown file)", as CPython's findCaller does when there is no frame. Two more entries came with it: `traceback._find_incompatible_extension_module` finds no extension module built for another Python version (the adaptation the manifest already had), and `re._compiler._bytes_to_codes` reads a charset's bitmap as the engine's code words from the bytes, little-endian as every target is, where CPython casts a memoryview (\[P2+\]): the same list, so a pattern with `re.I` and a letter whose case folding leaves Latin-1 (`s` and `ſ`, `k` and `K`), which logging compiles at import, no longer stops. The lock is unchanged. C-7-78 passes compiled; under the evaluators a record names no call site. C-7-02 (traceback formatting a ModuleNotFoundError, RL-60), which nothing replaced until now, failed compiled with an uncaught exception, the search's own import failing inside the handler; it now stops by name further on, at the path finder the suggestion of a module name asks for (E-04), which is still to be settled.

**2026-11-12 — subclasses of `set` and `frozenset` (C-3-63).** A `SetInstance`, kind `Set`, flag `HAS_ATTRIBUTES`, as `ListInstance` is for list (C-3-51); `SetObject` moved from runtime.hpp into object.hpp beside `List`, so that the instance's death sits with the others'. One thing is set's own: set keeps a weak reference list of its own (`tp_weaklistoffset`), so `subtype_dealloc` leaves the weak references to `set_dealloc`, which clears them after the instance's slots and dict and before the elements, where a list subclass's go before its slots (measured: a callback runs after the dict's values for a set subclass, before them for a list subclass). The model has it first (`RefClass.base_weaklist`, inherited with the layout; `test_a_base_that_keeps_its_own_weak_references_clears_them_after_the_dict` compares both orders with CPython's), and `release_attributes` takes the same flag. `set.__new__` makes an empty instance of the class whatever its arguments; `frozenset.__new__` refuses keywords only while the class's `__init__` is still frozenset's, names the class in its count's message (`tp_name`), and gives back the argument itself only for the exact type and an exact frozenset (`make_new_frozenset`). What iteration, `len`, `in`, truth and hashing read of a set now asks first whether a subclass overrides the slot, as list's and dict's do; the operators and comparisons already took a subclass's methods. What CPython's set code reads of another set's table it still reads directly (`PyAnySet_Check`: `set(s)`, `union`, `issubset` and the others see a subclass's elements whatever its `__iter__` says), and `set.__iter__` is the table's own iterator, which a subclass's `super().__iter__()` reaches without coming back. What a set's methods and operators make is the base type (`make_new_set_basetype`), `frozenset.copy` of a subclass a new frozenset; the methods' messages name the method (`set.copy()`), not the subclass. `set.__reduce__` and `frozenset.__reduce__` are written (`set_reduce`: the class, a list of the elements through the class's iteration, and `__getstate__`), which `copy` takes for a subclass. The repr asks the table whether it is empty, not the subclass's `__len__`. The exit walk finalises a set subclass's instance. C-3-63 passes on all three legs; `test_set_subclasses` compares the words and the order of a death. Left: subclasses of `bytes` and `bytearray`, which stop by name.

**2026-11-12 — subclasses of `bytes` and `bytearray` (C-3-64).** A `BytesInstance`, the base's kind with `HAS_ATTRIBUTES`, the attribute storage beside the octets; `BytesObject` moved into object.hpp with it. bytes has an item size, so its subclass refuses `__slots__` other than `__dict__` and `__weakref__` ("arbitrary __slots__ not supported for subtype of 'bytes'", as for int) and has no weak reference unless asked; bytearray's has both, and neither base keeps a weak reference list of its own, so a dying instance clears its weak references first (`subtype_dealloc`). `bytes.__new__` for a subclass makes the exact bytes the arguments ask for and copies the octets into an instance of the class (`bytes_subtype_new`); `bytearray.__new__` makes an empty one of the class, which `__init__` fills. Where CPython hands back the object it was given it does so for an exact bytes only (`PyBytes_CheckExact`, `return_self`, `STRINGLIB_CHECK_EXACT`): a full slice, `* 1`, `+ b""` (the empty left side asked first, as `bytes_concat` asks), strip and replace with nothing to do, split, splitlines, a join of one, `bytes(x)`, `__bytes__`; measured, the one exception is `partition` and `rpartition` when the separator is absent, which hand back the object whatever its class (`stringlib_partition`), and the separator they hand back is the one given. `__getnewargs__` makes a new bytes of the octets. `bytearray.__reduce__` and `__reduce_ex__` are written (`_common_reduce`: a latin-1 str below protocol 3, the octets from 3 on, an empty tuple for an empty one, and `__getstate__`), which `copy` takes for a subclass; a bytearray subclass's repr names its class. Iteration, `len`, `in`, subscription, truth and hashing ask a subclass's overrides first. Left: the buffer protocol (\[P2+\]); `bytes % ...` followed the same day (C-6-20). C-3-64 passes on all three legs; `test_bytes_subclasses` compares the words, the identities and a death's order.

**2026-11-12 — `bytes % args` and `bytearray % args` (C-6-20; PEP 461).** Every bytes formatting stopped by name. `bytes_format` (format.cpp) is `_PyBytes_FormatEx`'s loop over the format's octets, read as the C reads them (a signed `char` where it reads `*fmt++`, masked where it masks, so an octet past 0x7f is no flag, digit or letter and is named by its code in the error), with its conversions: `%b` and `%s` by the octets of a bytes-like object or what `__bytes__` gives (another answer is "must return a bytes"; an object with `__buffer__` stops by name, \[P2+\]), `%a` and `%r` by `ascii()`, `%c` from an int in range(256) or one octet (`byte_converter`'s three refusals), the integers by `_PyUnicode_FormatLong` after `__index__` for `o`, `x` and `X` and `__int__` otherwise, the floats by `PyOS_double_to_string`, and `FORMAT_ERROR`'s prefixes (the mapping key's repr, the argument's index, or none). What counts as a mapping is what has `__getitem__` and is no tuple, bytes, str or bytearray, so a list on the right formats nothing and leaves nothing over; what is left over is reported with "(required N, got M)". The result is a bytes, or a bytearray when the left is one, never a subclass; `bytes.__mod__`, `__rmod__` and bytearray's are slot wrappers, which a subclass's `%` reaches. The fast paths of the C (an exact int with no width, precision or sign; a float likewise; `%c` with no width) give the same octets as the general path and are not written. C-6-20 passes on all three legs; `pipeline/tests/test_bytes.py::test_as_cpython[percent_messages]` compares every refusal's words.

**2026-11-12 — the S-1 entry points in compiled programs, and what typing's class syntax needs (C-3-65).** A module sweep showed one stop across eighteen common programs: `typing.NamedTuple` and `TypedDict` in class syntax read the class body's annotations with FORWARDREF, which needs the second entry point of a compiler-generated `__annotate__` (decision S-1). Until now only the replay had them. C-3-13, C-3-21 and C-3-22 stopped compiled for the same reason.
* **The build.** It runs `pipeline/staging/annotations.py` over each module it compiles (`annotate_entries`), as the dry run does, with the same checks against CPython's code. It lowers the kept evaluators into one unit per module, keeping the annotations' positions (`lower(tree=...)`).
* **The main unit** lists each entry point by its function's file, qualified name and first line, the key a compiled function's code carries. The build declines an evaluator whose key another code object of the module shares. The runtime also checks the parameter and free variables, and stops by name on a mismatch.
  * One shape shares a key: a type parameter's bound or constraints together with its default (`class G[U: int = str]`), two evaluate functions both named `U` on one line. They stop by name when read with STRING or FORWARDREF. Telling them apart needs an anchor in the compiled `Code`, such as the first expression's position, since CPython's positions are not there (TODO).
* **`annotate_rt.py`** is compiled with the prelude into a module of the runtime's own, in no `sys.modules` (`RUNTIME_PYTHON`). Its `import os` moved into `entry_key`, which only the build and the replay call.
* **The key fixes.** Making it agree with CPython found two differences in `__code__`, now fixed:
  * a decorated class's annotate function starts at the first decorator's line;
  * a module-level generic function's annotate function is named `__annotate__` alone (`_PyCompile_SetQualname`).
* **What came with it.**
  * `__bases__` assigned the bases a class has, which `NamedTuple` does to every class, model first (`RefClass.reassign_bases`). The class moves last among each base's subclasses and it and its subclasses are modified. Other bases, or a metaclass with its own `mro()`, stop by name.
  * `types.CellType()` and `types.CellType(x)`.
  * ModuleType's `__annotations__` and `__annotate__` getsets, so module stores and deletes now honour ModuleType's data descriptors.
  * A ninth marked change to the annotationlib shim. `transmogrify`'s `obj.__class__ = ForwardRef` becomes `_bella.set_class(obj, ForwardRef)`. The runtime switches only between two classes whose instances have one layout and no dict (`RefObject.set_class`). A program's own `__class__` assignment stays \[P2+\] (spec section 3), so the shim asks the runtime for the one CPython performs inside annotationlib. The replay's hook does the assignment itself.
* **Evidence.** C-3-13, C-3-21, C-3-22 and the new C-3-65 pass compiled. `eval1` and `eval2` run CPython's annotationlib, which re-runs the evaluator's host function, so these cases are not in their baselines.
* **Found and noted** (TODO):
  * Compiled tracebacks through annotationlib show the shim's line numbers, shifted from CPython's file by the shim's comment lines.
  * `C.__bases__` and `C.__mro__` are new tuples at each read; CPython's are one object each.
