**2026-11-12 — match's runtime half in compiled programs, and a type's tp_name (C-3-66).** C-3-17, C-8-08 and C-8-09 compiled stopped at the intrinsic `match_class`: `runtime/prelude/match.py`, the MATCH_* instructions in Python, ran under `eval1` and `eval2` only.
* **The runtime.** `match.py` joins `annotate_rt.py` among the runtime's own modules written in Python (`RUNTIME_PYTHON`): compiled with the prelude, in no `sys.modules`, run at the first statement that needs one of its five helpers (`load_match`, `runtime/src/call.cpp`). Its `__intrinsics__` go to the runtime as a prelude module's do. Its helpers now carry the names the frontend calls (`match_is_sequence`, `match_is_mapping`), so the evaluators' map of names became a set.
* **A type's name in the messages is its tp_name**, which no attribute of the class tells: a C type made from a spec keeps the spec's whole name (`re.Pattern`, `itertools.count`, `_struct.Struct`), a class statement's is its `__name__`. `match.py` guessed from `__flags__` and `__module__` and printed `Pattern()` where CPython prints `re.Pattern()`, under the evaluators too. It now takes `_bella_tp_name_of`, given by whoever runs it: the runtime's primitive, compiled; under the evaluators, `eval1._tp_name`, which had the same guess. That one now reads the name from CPython's own words: `type(None).__new__(t)` raises `NoneType.__new__(<tp_name>): <tp_name> is not a subtype of NoneType` (`tp_new_wrapper`), no class but NoneType is a subtype of NoneType, and the two known halves give the name back whole. This also corrects eval1's other tp_name messages (`cannot unpack non-iterable X object`, `Value after * must be an iterable`).
* **Length hints through the old protocol's iterator.** CPython asks a list's length hint of the iterator it extends from, and `iter_len` (`Objects/iterobject.c`) passes the question to the sequence's `__len__` while the iterator has one. The runtime asked only objects of a program's classes. It now asks the sequence of a live sequence iterator too: `list(iter(r))`, `sorted`, a star display, a slice assignment, and the starred rest of an unpacking, whose list `PySequence_List(it)` builds after the names before the star are taken. A `__len__` that returns no int is no hint; a negative one is a ValueError that propagates, as in CPython. `tuple()` and `bytearray()` ask no hint on the pin and still do not.
* **`str.join`** goes through `PySequence_Fast` first, as `PyUnicode_Join` does: every item is taken before the first is looked at, so a generator runs to its end before `sequence item 1: expected str instance` is raised.
* **Evidence.** C-3-17, C-8-08, C-8-09 and the new C-3-66 pass compiled. C-3-66 holds the order in which a class pattern reads its attributes and a mapping or sequence pattern asks lengths, keys and items, and which exception each malformed pattern raises. `pipeline/tests/test_compiled_match.py` compares the words of nineteen errors and twenty-six hint paths with CPython, compiled and under eval1.
* **Found and noted** (TODO): no message in the runtime truncates a name to CPython's `%.80s` or `%.200s`. A type name longer than that is printed whole.

**2026-11-12 — the shims keep CPython's line numbers (C-3-67).** A compiled traceback through annotationlib showed the shim's line numbers, shifted from CPython's file by the shim's header and by changes longer than the lines they replace; `codeop` and `_collections_abc` had the same drift.
* **The rule.** Every line of CPython's stays at its own line number: a marked change takes as many lines as it replaces, and what a shim adds (its header, its helpers) comes after CPython's last line. A replaced line that has no counterpart is kept as a `# bella:` comment in its place (`_collections_abc`'s four probed types). `tools/tests/test_shims.py` checks it for every shim, beside the rule that every change is marked.
* **annotationlib's FORWARDREF of a hand-written annotate function.** The shim had a thirteen-line block that called the function with VALUE_WITH_FAKE_GLOBALS and answered VALUE on its `NotImplementedError`. CPython's own lines do that, so the block is gone. FORWARDREF's rebuild now passes `value_only=True` to `_bella.annotate_with`. For a function the compiler did not generate, that gives a stand-in, a built-in with no frame of its own: it calls the function with its own globals and raises its `NotImplementedError` as it is. CPython's line 839 then answers VALUE, and an error that call raises has CPython's frames and context. Anything else, an answer or another error, needs the re-run against substitute globals and is refused, as before.
  * `_build_closure` now runs for such a function, so `annotate_freevars` gives a hand-written function's own free variables. The stand-in reads none of the cells.
  * `has_entry_point` is no longer asked, and is gone from the runtime and the replay.
  * STRING of a hand-written function stops by name, as before.
* **The replay** (`sitecustomize.py`) gives the same answers, with one difference: its stand-in is a Python function, so the `NotImplementedError`'s traceback has a frame more. C-3-67 prints only the context's class; `pipeline/tests/test_entry_points.py` compares its frames compiled.
* **codeop**: the refusal is `codeob = _bella.refuse(...)` on CPython's line 117, in place of a refusal and an unreached `compile`.
* **Evidence.** C-3-67 passes compiled on every frame's line: NameError through `get_annotations` and `call_annotate_function`, `ForwardRef.evaluate`, and the hand-written rows, closure included. It is not in the eval1 and eval2 baselines: their tracebacks carry the interpreter's frames, and CPython's own annotationlib re-runs their host functions. The closure locks moved with the shims' import lines, which are now CPython's.

**2026-11-12 — UTF-16 and UTF-32 (C-7-79), and base32 (C-7-07).** The specification lists UTF-16 and UTF-32 among phase 1's codecs (section 7, Text); `str.encode`, `bytes.decode` and `codecs` stopped at them, and C-7-07 stopped first at `binascii.b2a_base32`.
* **The codecs** are in `runtime/src/bytes.cpp`, from `_PyUnicode_EncodeUTF16` and 32, `PyUnicode_DecodeUTF16Stateful` and 32, and stringlib's `utf16_decode`. byteorder 0 writes the native order's BOM and reads one to choose the order; below 0 is little-endian, above 0 big-endian, and the host is little-endian, as the reference's build is. Each decode error names `utf-16-le`, `utf-16-be`, `utf-32-le` or `utf-32-be` and covers the C's range:
  * UTF-16: `truncated data`, `unexpected end of data`, `illegal encoding`, `illegal UTF-16 surrogate`;
  * UTF-32: `truncated data`, the surrogate range, `code point not in range(0x110000)`.

  A handler's new position may fall inside a unit, and decoding goes on from there. A stateful call that is not final keeps a trailing part unit, or a high surrogate at the end, for the next call.
* **The encoders** call the handler for one character at a time, as the C does. Bytes it gives must be whole units and text must be ASCII, so `surrogateescape` fails for every surrogate. The UTF-32 encoder names itself plain `utf-32` for a byteorder other than -1, 0 and 1, so `surrogatepass` writes the native order's bytes even into a big-endian stream; that is CPython's answer too.
* **The handlers.** `surrogatepass` decodes UTF-16 and UTF-32 surrogates as `PyCodec_SurrogatePassErrors` does. `surrogateescape` takes at most four bytes; no UTF-8 error covers more, so UTF-8 is unchanged.
* **`_codecs`'** twelve functions take CPython's parameters by position, in the clinic's order: the buffer, errors, the byteorder as a C int, then final. The ex decoders return the byteorder they ended with.
* **base32.** `binascii.a2b_base32` and `b2a_base32` come from `binascii_a2b_base32_impl` and `b2a_base32_impl` (`cee9417`).
* **Evidence.** C-7-79 passes on all three legs, and C-7-07 now passes compiled. `pipeline/tests/test_system_modules.py::test_wide_codecs` and `test_base32` compare every message with CPython's.

**2026-11-12 — a class's `__mro__` and `__bases__` are objects (C-3-68).** Each read made a new tuple; CPython's `tp_mro` and `tp_bases` are one object each until they are replaced. Model first: `RefClass.bases` is the tuple the class was made with, and `reassign_bases` keeps the tuple assigned and makes the class and every subclass a new MRO tuple, equal to the old one (`_mro_hierarchy`, after `mro_hierarchy`); `refmodel/tests/test_cases.py` compares the identities with CPython's, and `selftest` stays green on three seeds. The header gives `ClassObject` a `bases_tuple` and an `mro_tuple`, owned, made at the first read when the class has none yet, released by `dispose` with the class. `type_new` keeps the tuple it is given, so `__bases__` is the very tuple a metaclass handed to `type.__new__`; a class made with no bases gets an `(object,)` of its own. `__bases__` assigned keeps the tuple assigned and drops the hierarchy's MRO tuples. Classes are immortal until the collector exists; a class and its MRO tuple form a cycle then, as in CPython.

**2026-11-12 — `cmath` (C-7-80; C-7-08 passes compiled).** `cmath` is \[P1\] (section 7, Numbers) and was not in the runtime. It is a prelude, `runtime/prelude/cmath.py`, ported from Modules/cmathmodule.c and its clinic. It runs over the C library through `_libm`, which answers the result and errno; `hypot` is new there, since cmath calls the library's and `math.hypot` is CPython's own. An argument becomes a complex through the runtime's `PyComplex_AsCComplex` (`_as_c_complex` in complex.cpp): `__complex__`, a complex subclass read as stored, then `__float__` or `__index__`.
* **Special values.** C99 Annex G's special values are the C's seven-by-seven tables.
* **Algorithms and errno.** Each function's algorithm comes line for line from the C, with the errno it leaves; the clinic's protected wrappers turn that errno into ValueError or OverflowError.
  * `log` with a base keeps the C's order: c_log of the base replaces x's errno, so `log(0, 2)` is `(-inf+nanj)`, and `_Py_c_quot` sets EDOM for a zero divisor.
  * `phase` raises OverflowError where glibc's `atan2` underflows and says ERANGE.
* **Checked against CPython.** The prelude, run under CPython with a ctypes `_libm` that reads the real errno, agrees with CPython's cmath bit for bit on 387,996 calls, every zero's and NaN's sign included.
* **NaN signs.** IEEE 754 does not interpret the sign of a NaN that an operation makes from two NaNs, and x86 gives the first operand's. CPython's build is the pin's, the same binary in the image. Clang vectorises `_Py_c_quot` and turns `a.imag*ratio - a.real` into `-a.real + ratio*a.imag`, which is the same number but takes the other NaN. The port follows the pin's machine code, with the first operand's NaN taken explicitly. The runtime's compiled `+` and `*` take the second operand's NaN (GCC commutes them), so the port does not rely on them.
* **Tests.** C-7-80 rounds what passes through libm (the libm rule of 2026-11-08) and prints classes of errors. `pipeline/tests/test_system_modules.py::test_cmath` compares every digit, sign and message on one host. The import-stop row in `test_compiled_imports.py` moved from cmath to `resource`, a C module outside the manifest.
* **Found on the way, with the author.** Of two NaN operands, CPython's float `+` and `*` give the left one's and Bella's compiled ones give the right one's; `-` and `/` agree. Complex `+` differs too: CPython's gives the right operand's NaN, a vectorisation artifact of the pin. Whether NaN signs follow the pin's machine code or are left unspecified, like libm's last bits, is a question for the author.


**2026-11-12 — `unicodedata` (C-7-81; C-7-15 passes compiled).** `unicodedata` is \[P1\] since review log D-8 and was not in the runtime. Its tables are CPython's own. `tools/unicodedata_source.py` copies the pin's three generated headers whole, from the release whose SHA-256 it names, as `tools/tokenizer_source.py` does for the lexer: Modules/unicodedata_db.h, Modules/unicodename_db.h and Objects/unicodetype_db.h become `runtime/src/unicodedata_db.inc`, `unicodename_db.inc` and `unicodetype_db.inc`, about 2.3 MB. `--check` compares them with a source tree; it runs by hand when the pin moves. Licence register L-03 covers the data; the port of unicodedata.c comes under CPython's licence stack, still open there, and the work proceeds.
* **The port.** `runtime/src/unicodedata.cpp` is Modules/unicodedata.c's algorithms in its shape and order, on code points instead of str objects:
  * a character's record and its changes back to 3.2.0;
  * the decomposition's text;
  * NFD and NFKD with the canonical ordering (both of the C's sorts), NFC and NFKC, and the quick check;
  * the names both ways (Hangul syllables, the derived names, the DAWG, the aliases and named sequences);
  * the blocks and TR29's grapheme rules.

  Objects/unicodectype.c's lookups over the type records answer decimal, digit, numeric and the XID predicates. The two places where the C would overflow a fixed array (its decomposition stack of 20 and its list of 20 skipped characters) stop by name instead; the tables never reach them.
* **The prelude.** `runtime/prelude/unicodedata.py` does what the C does with objects, through one primitive, `_ucd_call`:
  * the clinic's conversions and their words: a character is a str of length one, read as stored (`PyUnicode_GET_LENGTH`, not `len()`); `lookup` takes a str's UTF-8 or a bytes object's bytes, refuses a bytearray as not read-only, and says "a bytes-like object is required" for the rest;
  * the module's functions and the UCD type's methods, one C function each, given the module or the 3.2.0 database as self;
  * `ucd_3_2_0`, made by the module alone;
  * the grapheme iterator and its Segment objects, types of no module name;
  * `_ucnhash_CAPI`, an instance of `types.CapsuleType` with CPython's repr.

  A string comes back itself where the current database's quick check says it is normalised; a str subclass comes back as a str. The names table now has the module's types and PyCapsule.
* **Checked.** A sweep took every code point through both databases: each property, the name and its lookup back, the four forms and `is_normalized`, plus the 3.15 properties. It also covered the four forms and the grapheme clusters of 3,000 composable starters before and after 81 combining marks. Compiled, all eighteen of its checksums equal CPython's. The sweep takes 11m38s compiled against 26s under CPython: each call goes through the prelude, which a measurement may one day move into C++. C-7-81 prints the database's corner cases and the class of every error. `pipeline/tests/test_system_modules.py::test_unicodedata` compares the words of every error and every 61st code point on one host.
* **Found on the way, and fixed.** `bytes.__len__`, `__iter__`, `__contains__` and `__getitem__` called on a subclass's instance ran the subclass's overrides, and so did bytearray's, because the slot wrappers called `len()`, `iter()`, `in` and `[]`. They now read the object's own bytes, as tuple's and str's slots do (`runtime/src/slots.cpp`). C-3-64 has the rows.
* **Stop rows.** test_re's row for `\N{DIGIT ONE}` went, since it compiles now. The runtime's own stops that wait for the Unicode database are still there, and the type records they need are in the runtime now:
  * `int()`, `float()` and `complex()` of a str beyond ASCII;
  * a format specification or field name beyond ASCII;
  * the `namereplace` error handler;
  * a `__slots__` name beyond ASCII.

  `lookup` of a memoryview waits for memoryview (\[P2+\]); C-7-81 leaves it out.

**2026-11-12 — the Unicode database in the builtins (C-6-21).** The runtime's stops that waited for the database are gone; each place now reads the pin's tables as the C does.
* **Numbers from a str.** `int()`, `float()` and `complex()` of a str beyond ASCII parse it as `_PyUnicode_TransformDecimalAndSpaceToASCII` gives it (`decimal_and_space_to_ascii` in `runtime/src/unicode.cpp`):
  * a Unicode space becomes a space, and a decimal digit its ASCII digit;
  * the first character that is neither ends the text with a '?'; past ASCII, DEL is such a character, since the C's test is `ch < 127`;
  * the message names the str as given, the digit limit counts the digits of the text, and underscores are the C's.
* **Formatting.** A format specification's width and precision and a format field's index read any decimal digit (`Py_UNICODE_TODECIMAL`); zero padding stays the ASCII '0'. A `%` format's unknown character is shown with its code when `Py_UNICODE_ISPRINTABLE` says it is printable, and by its code alone when it is not.
* **namereplace.** The handler writes the name C API's answer (`getname` with aliases and named sequences), or the hex escape where there is none. It does so inside the ASCII, Latin-1, UTF-8, UTF-16 and UTF-32 encoders and as `codecs.namereplace_errors` (the `_codecs` prelude, through a `charname` operation). A decoder given it raises the TypeError of a handler that cannot take a UnicodeDecodeError. Like CPython, a plane-15 code point where the database keeps its aliases and named sequences is written with that alias's name or that sequence's (U+F0000 becomes `\N{NULL}`).
* **`__slots__`.** A slot's name is an identifier by `PyUnicode_IsIdentifier` (XID_Start or '_', then XID_Continue), now `is_identifier` in unicode.cpp, which `str.isidentifier` shares.
* **Tests.** The stop rows went, from test_bytes, test_codecs, test_complex, test_percent, test_str_methods and test_review_2026_11_03. `test_str_methods.py::test_the_unicode_database_in_the_builtins` compares the words of the new paths of `int()`, `float()`, `complex()`, the formats and `__slots__` with CPython's; namereplace's are in `test_review_2026_11_12.py` (RL-435: this entry first said every new path, and the handler was in none).

**2026-11-12 — the path finder's discovery, `_suggestions`, and traceback from its entries (C-8-13, C-7-82, C-7-83; C-7-02 and C-7-14 pass compiled).** C-7-02 stopped where traceback suggests a module name for a ModuleNotFoundError; three things stood behind it, and unittest needed two more.
* **Discovery in the closed world.** `PathFinder.discover` (3.15) lists the modules a path entry holds, and traceback asks every finder of `sys.meta_path` that has the method. The registry stands for the path finders (2026-11-03), so the path entry finder of a directory lists the modules the build found in it, named under the parent package (or at the top level for `sys.path`), sorted, each with the spec an import gives it (`_bella_discover` in imports.cpp, through bootstrap.py's `_spec_from_compiled`). The rest is CPython's text, laziness and the ValueError for a module that is no package included.
  * A module's directory is its file's, and a package's is the one that holds it. The build resolves symbolic links, so a module reached through one is listed where its file is, as its `__file__` says.
  * What the build did not compile is in no list: the manifest's omissions, and CPython's frozen modules the program does not reach (the path finder lists files, whatever is frozen).
  * A namespace package, whose portions the registry does not keep, stops by name.
  * Section 8 already says discovery finds only the modules compiled into the program. CPython lists every file, the program's own and the main script among them, so a case asks only what both agree on.
* **`_suggestions`.** The module traceback imports for every "Did you mean" is a prelude now (`runtime/prelude/_suggestions.py`, a core module of the manifest's), with Modules/_suggestions.c's argument checks, messages and error order. The choice is the runtime's `keyword_suggestion`, now Python/suggestions.c line for line. It had left out two limits: nothing from 750 candidates on, and nothing when more than 40 bytes of UTF-8 differ once the common prefix and suffix are trimmed. Every keyword-argument hint shares it, ceval's and the preludes' clinic.
* **traceback from its entries.** The manifest's adaptation for traceback is "formatting from source maps; file and line in release builds (D-06)". A traceback's entry stands for its frame, and three more functions are replaced:
  * `_walk_tb_with_full_positions` yields each entry itself with its line and no positions, where CPython reads the frame's code positions (E-02);
  * `StackSummary._extract_from_extended_frame_gen` reads an entry's file and function from the code it stands for (the intrinsic `__bella_traceback_code__`, the code object `__code__` gives) and gives the summary an empty line, so nothing is looked up. No source line follows the file and line, and `FrameSummary.line` is `''` (D-06);
  * `clear_frames` clears each entry (`__bella_traceback_clear__`): an entry holds the locals of a frame the exception left, and gives them up last first, as `frame.clear()` releases them. A frame still running has given the entry none, where CPython's refuses and `clear_frames` goes on. unittest's `assertRaises` calls it on every exception that passes through.

  Limits, `sys.tracebacklimit`, the folding of a recursion and chains are CPython's text. An object that is no traceback of the runtime's (one of the program's own making that stands for a traceback or a frame) is walked, read and cleared as CPython does it, and `capture_locals` stops by name. `traceback.tb_next` can be assigned now, with CPython's checks (None or a traceback, no delete, no loop): unittest relinks a failure's traceback.
* **unittest's own entries.** `TestResult._is_relevant_tb_level` reads `tb.tb_frame.f_globals` to hide unittest's frames. It is replaced (a new entry in `[adaptations]` and `[replaced]`) by the globals of the entry's frame: an entry now holds its frame's module, with a count, as CPython's frame holds its globals (a staged text's module lives only as long as something holds it), and `__bella_traceback_globals__` gives its dict, or an empty dict for `_PyTraceback_Add`'s entry, as CPython's has. The spec's Development row names both adaptations; it names no new commitment, only how the P1 rows are kept without frames (E-02). The locks are unchanged.
* **Left.** traceback's suggestion for a raised AttributeError or NameError reads the innermost frame's locals (`tb.tb_frame.f_locals`, and NameError's globals and builtins), and still stops at `tb_frame`; an AttributeError or NameError made without raising it formats with its suggestion. `traceback.walk_tb`, `walk_stack`, `extract_stack` and `print_stack` read frames and stop the same way. A frame that caught the exception and returned keeps its locals through the traceback in CPython and not here (RL-222, open since step 3c), so `clear_frames` has nothing of such a frame to release.
* **Cases and tests.** C-8-13: discovery under a package and at the top level, the specs, the suggestions for a package's modules. C-7-82: `_suggestions` at its limits, its errors by class, traceback's suggestions for an attribute, an ImportError's name and a NameError without a frame. C-7-83: extract_tb, format_tb, the limits, a folded recursion, a chain, TracebackException and print_exc, the first line of each piece only. All three pass compiled and agree under CPython in the image. `pipeline/tests/test_traceback_entries.py` compares with CPython on one host: the words of the new errors; unittest's reports of failures and errors, `assertRaises` in its three outcomes among them, which name the test's frames and none of unittest's; the death of a frame's locals at `clear_frames`; and the globals of a staged text's entries after its namespace is dropped. Compiled only, it checks that a module beside the program is discovered and suggested, and the capture_locals stop.
* **Reviewed before the commit.** A second pass over this work found the entry's module held without a count (a text's module could be freed under it), frozen modules the build does not compile placed in the standard library's directory whatever their name, the walk yielding an object that is no traceback as if it were one, and `clear_frames` missing, which the test of unittest's reports met at `assertRaises`. All four are fixed above.

**2026-11-12 — review F21 (RL-430 to RL-444).** The author asked for the last commits made under Claude Opus 5.5 to be read again; a review agent read `6519433..3234866` (cmath, unicodedata, the database in the builtins) without building, and the session reproduced its fifteen findings compiled (review log, F21). All were confirmed. What changed:
* **A base's own hash (RL-430).** `bytes.__hash__` and `frozenset.__hash__` compute the base's hash from the object, so a subclass's `__hash__` may call `super().__hash__()`; before, that never ended. A sweep of every inheritable built-in and every method it has, 360 pairs, now agrees with CPython and stays as a test.
* **namereplace (RL-431, RL-432, RL-435).** The handler gives back where its loop stopped, as `PyCodec_NameReplaceErrors` does. Its first use imports `unicodedata` and takes the module's capsule, as `_PyUnicode_GetNameCAPI` does, so the module is in `sys.modules` afterwards and what is no capsule is refused with `PyCapsule_Import`'s words.
* **unicodedata (RL-433, RL-434, RL-444).** Each answer is a new str, as `PyUnicode_FromString` makes it. The two primitives that read a Segment's or an iterator's fields no longer trust them: `PyUnicode_Substring`'s clamps, and a stop by name for fields the C could not hold. The prelude reads the Unicode version and the names' longest length from the copied headers, and the copier takes its stamps from its source and checks the archive's hash.
* **Smaller.** `int()`, `float()` and `complex()` of an ASCII str read its text where it is (RL-437); one maker for a C type without instances (RL-441); the slot loops name each type once (RL-440); a docstring (RL-442); the direct-run blocks of the two test modules of the range (RL-443).
* **Left open, in TODO.** The per-call cost of the unicodedata prelude (RL-436), the two copies of str's character properties (RL-438), the calling-convention helpers every prelude types again (RL-439), and the direct-run blocks of nine more test modules (RL-443). Found by the sweep and left as the stops they are: `list.__iadd__` and `list.__imul__` read as attributes, and a module attribute of the names table read after the program deleted it.

**2026-11-13 — the running stack, a frame of the runtime's own, list's in-place methods, and names a program deleted (C-7-84, C-8-14, C-3-51).** Three stops left by the last two entries, and what a sweep of the manifest's modules showed.
* **The running stack.** `traceback.extract_stack`, `format_stack` and `print_stack`, asked for the running stack (no frame given), asked `sys._getframe` and stopped. They are replaced (`[replaced]`, three more functions of traceback): the runtime gives the stack as `walk_stack` would (`__bella_stack__`: a frame and the line it is at for every frame a traceback shows, the asking frame's first), and the rest is CPython's text through `StackSummary.extract`, limits and `sys.tracebacklimit` included. A frame given is walked as CPython walks it, so what is no frame raises CPython's AttributeError. `walk_stack` and `walk_tb` hand frames to the program and still stop by name (E-02), as does `capture_locals`.
* **A frame of the runtime's own.** The last entry let a traceback's entry stand for its frame inside traceback. That also let a program pass a traceback where `StackSummary.extract` wants a frame and get a summary, where CPython raises AttributeError. The walk now gives a frame object of the runtime's (`__bella_traceback_frame__`; a new kind, `FrameObject`: its code and its module, of the type `types.FrameType` is), and the summary reads the code of such a frame only (`__bella_frame_code__`, in place of `__bella_traceback_code__`). Nothing of it is the program's to read: its attributes stop by name through the names table, and so does its repr now, for this frame and for the one a signal handler is given, where object's repr printed another text than CPython's (`<frame at ..., file ..., line ..., code ...>`).
* **`list.__iadd__` and `list.__imul__`.** list has its two in-place sequence slots as attributes (slot wrappers over `list_inplace_concat` and `list_inplace_repeat`), so a subclass's `__iadd__` can ask the base for it. The operators themselves were right before and take the same path as before.
* **A name the program deleted.** A native module's missing name stops by name when the names table has it. That also met a name the program itself had deleted (`del math.pi`, `vars(math).pop("tau")`, `del __file__`), where CPython raises AttributeError, ImportError or NameError. The runtime now records each native module's names once it is filled, sys's and builtins's before the program starts, and a program module's first names before its body runs. A recorded name that is absent later is one the program deleted, and is absent as in CPython: for attribute reads, `dir()`, the from-import and the global lookup, which goes on to builtins (so `__name__` after `del __name__` is `'builtins'`). A name of CPython's that was never there still stops. The names of `builtins` itself are not a dialect program's to delete (E-06), so the case leaves them alone; one row of `test_system_modules.py` holds that such a program gets CPython's NameError today and not a stop that blames the runtime, and goes when builtins are bound at compile time.
* **The sweep.** One program per module of the manifest (118: the roots, mapped and core) printed every public name with its type, its module and whether it is callable, compiled and under CPython. 101 agree. The others are the spec's own (decimal, datetime, hashlib, hmac and secrets without their C accelerators, D-20), what stops by name as decided (the bootstrap modules' listing, `unittest.IsolatedAsyncioTestCase`, three modules no program imports), and a list of names the runtime does not have yet: in builtins `input`, `exit`, `quit`, `copyright`, `credits`, `license`, `help`, `breakpoint`, `locals`, `aiter`, `anext`, `__build_class__` and `__debug__`; fifty of sys (the hooks `displayhook` and `breakpointhook`, `abi_info`, `gettrace`, `getprofile`, `getrefcount`, `path_hooks`, the lazy-import switches, and CPython's internals); `zlib.__getattr__`, `_locale._DATE_FMT`, `_ast._is_abstract`. `input` and site's `exit` and `quit` are the next step.
* **Cases and tests.** C-7-84: the stack at module level, in functions, methods, a generator, a comprehension and a built-in's callback, the limits, `format_stack` and `print_stack`, and what is no frame. C-8-14: a name deleted from math, itertools, sys, time, errno, string and the module itself, by `del`, `delattr` and `vars()`, and set again; it passes under both evaluators and compiled. C-7-84 passes compiled only: an evaluator's frames are its own, so its stack is empty there, as logging's caller is. C-3-51 gained the in-place methods as attributes. `test_traceback_entries.py` compares the words, a staged text's frames and a frame handed back to `StackSummary` with CPython, and holds five stops; `test_list_subclass.py` the in-place methods' arguments and errors; `test_system_modules.py` the words of a deleted name and an emptied module dict. The sweep of a base's own methods (review F21) takes list's two in-place methods in.
* **Left.** A slot wrapper's `__doc__` and `__text_signature__` stop by name (`list.__add__.__doc__`). A handler's frame could carry its code and line the same way, so that `traceback.print_stack(frame)` in a signal handler works: the author's, with the frames question. A class of a C module that allows it (ast's node classes) with a name deleted is not recorded and stops as before.

**2026-11-13 — `input()`, `breakpoint()`, and the names site adds to builtins (C-6-22).** The sweep of the manifest's modules (the entry above) listed the builtins the runtime stopped at. Three of them the spec had already decided: `input` is \[P1\] in the Builtins row, `breakpoint()` is D-11, and `exit`, `quit`, `help`, `copyright`, `credits` and `license` are the row's own exception ("which `site` adds and the closed world does not").
* **`input`.** A function of the builtins prelude, `builtin_input_impl` in its order: the three standard streams (one that is None or gone is "lost"), `sys.stderr` flushed, then one of two ways.
  * At a terminal (sys.stdin and sys.stdout are the process's descriptors 0 and 1 and both terminals): the prompt is encoded as sys.stdout would encode it and goes to the C stderr, and the line comes from the C stdin (`PyOS_Readline`, which is `PyOS_StdioReadline` while no readline module is loaded; the manifest has none). The last byte goes as the newline, then a carriage return, and the rest is decoded as sys.stdin would decode it; nothing read is EOFError without arguments. A read a signal interrupts runs the handlers that are due and goes on unless one raised (`my_fgets`); `_PyOS_InterruptOccurred` is `interrupt_occurred` in signals.cpp.
  * Anywhere else: `str(prompt)` written to sys.stdout, which is flushed, and `sys.stdin.readline()` without its one newline (`PyFile_GetLine`: a str or a bytes as given, EOFError for an empty one, TypeError for anything else, with `%T`'s names). A flush that fails is passed over, as `PyErr_Clear` passes over whatever was raised.
  * No event is audited: `sys.addaudithook` is not in the sys surface, so no hook can be there.
* **`breakpoint` (D-11).** D-11 says a warning and on; it does not say which warning. A compiled program is a CPython without pdb, and CPython has words for that: `sys.breakpointhook` warns `Ignoring unimportable $PYTHONBREAKPOINT: "pdb.set_trace"` (a RuntimeWarning at the caller's line) and returns None. `breakpoint()` does that, and nothing at all under `PYTHONBREAKPOINT=0`, as CPython. So the same program gives the same output under CPython once `pdb` cannot be imported there, which is how the test compares them. sys has no `breakpointhook` of its own (the sys surface of section 7), but one the program puts there is called with the arguments, as `builtin_breakpoint` calls it. A hook named by `PYTHONBREAKPOINT` would be an import by a computed name and stops by name. **For the author:** the wording is my reading of D-11; a text of Bella's own would be one line to change.
* **What site adds.** The names table was made by a CPython that had run `site`, so the six names stopped "CPython has it; the runtime does not yet". `tools/cpython_names.py` now leaves out what `_sitebuiltins` made (it checks that these are the six), and a compiled program answers as `python -S` does: NameError, AttributeError on `builtins`, and none of them in `dir(builtins)`.
* **A limit that is older than this.** The runtime shows a warning through CPython's `warnings` module, which `_warnings` imports at the first warning it shows, as the C does. A program that imports `warnings` nowhere (nor a module that does) has no such module compiled in, and stops by name at that import: at `breakpoint()`'s warning as at any other of the runtime's. This is the question already open for `copyreg` (TODO: what a prelude imports is not in a program's closure), and D-11's "so a forgotten call does not fail" makes it sharper: **for the author**, whether `warnings` (and `copyreg`) are linked into every program, or a program that can reach the import gets them.
* **Cases and tests.** C-6-22: `input()` over stand-in streams that note what is asked of them, in which order; the results and the errors by class; the lost streams; a StringIO, a BytesIO and a text file as sys.stdin; `breakpoint()` under `PYTHONBREAKPOINT=0`. It passes compiled and agrees under CPython in the image. `pipeline/tests/test_input.py` compares with CPython on one host: the words; eleven sessions at a pseudo-terminal (a line, the end of the input, the end after part of a line, prompts that are no str, a null in the prompt, a prompt the terminal cannot show, bytes that are no text, a stream that is no terminal's, a handler that returns, one that raises, an interrupt), each waiting for the prompt before it types; `breakpoint()` with pdb unimportable; and the six names against `python -S`. `runtime/tests/test_prelude.py` runs the prelude's `input` and `breakpoint` under CPython against the builtins.

**2026-11-13 — the names the runtime lacks, kept as a list; the callables' own slots and the docstrings of slots and methods (C-3-69).** The sweep two entries up read each module's public names. This one asks the names table itself: every name of CPython's built-in modules and of each built-in type that a compiled program does not find.
* **The list.** `tools/missing_names.py` compiles one program that imports every native module of the manifest and prints each module's own names and the own names of every type it can reach (from those modules, builtins, sys and types, and a sample of objects: 379 types). What the table has beyond that is `conformance/names-baseline.txt`, one `scope:name` a line, and `pipeline/tests/test_missing_names.py` holds the two equal: a name that goes missing fails, and one that arrives is taken off. It started at 466 names and is at 414. It is not a to-do list as it stands: 185 of them are the dialect's own (sys beyond its surface, memoryview, the asynchronous types, frames, code objects and their proxies), and each of the others stops by name when a program reads it.
* **The callables' and the singletons' own slots.** NoneType has `_Py_BaseObject_RichCompare` as its own `tp_richcompare`, so its six comparisons are its own wrappers; a bound method, a built-in function and a method-wrapper have equality and a hash (the same function of the same object); every callable kind has `__call__` and `__repr__`. The runtime did each of these as an operation and had none of them as a name (`f.__call__`, `m.__eq__`, `None.__eq__` stopped). Each is now a slot wrapper over the operation. No class inherits from these types, so the wrapper cannot meet an override, and `hash()` and `repr()` reach each kind before they look for a method, so the wrapper cannot call itself (the trap of RL-430).
* **Docstrings.** A slot wrapper's `__doc__` is the slot's own text, one per slot name whatever the type (`Return self+value.`), but for a weak proxy's `__iadd__` and `__imul__`, which are number slots where list's are sequence slots. The names table records it by name with those four exceptions (83 rows). A built-in type's method had no `__doc__` either (`list.append.__doc__` stopped, and with it `functools.wraps(str.upper)`): 915 rows, 70 KB of text, by type and name.
* **The containers' `__repr__`.** list's, tuple's, dict's, set's and frozenset's are functions of the prelude, and were functions to a program too (`type(list.__repr__)` was `function`, with no `__objclass__`). They are presented as the slot wrappers they stand for, as a prelude class's methods already were.
* **Four names.** `builtins.__debug__` (True: assertions are on, D-09); `_locale._DATE_FMT`; `zlib.__getattr__`, the module's own for the deprecated `__version__` (its DeprecationWarning, then `"1.0"`); `_ast._is_abstract`.
* **Seen and left.** A static method of a built-in type is the builtin itself in the type's dict, where CPython's dict holds a `staticmethod` (`vars(str)["maketrans"]`). Instantiating an abstract AST node class still stops where CPython warns (the stop is older than `warnings`). `UnicodeEncodeError.__init__` and its like cannot be shown as the slot wrappers they are, and stop by name.
* **Cases and tests.** C-3-69 holds what a program sees of all this, on every leg. The test module compares with CPython on one host: each kind's own slots called through the type and through the object with every arity, a wrong self and a keyword (132 rows); the kind and the docstring of every slot wrapper and method of 54 types that the runtime has (over 900 rows, and every row it lacks is on the list); the containers' `__repr__`; the four names with their errors.

**2026-11-13 — checks per step, and the full check nightly (the author's decision).** The full set of checks takes about 80 minutes on the development host (the compiled corpus 42, pytest 24) and two hours in CI. It ran once per increment, three times this day, and CI ran it again on every push: some four hours of waiting. The rule that said "focused tests per step, the full check for cross-cutting changes" had become the full check every time, since nearly every step touches a header, the runtime or the frontend.
* **Per step:** the new or changed case on the legs it should pass; the test modules of the area touched; lint (a cold mypy on a new case) and `make verify`; the missing-names check when a name is added; and the six compiled targets when the runtime changed (the author: they stay in, two minutes, the gates' evidence). A new case joins a baseline on the strength of its own run.
* **Nightly:** the Docker job of `ci.yml`, on a schedule (02:00 UTC, main's head), on demand and for a pull request. A push runs the smoke job alone. A green night moves the tag `nightly-green` to the commit it checked, so a known-good state stays visible.
* **A red night** is fixed first, by focused runs over that day's commits. Until then main can be red: a regression outside the area a step tested is found up to a day late. That is the price, taken knowingly.
* **A gate claim** still gets a full run before it is made.

**2026-11-13 — the generic attribute slots a C type names itself (C-3-69 extended).** Thirty-five more names off the list of missing names (379 now). A type written in C that sets `tp_getattro` itself has `__getattribute__` in its own dict even when the function is `PyObject_GenericGetAttr`, the one object has: collections' deque and defaultdict and their iterators, every itertools type, `functools.partial`, operator's three getters, `struct.Struct`, `unicodedata.UCD`, the tokenizer's iterator, and `ast.AST` (which names `tp_setattro` too, so `__setattr__` and `__delattr__`). The names table records which types these are (`generic_getattro`, `generic_setattro`: the slot's pointer compared with the generic function's, read from the type object), and a prelude's class of such a type gets its own wrapper over object's. The types whose lookup is their own (module, super, type, method, the weak proxies, `types.GenericAlias`, `typing.Union`, the lazy import) stay on the list: each needs its own function.
* **A module's own `__getattr__`** (zlib has one since the entry above) is now asked after the names table: a name CPython's module has never reaches it in CPython, so a name the runtime lacks stops by name there too and does not get the hook's AttributeError.
* **Found while doing it:** an edit to `tools/cpython_names.py` misplaced a block and the regenerated table silently lost the methods' docstrings; `make verify` compares the table with what the tool writes, so it agreed. C-3-69's docstring rows caught it. The first step under the new rule (the entry above) was checked by the case on its legs, 64 tests of the area, both evaluator corpora, the six compiled targets, lint, verify and the missing-names list.

**2026-11-13 — a closure's cell (C-3-70).** `repr()` of a cell printed object's text, `hash()` of one answered, and two cells compared stopped by name. A cell is now CPython's: `cell_richcompare` (by what the two hold, through the whole comparison protocol, so two cells of unordered values raise that TypeError; an empty cell before anything else), no hash (`__hash__` is None: a type with a comparison of its own and no `tp_hash`), and `cell_repr` with the held object's type name cut at 80 characters. Eight names off the list of missing names (371). The case passes compiled only: both evaluators keep their closures in cells of their own, which do not compare as CPython's (eval1 overflows its stack, eval2 raises another TypeError), so it is in neither evaluator's baseline. The rows were first added to C-3-69 and broke it under both evaluators; a timing-dependent case (C-7-76) passed in the same run and hid the regression in the count, so the corpus tools' output is now read for its REGRESSED lines, not for its totals.

**2026-11-13 — two stale stop rows, found by the smoke job.** The first pushes under the per-step rule failed the smoke job's pytest: `test_re.py` and `test_review_2026_11_03.py` each held a row that expected a method's or a slot wrapper's `__doc__` to stop by name, which the docstrings of the entry above ended. Nothing was wrong in the runtime; the rows went, and the method's docstring is compared with CPython's instead. The per-step set gains one item for a step that makes something work that stopped: the tests that hold stops by name, selected by their names (74 tests, under four minutes), as `CLAUDE.md` now says.

**2026-11-13 — what a built-in iterator answers about itself (C-3-71).** `operator.length_hint(iter([1]))` and `copy.copy` of any built-in iterator stopped by name: the iterators had `__iter__` and `__next__` and nothing else. Each now has the methods its C type has, written from that type's own functions: `__length_hint__`, `__reduce__` and `__setstate__` for the iterators of a list (both directions), a tuple, a range (the C-long one and the long one), a str (two types, below), a bytes, a bytearray and the old protocol's; the first two for the six of a dict and a set's (CPython gives those no `__setstate__`: `__reduce__` runs a copy of the iterator into a list and leaves the iterator where it stood); and in the prelude `reversed` (all three), `enumerate` and `filter` (`__reduce__`), `zip` and `map` (`__reduce__` and `__setstate__`, which is the strict flag) and `iter(callable, sentinel)` (`__reduce__`). Fifty-five names off the list of missing names (316 now).

* **How an iterator stands.** The methods show state that nothing showed before, so three things follow CPython's objects more closely. A list's or a bytearray's iterator below index 0 gives nothing and keeps its sequence: that is where `__setstate__(-1)` puts one and where a reverse list iterator is after its last item, and a list's can be moved back from there (a bytearray's cannot: its `__setstate__` leaves one below 0 alone). A range's iterator keeps where it stands after its end, so its `__reduce__` is `range(12, 12, 3)` and not nothing. A set's iterator counts what is still to come, as a dict's did.
* **Differences that were silent, found while writing the case, all fixed:**
  * the iterator of a str beyond ASCII, or of a str subclass's instance, is `str_iterator`; the runtime had one type for every str and called it `str_ascii_iterator`;
  * an exhausted `reversed(a_list)` holds the list (it stands at -1 and never lets go); the runtime let go, which a weak reference to a list subclass's instance showed;
  * a reverse dict iterator stands at an entry of the table. When a resize packs the table shorter under it, CPython ends it there (`i >= dk_nentries`), and when the rebuilt table puts an entry back under one that expects no more it raises `dictionary keys changed during iteration`; the runtime clipped the position and went on, so it gave keys CPython does not;
  * a built-in type without a docstring has `__doc__` None in its own dict (`type_dict_set_doc`), so `iter([]).__doc__` is None; the runtime's types had no entry and their instances showed object's docstring. Every C++ type now gets the entry the names table says (the iterators, the dict views, the generator);
  * `reversed.__length_hint__` asked the sequence's `__len__` after the last item, where CPython asks only while an index stands;
  * the old protocol's iterator at `sys.maxsize` (reachable through `__setstate__` only) raises OverflowError `iter index too large`.
* **The missing-names program** samples more kinds of iterator than it did (a str beyond ASCII, a long range, the old protocol, `iter(callable, sentinel)`, a dict's reversed views), so their types are now measured too; none of them lacks a name.
* **Cases and tests.** C-3-71 on every leg: each type's three answers fresh, after one item, copied (shallow and deep) and exhausted; `__setstate__` over thirteen states at four moments; the dict and set rules above; the weak-reference row; the wrappers of iterators. `test_iteration_builtins.py` compares the messages with CPython for 25 kinds of iterator (650 lines: each method through the object and through the type with every arity, a wrong receiver, a keyword, and each kind of state). Checked per step: the case on its three legs, 180 tests of the area and 74 that hold a stop by name, both evaluator corpora, the six compiled targets, lint, verify and the list of missing names.

**2026-11-13 — how a range, a slice, two singletons and the callables say they are made again (C-3-72).** Ten more names off the list (306 now), all `__reduce__`: `copy.copy` of a bound method stopped by name at `method.__reduce__`, and so did the method asked for by hand on any of these. Each is CPython's own function: a range and a slice give their type and their three fields; `Ellipsis` and `NotImplemented` their names; a built-in function of a module its name, and a built-in method `getattr` of what it is bound to and its name (`meth_reduce`); a bound method `getattr` of its object and its function's `__name__`, a private name mangled with the class's name as a class body would (`method___reduce___impl`, `_Py_Mangle`: not a name with a dot, not for a class of underscores only, the class's leading underscores dropped); a method descriptor, a slot wrapper and a member descriptor `getattr` of their type and name (`descr_reduce`); a method-wrapper `getattr` of its object and name. A getset descriptor, a class-method descriptor, a function and None have none of their own in CPython either and are refused by object's.

* **A static method's two answers.** `str.maketrans.__reduce__()` is `(getattr, (str, 'maketrans'))` and `str.maketrans.__self__` is None: `type_add_method` binds a static method to its type, `__reduce__` reads that field, and `__self__` reads it through `PyCFunction_GET_SELF`, which gives NULL for `METH_STATIC`. The runtime has both now; the case's first run compiled caught the second.
* **Seen and left (TODO).** An inherited built-in method called with the wrong number of arguments names the receiver's type when the receiver's type has its own `tp_getattro` (`k.meth.__reduce_ex__()`: `method.__reduce_ex__() takes exactly one argument`) or when the call goes through the bound object, and names `object` otherwise; the runtime says `object` every time. It is the message of an error that is raised either way, and it is older than this step; exact needs a bound built-in's call to know what it is bound to when it words the error.
* **Cases and tests.** C-3-72 on every leg. `test_missing_names.py` compares the messages with CPython for 48 values (each through the object and the type, with an argument, a keyword, no receiver, `__reduce_ex__` at two protocols), the descriptors themselves with wrong receivers, the types that have none, and `copy.copy` and `copy.deepcopy` of each kind (379 lines). Checked per step: the case on its three legs, the area's test modules and the 74 tests that hold a stop by name, both evaluator corpora, the six compiled targets, lint, verify and the list of missing names.

**2026-11-13 — a mappingproxy's operators (C-3-73), and what was found beside them.** `types.MappingProxyType(d) | {...}` raised `TypeError: unsupported operand type(s)` where CPython gives the merged dict: a wrong answer, not a stop by name, because the operator table (`tools/cpython_names.py`, `binop:` rows) is measured over a fixed set of types and mappingproxy is not one of them. The proxy now has CPython's number slot on either side (`mappingproxy_or`: each proxy stands for its mapping and the two are or-ed as any two objects are, so the answer is the mapping's own, a reflected method is handed the mapping itself, and the TypeError names the mapping's type), `|=` refused in its own words, `reversed()` as the mapping's, and the four orderings as names that decline. Nine names off the list (297 now).

* **`==` with an object of another kind is given a copy.** `mappingproxy_richcompare` in 3.15.2 (gh-152405) hands an exact dict to a dict, a frozendict or another proxy only; anything else is compared with a copy, so a foreign `__eq__` cannot change the dict behind the proxy, a class's least of all. The runtime handed out the live dict. (CPython also spares an exact OrderedDict the copy; here it gets one, which compares the same and cannot be told apart.)
* **A proxy's hash was cut to 63 bits.** `hash(MappingProxyType(frozendict(a=1)))` differed from the frozendict's hash for most hash seeds: the slot returned the 64-bit hash through a small int. It was the only place in the runtime that returns a hash that way (every `Obj::small(` was read). The case's first compiled run agreed by chance of the seed; the harness leg, which fixes the seed, did not. A test runs twelve seeds.
* **`OrderedDict | frozendict`** raised (the prelude read the other operand with `dict.items`); it merges now, as `PyDict_Items` does for either.
* **A Unicode error's members.** `UnicodeTranslateError.encoding` (the three errors share one C struct; a translate error's is never set and reads None) came off the list, and its test showed the rest: `encoding`, `object` and `reason` are objects of any type and can be deleted (they read None; `str()` shows `<NULL>`), `start` and `end` are `Py_ssize_t` (an int that fits, kept as that number, `can't delete numeric/char attribute`), and `__str__` asks the reason for its `str()` before the encoding and then checks the object (`UnicodeError 'object' attribute must be a string`, or `is not set`). The runtime refused every deletion, took any value for the two numbers, asked the encoding first and checked nothing.
* **Not done:** the operator table still measures a fixed set of types. A type outside it that has an operator CPython handles and the runtime does not answers with a TypeError and not a stop; the list of missing names catches it only where the operator is also a name in the type's dict (as it did here). Widening the table to every type the missing-names program reaches is TODO.
* **Cases and tests.** C-3-73 on every leg, C-7-29 with the three errors' members. `test_missing_names.py`: five kinds of proxy against 25 kinds of operand on either side, in place and by name, with every arity (273 lines of CPython's words); the hash under twelve seeds; the Unicode errors' members deleted, set to six kinds of value and printed. Checked per step: the two cases on their three legs, 19 test modules of the area and the 74 that hold a stop by name, both evaluator corpora, the six compiled targets, lint, verify and the list of missing names.

**2026-11-13 — comparisons that decline, and `~` on a bool (C-3-74).** Twenty-six more names off the list (271 now). A C type with a `tp_richcompare` has all six comparison names in its dict, whatever the function answers; `types.SimpleNamespace`, `types.GenericAlias`, `weakref.ref`, `_contextvars.Context` and a ParamSpec's `args` and `kwargs` compare for `==` and `!=` only, so their four orderings are names that answer NotImplemented, and a namespace's `!=` is its dict's (`namespace_richcompare`). Asked for by name they stopped; the operators already said the two cannot be ordered, and still do.

* **`~` on a bool** stopped by name ("a DeprecationWarning in CPython 3.15"). It now warns as `bool_invert` does (`PyErr_WarnEx`, so the warning points at the line of the `~`, is shown once for a place by default, and is an error under a filter that makes it one) and gives the int's answer; `bool.__invert__` is bool's own slot wrapper, and int's `__invert__` given a bool does not warn. The warning goes through the `_warnings` prelude like the import system's (`warn_ex`, now callable from the rest of the runtime).
* **The limit that is older than this:** showing a warning imports `warnings`, so a program that inverts a bool and imports `warnings` nowhere stops by name at that import, where before it stopped at the `~`. Whether `warnings` is linked into every program is the author's open question (TODO).
* **Left (TODO):** the runtime's other stops at a DeprecationWarning can now warn the same way.
* **Cases and tests.** C-3-74 on every leg. `test_missing_names.py`: each of the six types' six names with every arity and a wrong receiver, the operators beside them, and the full warning for five ways of inverting a bool with its line and its text on stderr (57 lines). Checked per step: the case on its three legs, ten test modules of the area and the 74 that hold a stop by name, both evaluator corpora, the six compiled targets, lint, verify and the list of missing names.

**2026-11-14 — the collector finds its objects by trial deletion over candidate roots (the author's decision).** The first of the three questions open since 2026-11-12, put again with a sample: CPython's lists (a 16-byte link in each container and the three generations, which is what `refmodel/collector.py` has; recommended) or the design draft's trial deletion over candidate roots (no link; an object whose count drops without reaching zero becomes a candidate). **Decided: trial deletion.**

* **What stays CPython's:** what happens once the unreachable set is known, the 3.15 sequence the model already has (weak references with callbacks, every finaliser once, the reckoning again for what a finaliser revived, the remaining weak references, then each object's clear).
* **What follows from the choice:** a group's finalisers run in the collector's order, not in the order of CPython's list (section 9 and D-04 leave a group's order open); there are no generations, so what `gc.collect(generation)`, `get_count()` and `get_stats()` say and when an automatic collection runs are put to the author again for this mechanism (the two questions still open); a structure whose count drops often is a candidate at every collection.
* **The model first:** `refmodel/collector.py` is gc.c rule for rule and is to be rewritten as trial deletion before the header gets it; the oracle goes on comparing a collection's deaths with CPython's as a multiset, and what the model's tests compare of generations goes with them.

**2026-11-14 — gc's numbers are the runtime's own (the author's decision).** The second question, put again for trial deletion. Measured on 3.15.2: `gc.collect()` after a self-referencing instance gives 1, after a closure cycle 5 (the list, the function, its cell, the closure tuple and the function's annotate function), after an empty local class 4; and of an instance that survived a full collection and was then let go, `gc.collect(0)`, `(1)` and `(2)` give 0, 0 and 1. The runtime has no closure tuple, builds a class differently and, with trial deletion, has no generations. **Decided:** `gc.collect()` returns the number of the runtime's objects it freed; the generation argument is checked as CPython checks it and otherwise ignored, every collection being a full one; `get_count()` and `get_stats()` give the runtime's own counters in CPython's shapes. All of it joins D-04 as an observable, with a lint rule for a `gc.collect()` result, `get_count()` or `get_stats()` that reaches output.

**2026-11-14 — when a collection runs (the author's decisions).** The third question, in two parts for trial deletion.

* **The trigger is the candidate buffer's size** (of three: CPython's first threshold alone; the threshold with CPython's quarter rule for full collections, recommended; the buffer's size). The moments of automatic collections are then the runtime's own, which D-04 already says of cycles. An exact build runs a triggered collection at the next look, which are CPython's points.
* **A lean build collects only at `gc.collect()`, at the runtime's own looks and at exit** (of four: at the allocation that crosses the threshold; one check kept in lean generated code; this; at a frame's entry with a backstop, recommended). A lean program that makes cycles and never waits keeps them until it asks or ends; D-24 lists it when the collector is in the header.
* **Taken here, for the author to overrule:** `gc.get_threshold()` stays `(2000, 10, 10)`; the first number is the buffer size that triggers a collection, the other two are kept and returned and decide nothing; `set_threshold(0)` and `gc.disable()` turn automatic collection off, as in CPython. And the size that triggers grows with the heap: the candidates must exceed the first threshold and a quarter of the containers alive after the last collection. The reason, measured: in a loop that keeps 2,000,000 new lists, each new list is a candidate (its count drops from 2 to 1 once it is appended) and so is the list that keeps them, so a fixed size of 2000 would walk that list a thousand times, the quadratic case gc.c's comment on issue 4074 describes; CPython runs 913, 83 and 6 collections of its three generations there, in 0.66 s.

**2026-11-14 — a minimal memoryview is \[P1\] (the author's decision).** The specification makes memoryview \[P2+\] (the Builtins row and the `bytes`, `bytearray` row), and the phase-1 closure makes one at 14 places in 9 modules (`base64` 3, `shutil` 2, `urllib.parse` 2, `importlib._bootstrap_external` 2, `pathlib`, `hashlib`, `socket`, `subprocess`, `re._compiler`): compiled, `base64.encodebytes(b"hello")` and `pathlib.Path.write_bytes` stop by name, and so do two \[P1\] cases (C-7-10 at `write_bytes`; C-7-01 makes one itself). Of three (keep it \[P2+\]; keep it and replace the library's function bodies, about eight; a minimal memoryview in \[P1\], recommended) **decided: the minimal memoryview.** One-dimensional views of octets over bytes and bytearray: construction with CPython's TypeError, `len`, indexing, slicing, `tobytes`, `release` and the context manager, `format`, `ndim`, `itemsize`, `readonly`, a view wherever bytes-like data is taken, and the export count that makes resizing an exported bytearray a BufferError, which is a lifetime and so goes into the reference model first. Other formats, `cast` and the buffer protocol on a program's own classes stay \[P2+\]. The specification's two rows change with the increment, under a version entry.

**2026-11-14 — `locals()`, `vars()` and `dir()` answer for every function (the author's decision).** \[P1\] by the specification (section 3, PEP 667); C-8-03 stops at the name. Of three (every function answers; only a function that names `locals`, `vars` or `dir`; only a call written as one of the three) **decided: every function**, so the builtin under another name and the builtin handed to another function answer as CPython's do (`f = locals; f()`, and `helper(locals)` giving the helper's own). The recommendation of 2026-11-12 was the second, for the cost of the first in generated code; that cost was wrong: every compiled frame already hands the runtime the addresses of its locals (`FrameGuard`, for the locals a traceback's entry keeps) and its code record has their names in CPython's order, so the dict is made from what is there, for one pointer stored at a frame's entry. **Noted for phase 2:** unboxed locals must then stay readable at every call; to weigh there, with measurements. `locals()` inside an inlined comprehension, whose variables are the frame's around it, stays a stop by name at first (RL-409).

**2026-11-14 — a warning in a program without `warnings` is printed as CPython's C prints it (the author's decision).** CPython loads neither `warnings` nor `copyreg` at start: it imports the first when a warning is shown (`_warnings.c`, `get_warnings_attr` with `try_import`) and the second at the first `__reduce_ex__` (`_PyType_GetSlotNames`). A compiled program holds the modules its imports reach, so `flag = True; print(~flag)`, a forgotten `breakpoint()` (D-11) and `P().__reduce_ex__(2)` stopped by name at those imports. Measured: only a program that imports no library module written in Python lacks the two (13 modules compiled in, a 34 MB binary: nothing, or `math`, `sys`, `time`, `itertools`); any of `os`, `re`, `abc`, `enum`, `collections`, `functools`, `typing` and the like brings a core of 79 with both (72 MB). Of three (link the core into every program; leave the stops; CPython's own fallback for `warnings`, recommended) **decided: the fallback.** `get_warnings_attr` says "Fallback to the C implementation if we cannot get the Python implementation": when the import fails, `show_warning` prints `file:line: Category: text` and the source line itself, and under CPython with the import made to fail the sample prints the same lines as with it. A compiled program without `warnings` linked takes that path. What still differs is a deviation to register with the increment: `'warnings' in sys.modules` stays False afterwards, and the source line keeps trailing whitespace the Python implementation strips. `copyreg` has no fallback in CPython (a failed import there is an error CPython would not raise), so it stays a stop by name, reached only by `__reduce_ex__` or `__reduce__` called by hand in a program that imports neither `copy` nor `pickle`.

**2026-11-14 — the sign of a NaN made of two NaNs is unspecified (the author's decision; spec v0.18).** Measured again: of `p = nan` and `n = -nan`, CPython's float `p+n`, `n+p`, `p*n`, `n*p` give `+ - + -` and a compiled program's `- + - +`; `-` and `/` agree; complex `+` differs the other way round, `-` and `*` agree. On x86 the result is the first operand's NaN and the compiler chooses which operand that is (GCC commutes `+` and `*` in `float_binary`; Clang vectorised the pin's `_Py_c_sum`). Of three (match the pin's machine code, with a fix-up on every float `+` and `*`; leave it unspecified, recommended; the first for floats and the second for complex) **decided: unspecified**, as IEEE 754 leaves it and as section 6 already leaves the last bit of the C library's functions. The recommendation of 2026-11-12 was the first, weighing only today's boxed arithmetic; against it: half of CPython's answer is its compiler's vectoriser, a program sees the bit only through `math.copysign` or `struct`, and the fix-up would stay on float arithmetic into phase 2's unboxed loops. The runtime does not change; cases do not print these signs; the cmath port spells out the order its C uses and is exact either way.

**2026-11-14 — D-25: four of its places accepted, the C's long loops to be closed (the author's decision).** D-25 lists where an exact build runs a signal's Python handler at another point than CPython; (a) was approved on 2026-11-11 and (b) to (f) waited. **Decided, as recommended:** (b) the backward jumps of CPython's block layout that are not breaks (the end of a match case or of a with statement that ends an except clause), (c) `all`, `any`, `tuple`, `list` and `set` over one generator expression (a `<genexpr>` entry in a raising handler's traceback that CPython does not have; a lint rule for the shape in a program that installs a handler), (d) a first import (the handler runs at the imported module's entry, not in `_find_and_load`) and (f) a handler owed while an uncaught exception is reported are **accepted**; each needs a handler and a signal arriving at exactly that point, and each can be closed later if a program needs it. (e), the looks CPython's C takes in its own long loops, is **to be closed**: measured, `re.match(r"(a+)+$", "a" * 27 + "b")` under `signal.alarm(1)` with a handler that raises is interrupted after 1 s in CPython and only when the match is over, after 7 s, compiled, and Ctrl-C cannot stop such a match either. The runtime takes a `check_signals()` where the C has one (`_sre` every 4096 steps; big-int multiplication, division and conversion to decimal; the start of `str()`, `repr()` and print), a conformance case first. The register's entry is accepted with (e) listed until the last check is in.

**2026-11-14 — the "Enable tracemalloc" line is left out and listed under D-20 (the author's decision).** CPython's warning hook (`Lib/_py_warnings.py`, `_formatwarnmsg_impl`) adds `ResourceWarning: Enable tracemalloc to get the object allocation traceback` beneath a shown warning that has a source, when `tracemalloc` can be imported and is not tracing; `tracemalloc` is excluded (E-03) and omitted from `_py_warnings`' imports, so a compiled program takes the path the hook has for an import that fails and prints the warning's first two lines only (measured again on an unclosed file). Of three (accept and list; a stand-in `tracemalloc` that never traces, reachable from `_py_warnings` only, recommended; CPython's `tracemalloc` over a native part that never traces) **decided: accept and list.** D-20 names the line (the register and section 11); the tests keep comparing standard error without it and checking that it is absent; the fallback of the same day for a program without `warnings` prints as CPython's C does, which has no such line either.

**2026-11-14 — traceback can read the frame a signal handler is given; the program still cannot (the author's decision).** A handler's frame is an object of the type `frame` with nothing in it (E-02, v0.17), so `traceback.print_stack(frame)`, the usual way to show where a program was when a signal arrived, stops at `frame.f_lineno`; measured, CPython lists the interrupted stack (`<module>`, `work`) and `print_stack()` without the frame already works compiled, with the handler's own entry last, as in CPython. Of three (leave it; traceback reads the frame and the program does not, recommended; open `f_lineno`, `f_code` and `f_back` to the program, narrowing E-02) **decided: the second.** The frame a handler is given carries the record of the frame it interrupted, as a traceback entry's frame carries its code since 2026-11-13: `print_stack`, `format_stack` and `extract_stack` given it list that stack as CPython does (file, line and function; no source text, D-06), and `repr()` of it is CPython's text. Its attributes stop by name as before, so E-02 stands as written; a lean build gives `None` as before (D-24). The frame is read while its handlers run; one kept and walked afterwards stops by name, since the lines it would give are no longer those of its frames. The spec's Development row names it with the increment, under a version entry; a conformance case first, on the compiled leg.

**2026-11-14 — `breakpoint()`'s warning keeps CPython's words (the author's decision; D-11).** D-11 (decision 9) says a compiled `breakpoint()` warns and goes on, and left the words open; the increment of 2026-11-13 took the ones CPython's `sys_breakpointhook` has for a hook it cannot import (`Python/sysmodule.c`: `"Ignoring unimportable $PYTHONBREAKPOINT: \"%s\""`, the name `pdb.set_trace` when the variable is not set), a RuntimeWarning at the caller's line. Of two (those words; a text of Bella's own that says what happened, a one-line change) **decided: CPython's words**, as recommended: the output is what a CPython without `pdb` gives for the same program, a filter written against CPython's message matches, and the warning differs from CPython in one way (no debugger) and not in two. The text names an environment variable the user never set; the plain explanation is the lint rule's, which D-11 has. The register's entry says so.

**2026-11-14 — the type a wrong-arity message names is left as it is (the author's decision; D-08).** Seen with C-3-72: an inherited built-in method called with the wrong number of arguments names the type that defines it when CPython calls the unbound descriptor (a direct call on an object whose type has the generic attribute lookup: `range(3).__reduce_ex__()` says `object.__reduce_ex__()`), and the receiver's type when a bound object is made first (`b = range(3).__reduce_ex__; b()` says `range.`; a receiver whose type has its own `tp_getattro`, a bound method or a program's class with `__getattr__` or `__getattribute__`, says `method.` or the class's name). The runtime names the defining type every time; measured again on four routes. It is the wording of a TypeError that is raised either way, which D-08 (decision 11) leaves free, so nothing was unregistered. Of two (leave it; give the arity check the route the method was reached by, with a sweep over receivers and routes) **decided: leave it**, as recommended. If D-08 is tightened to identical messages, the sweep that follows takes this with the rest.

**2026-11-14 — the annotationlib shim's `value_only` stays (the author's review).** Since C-3-67 the shim's work for an annotate function a program wrote itself is two marked lines on CPython's own (`_bella.annotate_with(annotate, ..., value_only=True)` in FORWARDREF's rebuild) and a native stand-in in the runtime (`i_value_only_call`: the function called with its own globals, its NotImplementedError raised as it is, so that CPython's own `except` line answers VALUE; anything else stops by name, \[P2+\] by S-1), in place of thirteen lines of the shim's own that moved CPython's lines. Put to the author with a sample of both kinds of function (the one that supports VALUE only answers as in CPython; the one that also supports the re-run with substitute globals stops by name as decided). Of two (keep it; move the stand-in into Python at the shim's end, which would put a frame of its own into a compiled traceback) **decided: keep it.** The staging replay's stand-in stays a Python function with one more frame, so those frames are compared on the compiled build only.

**2026-11-14 — the sentence on the running stack leaves the specification (the author's decision).** With C-7-84 (2026-11-13) the Development row of section 7 gained half a sentence, "the running stack of `extract_stack`, `format_stack` and `print_stack` is the frame chain the runtime keeps, replacing `sys._getframe`", with no version entry. Put to the author: keep it and enter it in the history (recommended), take it out, or keep it without an entry. **Decided: out.** It says how `traceback` is served and not what a program sees; that belongs to the design document, whose section 10.4 now has it with the other replaced functions. The specification is back at v0.17's words there, so the history needs no entry. The rule holds for what follows: the increment for the frame a signal handler is given states in the specification only what a program sees and puts its mechanism in the design document, and a change to the specification's text is the author's, asked before it is made.

**2026-11-14 — the nightly's tag is moved by a job of its own (the author's decision).** The full Docker job had `contents: write` so that its last step could move `nightly-green`; the permission covered every step and every event the job runs for (the nightly, a run on demand, a pull request), where one step of the nightly needs it. The exposure was small (the code under test runs in the image, whose build leaves `.git` out; a fork's pull request has a read-only token whatever the file says), so this is least privilege and not a hole found. Of three (leave it; a job of its own for the tag, recommended; no tag, the last green night read from the run list) **decided: a job of its own.** `pinned` has `contents: read`; `mark-green` needs it, runs for the schedule only, has `contents: write` and does nothing but move the tag. The workflow's default is `contents: read`, so the smoke job has no more either.

**2026-11-14 — a pull request runs the smoke job only (the author's decision).** When the full check moved to the night (2026-11-13) it stayed on for pull requests: every push to one started the two-hour Docker job, and nothing cancelled a superseded run. No pull request has been opened on the repository yet. Of three (leave it; the smoke job only; the full check with superseded runs cancelled, recommended) **decided: the smoke job only.** The full check runs on the schedule and on demand; for a pull request it is started by hand on its branch before the merge when that is wanted, and the night after the merge checks main in any case, so a regression a pull request brings is found there by the next morning and fixed first, as any red night is.

**2026-11-14 — signals inside the runtime's own work (C-7-85; D-25 (e) as the author decided).** A signal's handler now runs where CPython's C calls `PyErr_CheckSignals` in work of its own, and what it raises comes out of that work.
* **The regular-expression engine.** `sre_lib.inc` is CPython's file and already had `MAYBE_CHECK_SIGNALS` every 4096 steps, with `PyErr_CheckSignals()` defined as 0. It is the runtime's check now. The engine is C that answers an error code and frees what it holds on the way out, so what a handler raises is kept until the engine has returned `SRE_ERROR_INTERRUPTED` and is raised then, as `pattern_error` lets it fly. A match that ran 7 s compiled is left after the first timer arrival.
* **`PyObject_Str` and `PyObject_Repr`** begin with the check (`str_of`, `repr_of`): a handler that is owed runs before the object's `__str__` or `__repr__`, in the frame that asked. That covers `str()`, `repr()`, `ascii()`, print (each argument, the separator and the end), `%s`, `%r`, `%a`, the conversions of an f-string, `format()` and `str.format` through object's `__format__`, the containers' repr and an exception's text; an object's `__str__` called by hand, `object.__str__`, a `__format__` of its own, `len` and `int` do not look, as in CPython.
* **What "owed" is.** Measured on 3.15.2: when two signals arrive together and the first handler raises from a check the C made itself (`pthread_sigmask`), the second stays noted in `is_tripped` alone; the evaluation loop's note was taken down when the first handler's frame began. No loop turn, function entry or end of a built-in call finds it, only the next check the C makes itself. The runtime had the two notes already; the case's second half is built on this and needs no clock.
* **Ints.** An int's decimal text looks once for any int but zero (`long_to_decimal_string_internal` has `SIGCHECK` in its loop over the digits). bn's loops look where `x_mul` and `x_divrem` do: every row of a basecase product or square and every word of a long division's quotient (a hook in `bn.inc`, `BN_LOOK`, named in its header as the first change to SEL's copy). `int()` of a decimal text of up to 6000 digits does not look (CPython's C converts it with no check and hands longer ones to `_pylong`), so the hook is held there.
* **What was tried and taken out.** A look at the int layer's entry for every product that CPython's digits would loop over (an operand of 2**30 or more) made `2**63` look, which CPython's compiler folds and which five preludes compute for a bounds test: three signal tests failed, a handler running inside `_thread.interrupt_main` and before `os.read` had read. A rule that left the checks out under a prelude's frame fixed those and broke `map(str, ...)`: a prelude calling a function the program passed in must check as the C does. Kept: no look at the int layer's entry, and none hidden under a prelude.
* **What is left, in the register under (e) and put to the author:** ints between 2**30 and 2**64 (measured: `mid * 3`, `mid // low`, `mid % low` and `mid ** 1` with `mid = 2**40 + 1` look in CPython and not compiled; `divmod`, a product that leaves a word and everything on longer ints agree); a quotient of two long integer literals, computed at run time; `_pylong`'s frames in a handler's stack listing; a prelude's `str()` of a number of its own in a message.
* **The report of an exception** (`print_traceback`) holds the checks while it writes: it stands for traceback's Python, and D-25 (f) says a handler owed then runs after the report.
