* **Cases and tests.** C-7-85, compiled leg (an evaluator's frames are its own, as for C-7-76 and C-7-84): 24 pieces of work cut short by a timer, 83 rows of what looks and what does not with a handler owed, six with a handler that raises. A timer that came before the work began ends it at the work's first look with the same line, so nothing depends on when it came. Checked per step: the case on the compiled leg and under both evaluators (it fails there), the signal, fidelity, regular-expression, big-int, format, traceback and prelude test modules, the six compiled targets, lint and verify.

**2026-11-15 — what is left of D-25 (e) is accepted (the author's decision).** The four differences C-7-85 left under (e) stay as deviations: an operand between 2**30 and 2**64 in a product, quotient, remainder or power does not look (CPython's 30-bit digits do; a compiled int is one machine word there); a quotient or remainder of two long integer literals is computed when the program runs, and looks (CPython's compiler folds it); `_pylong`'s frames are not in a handler's stack listing; a built-in the runtime writes in Python asks for the text of a number of its own as a program would, and looks.
* **Why:** each needs a handler that is already owed (two signals arrived together and the first handler raised) and one narrow shape besides. Closing the first would put a test of the operands' size on every `*`, `//`, `%` and `**` of ints, and that test would stay in phase 2's unboxed loops; the second needs CPython's constant folding in the frontend; the third the frames of a module the runtime does not have; the fourth a text helper without the check at some 227 places in 54 prelude files, nearly all on error paths.
* **What changes:** nothing in the code. D-25's note and the specification's status column say accepted; D-25 is `accepted` whole, with its revisit at phase 2 as before. The fourth was read from the code and not measured; the register says what it says on that strength.

**2026-11-15 — a warning in a program that holds no `warnings` module (C-7-86, D-27; the author's decision of 2026-11-14).** `flag = True; print(~flag)` stopped at `importing 'warnings'`; it now prints CPython's two lines and goes on.
* **The import.** `_warnings` asks for `warnings` as the C does (`get_warnings_attr`: `PyImport_Import`, and an ImportError means the C prints). For that one call a module the program does not hold is not found, as one that is not installed, where any other import of it stops by name: the program's own `__import__` of `warnings` still stops, after a warning as before one. The import runs whole, so a `warnings` the program put in `sys.modules` or gives through a finder of its own is used, as in CPython. The same for `io` when the source line is read: a program without the module reads through `_io`, where `io.open` and `io.TextIOWrapper` are.
* **The printing** is `show_warning` as it was (a write for each part; the first that fails ends it), and beneath it now `display_source_line` and `_Py_FindSourceFile`, ported call for call: `io.open(filename, "rb")`, else the last part of the name in each directory of `sys.path`; the descriptor; the encoding; a rewind; `io.TextIOWrapper(binary, encoding)`; `readline()` until the line; the line without the spaces, tabs and form feeds before it, after two spaces. This also ends the older stop for a program whose `warnings._showwarnmsg` is gone.
* **The encoding** is `_PyTokenizer_FindEncodingFilename`. A first version found the UTF-8 mark and the coding spec by hand; a sweep against CPython (the import blocked by the program, so that both print through the C) showed four shapes it got wrong, all of them files where CPython prints no line: the tokenizer had found an encoding and then failed (a spec against the mark, a NUL, a first token that is none), its error is still set when the C calls `io.TextIOWrapper`, and the call fails before the type makes its object. Knowing when the tokenizer fails needs the tokenizer, and the runtime has CPython's lexer as the C's text (C-7-53), so the file's side of it was written beside the readline's in `runtime/src/tokenizer.cpp`, each function after the C one it is named for (`check_bom`, `get_coding_spec`, `check_coding_spec`, `fp_setreadl`, `tok_underflow_file`, over a FILE on a copy of the descriptor). The prelude is told the encoding and whether an error was left, and does what the C then does: closes the file and prints no line. After that the sweep agrees on all 1538 rows.
* **D-27** lists what a program can see: `warnings` not in `sys.modules`; white space after the line and white space before it other than spaces, tabs and form feeds; a file found by its name's last part on `sys.path`; the text in parts; what a write raises dropped. The first two were named when the author decided; the others were found here. The specification has the row and v0.19.
* **The harness** can make CPython show D-27 (the import blocked at start-up, as D-20's modules are), so C-7-86 has two expectations and a compiled program must give the second one row for row. `pipeline/tests/test_warnings_without_the_module.py` holds the sweep, where the file is looked for, an `io` whose functions the program replaced, the writes and what goes wrong in them, and the stops that stay.
* **Left as stops by name:** `warn_explicit` given `module_globals` (older; the C would print the loader's line as it stands), a deleted `sys.stderr`, a bool as the file's descriptor, and an `io.TextIOWrapper` of the program's own when the tokenizer failed after it found an encoding (CPython runs it with the error set).
* **Checked per step:** the case compiled; the new module and those of warnings, the tokenizer, imports and the prelude (150 tests); the tests that hold a stop; lint, a cold mypy on the case, verify (410/410); the six compiled targets; both evaluator corpora (no regression; the case fails there, as the evaluators import `warnings`). `PYTHONWARNINGS` is not read by a compiled program with or without the module (D-18), so nothing is added there.

**2026-11-15 — `locals()`, `vars()` and `dir()` of the calling frame (C-8-15, C-8-03 compiled; the author's decision of 2026-11-14).** `locals` was a name the runtime lacked, and `vars()` and `dir()` without an argument stopped by name.
* **What they read** is `_PyEval_GetFrameLocals` of the frame that called: `detail::current_frame()`, the nearest frame CPython has (a function of the prelude stands for C and is not linked in), so `f = locals; f()`, `functools.partial(locals)()` and `operator.call(vars)` answer for their caller and `helper(locals)` for the helper. A module's body gives its namespace and a class's the mapping its body runs in (the one `__prepare__` gave, whatever it is); a function gives a dict of its own each time.
* **A function's dict** is made from what the frame already hands the runtime. Every compiled frame gives its guard the addresses of its variables (for the locals a traceback's entry keeps); the frame record now keeps the pointer to them (`FrameRecord::slots`), and a code record says, for each name CPython's code object has, in CPython's order (`co_varnames`, then the `co_cellvars` not among them, then `co_freevars`), where among them it is (`Code::frame_slots`; -1 for a name the compiled function has no variable for, which is never bound). The compiler's own variables have no name there and so are not in the dict. A variable shared with inner functions or taken from outside is its cell's value; what is not bound is left out. One pointer stored at a frame's entry, as the decision said; nothing else in a frame changes.
* **A class body's frame** records its namespace (`FrameRecord::ns`, one store after the guard); a module's is its module's (the locals mapping of an `exec`, else its dict).
* **`dir()`** is `_dir_locals`: the keys as a list (a dict's own, else the mapping's `keys()`), sorted by `list.sort`.
* **A generator expression's first variable** was known to the code record by the frontend's name for it (`_it73`); it is `.0`, as CPython's code object has it. Found by the case, fixed in the frontend (`bella_public`), so `gi_code.co_varnames` is right too.
* **Left as stops by name:** the three inside a list, set or dict comprehension. PEP 709 runs it in the frame around it, and CPython's dict then has the comprehension's own variables too, for as long as it runs; the frame counts the comprehensions running in it (RL-409), and the three stop there. `eval` and `exec` in a function with the frame's locals stop as before; they can be given this dict next.
* **Noted for phase 2,** as the decision did: an unboxed local must stay readable wherever one of the three can be called.
* **Cases and tests.** C-8-15: every kind of frame, the order of the names, what is and is not bound, the snapshot, the builtins under other names and in other hands, the namespace of a module and of a class body, `__prepare__`'s mapping. `pipeline/tests/test_frame_locals.py`: a function's variables of every kind (parameters, every binding statement, cells of several depths, `__class__`, a generator between its yields, generic functions, names a comprehension used), the namespaces (a dict subclass and a mapping that is no dict, whose `keys()` `dir()` asks), and the stops. The row of `test_review_2026_11_03.py` that held `locals` as a name the runtime lacks now holds `aiter`.

**2026-11-15 — the staging replay and D-27 (the smoke job's failure on `3c1ae85`).** `make staging-check` runs every P1 case on the replay, which is CPython with the whole library, and a case that declares a deviation must give its second expectation there. C-7-86's is CPython without `warnings`; the replay had it, and the job failed. I had not run the staging check for the step: it is not among the per-step checks, and takes 14 seconds, so it is run from now on with every step that adds a case or touches the harness.
* **Tried first:** the replay blocks `warnings` for every program whose imports do not reach it, as the build finds them, which is what a compiled program is. C-8-12 then failed: CPython itself imports `traceback`, and with it `warnings`, to report an uncaught exception, so with the module blocked its report of an exception group is the C's own, where a compiled program's report needs no module. The block cannot follow the closure in a CPython.
* **Done:** the replay blocks `warnings` at run time for a case that declares D-27 (`pipeline/staging/run.py`), as the harness does when it records the second expectation; the dry run is unchanged. The harness says that such a case must not end in an uncaught exception.

**2026-11-15 — the frame a signal handler is given, read by traceback (C-7-87; the author's decision of 2026-11-14).** The frame was an object of the type `frame` with nothing behind it, so `traceback.print_stack(frame)` in a handler stopped at `frame.f_lineno`.
* **What it is now:** a frame object of the runtime's own, as a traceback entry's frame is, that stands for the frame the signal interrupted (the nearest frame CPython has when the handlers are called) for as long as the handlers of that arrival run. One object for the arrival, as before, so two handlers get the same one; a handler that is itself interrupted is the frame of the inner arrival.
* **What reads it:** `traceback.extract_stack(f)`, and through it `format_stack(f)` and `print_stack(f)`, ask the runtime for the stack from that frame outwards (`__bella_stack__(f)`: a frame and the line it is at for each frame a traceback would show), where CPython's `walk_stack` reads `f_lineno` and `f_back`; with a limit as CPython's. `repr()` is `frame_repr`'s text: the address, the file, the line the frame is at and its function. No source line under an entry (D-06).
* **What does not:** the program. Every attribute stops by name as before (E-02), and so does `traceback.walk_stack(frame)`, which is CPython's code reading them. A lean build gives `None` (D-24).
* **Afterwards.** When the arrival's handlers are done the interrupted frames go on, and the lines the frame would give are no longer theirs: a frame kept and then listed or printed stops by name, where CPython lists the stack as it is by then.
* **The specification** names it in the Development row and in E-02's note of v0.17, under v0.19; the mechanism is in the design document.
* **Cases and tests.** C-7-87, compiled leg (an evaluator's frames are its own): the stack three calls deep and in a module's body, against the same functions without a frame; the limits; a method, a property, `__len__`, a generator, a call a built-in makes, a lambda, a class body; two signals of one arrival; a handler interrupted; `print_stack` on stderr. The stops in `test_traceback_entries.py`: a kept frame's `repr()` and listing, `f_lineno`, `walk_stack`.

**2026-11-15 — traceback's suggestions from the frame that raised (C-7-88).** Formatting an AttributeError or a NameError with its traceback stopped by name at `tb_frame`, which CPython's `_compute_suggestion_error` reads: for a NameError the names its frame could see, for an AttributeError whether the object is the `self` of the method (which may then be offered its private names). So `traceback.print_exc()`, `logging.exception` and a unittest failure ended the program for the two commonest mistakes; noted as left on 2026-11-13.
* **What reads the frame now.** `_compute_suggestion_error` is CPython's text with its three frame reads replaced (`runtime/replaced/traceback.py`, `[replaced]`): `__bella_traceback_names__(tb)` gives the frame's `f_locals`, `f_globals` and `f_builtins` for an entry of the runtime's. The locals are made as `locals()` makes them, from the code record's table: of a frame still running, from the frame; of a frame an exception left, from what that exception's entry took over; a class body's and a module's are their namespace (a class body's is kept by the entry when an exception leaves it).
* **Two exceptions of one frame.** `except NameError: raise KeyError(...)` prints the first under the second, and the first's entry for the frame has no locals: the second took them. CPython's entries hold one frame object between them. The frame's entries now share a link that says which of them has the locals, for as long as that entry lives, and the others read them there. Nothing is held longer or shorter than before.
* **What a comprehension leaves.** Measured: once an exception has left an inlined comprehension, CPython's frame no longer shows the comprehension's variables, so the frame's own are what the suggestion is made from, here as there.
* **Tried and taken back: the frame's locals kept by its traceback when the frame is over.** A frame that caught the exception and returned has released its locals, where CPython's traceback keeps them; I wrote that (the entries of a frame share what it leaves, filled at its end by any way out) and case C-9-11 passed. It is RL-222, which the author decided on 2026-11-03 to take with the collector (D-12, part 2): an exception kept in a local of its own frame then makes a cycle nothing frees before step 3c. The change was taken out again before any commit; C-9-11 stays as the case that step will have to pass (it fails compiled now, and is in no baseline), and such a frame's suggestion stops by name.
* **The manifest.** `[dynamic.traceback]` reviewed the `__import__` in that function; a replaced function's imports are not counted, so the entry went and the `[replaced]` line says that the replacement keeps CPython's body and its imports (`unicodedata` and `_suggestions`, which the runtime has; the module an ImportError names, already imported). Both closures recomputed: the same 183 modules.
* **Still stops by name:** a frame that is over and whose locals went with it (it caught the exception and returned; a generator's that waits; the entry that had them is gone), and a frame with a list, set or dict comprehension running in it.
* **Not in this step:** the report of an uncaught exception, which the runtime prints itself, still adds no suggestion. Put to the author.
* **Cases and tests.** C-7-88 (30 rows: what is added for an attribute, a private one from inside and outside a method, a nested one, another kind of object; for a local, a global, a builtin, `self.name`, a forgotten import, a shared name, a generator, a lambda, a comprehension, a class body, the module's body; for a name a module lacks). `pipeline/tests/test_traceback_suggestions.py`: every way to the text (`print_exception`, `format_exc`, `TracebackException`, logging, unittest; chains and groups; the frame still running; names that are not ASCII) and the stops.

**2026-11-15 — three findings on the way (RL-445 to RL-447): what a comprehension uses of the function around it.** The full test run after C-7-88 failed in `test_slot_tables_are_cpythons` on the case's function with nested comprehensions; the test's allowance did not follow a comprehension inside a comprehension. Looking at what it allows for showed three defects, none of them the case's.
* **RL-445, `co_cellvars`.** A variable that only inlined comprehensions read is a cell of the compiled function (each comprehension is a function of its own) and a fast local of CPython's. The code object's table already left out the names a comprehension assigns; it now leaves out every cell that no scope with a frame of its own captures (a function, a lambda, a generator expression, a class body), a parameter and a comprehension's own variable included.
* **RL-446, `locals()` (my commit of the same day).** The runtime took "is a cell" from `co_cellvars`, which is CPython's list and not the frame's, so a variable assigned by a comprehension came out as its cell object. The code record's table now says it for each variable (`Code::frame_slots`: twice the place, plus one for a cell).
* **RL-447, the order of `co_varnames`.** Names a nested comprehension binds outward come in the symbol table's order: its generators, with their conditions, before its element; a dict comprehension's key before its value (measured on 3.15.2).
* **The test** follows comprehensions nested in comprehensions.

**2026-11-19 — the runtime's own report of an exception keeps its last line: D-28 (C-7-89; the author's decision).** The report a compiled program prints for an uncaught exception ends with the exception's message, where CPython's goes on `. Did you mean '.total' instead of '.totl'?`. Put to the author on 2026-11-15 with four routes: (A) the last line from `traceback` in a program that holds it, (B) the whole report from `traceback`, (C) `traceback`'s rules ported into the runtime, (D) left as it is and registered. The author chose D.
* **What the deviation covers,** measured on 3.15.2 before it was written down. CPython has `traceback` print every report its C makes of an exception (`_PyErr_Display` calls `traceback._print_exception_bltin`): the uncaught exception, `sys.excepthook` and `sys.__excepthook__`, and the unraisable hook's (a `__del__`, an atexit callback, a generator's close). The module adds to the last line of an AttributeError, a NameError, an ImportError and a ModuleNotFoundError (`TracebackException.__init__`), and for a ModuleNotFoundError whose `name` is a module of the standard library it replaces the message with `Standard library module 'x' was not found`. So an uncaught import of a module phase 1 leaves out (D-20) reads `No module named 'bz2'` compiled and `Standard library module 'bz2' was not found` on CPython without the module. The question as put named the hints only; the replaced message and the unraisable hook's reports are the same decision's and are in the register's text.
* **Nothing else differs.** CPython's report with every addition cut is the compiled program's line for line: the entries, the lines between chained exceptions, a group's box, the notes (`pipeline/tests/test_runtime_reports.py`, a program that holds `traceback`, whose own functions give the additions in the same run).
* **How CPython shows it.** With the import of `traceback` blocked, `_PyErr_Display` prints with its C (`print_exception`), and that is the compiled program's report for every exception that is not a group and has no notes; the C prints a group as one exception and leaves the notes out. So the harness simulates D-28 by blocking `traceback` at start-up (`SIMULATIONS`, beside D-20 and D-27), C-7-89 has two expectations, and a case that declares D-28 reports no group and no note and imports nothing that imports `traceback`. The staging replay blocks the same import for such a case.
* **Not changed:** the runtime. `traceback`'s own functions give the additions as before (C-7-88); a program that wants them in its last words can install a `sys.excepthook` that calls `traceback.print_exception`.
* **Not covered:** a SyntaxError's keyword hint, which needs what the parser leaves in the exception (E-01: no parser in a compiled program); a thread's report (threads are [P2+]; CPython's is its C's too and will differ the same way).
* **The specification:** the D-28 row and its paragraph in section 11, a clause in section 8's "Entry and exit" (the last line per D-28), v0.20. The mechanism is in the design document (10.4).
* **Cases and tests.** C-7-89, compiled leg (the evaluators refuse a name bound nowhere, as for C-7-88): twenty kinds of exception through `sys.__excepthook__`, `sys.excepthook` with and without the traceback argument, and the last one uncaught; what the program reads of each exception on standard output. `test_runtime_reports.py`: the reports with an address, a group, notes, the atexit callback after the uncaught exception; the two ModuleNotFoundError rows under D-20's simulation.
* **Checked per step:** the case on CPython (both expectations) and compiled; the new module and the harness's and staging's tests; lint, a cold mypy on the case, verify; the staging check. The runtime did not change, so the compiled targets were not rerun.

**2026-11-19 — the number methods' deprecated answers warn (C-6-23).** Five of the runtime's stops stood where CPython warns and goes on: an `__index__`, `__int__`, `__float__` or `__complex__` that answers with an instance of a strict subclass of the type asked for (a bool for an int too), and a complex number given to `complex()` as its real or imaginary part. They warn now, through `warn_ex` as `~` on a bool does, and give CPython's value.
* **What each gives,** from Objects/abstract.c, floatobject.c and complexobject.c: `PyNumber_Index` and `PyNumber_Long` the value as an exact int; `PyNumber_Float` an exact float and `PyFloat_AsDouble` the double; `try_complex_special_method` the subclass's instance, whose two parts are read. `complex_new_impl` with a complex imaginary part takes that part's imaginary half off the real one, which the runtime had not written because it stopped before (`complex(1, 2j)` is `(-1+0j)`, `complex(1j, 2j)` is `(-2+1j)`). The warning's text is 3.15's (`GivesIndex.__index__() must return an int, not Wide.  The ability to return an instance of a strict subclass of int is deprecated, ...`), with the classes' full names as `%T` prints them.
* **Where it warns:** at the conversion, so every place that takes a number has it, at the line of the call (a prelude's frames are not CPython's). Under a filter that makes the warning an error the conversion fails with it after the method was called; `struct` answers that with its own error, as its C does.
* **RL-450, found by the case:** `struct` gave no warning for a float. Its prelude had `PyFloat_AsDouble` and `PyComplex_AsCComplex` written out by hand; it uses the runtime's now, and an unused hand-written `PyNumber_Index` left `_csv.py`.
* **RL-448, found by the sweep:** `struct.error` raised compiled had the exception it replaced as its `__context__`, where CPython's has none. The preludes wrote the C's `PyErr_SetString` over a pending error as `raise New(...) from None` inside the `except` clause, which keeps the first exception (and its traceback) and sets `__suppress_context__`. Measured at every place a program can reach: CPython's exception has the caller's handled exception as its context, or none, and the flag false. Sixteen places raise outside the handler now (the review log lists them); the one `from None` left in the preludes is importlib's own text.
* **RL-449, found while fixing that:** a `csv.reader` at the end of its input passed the input's StopIteration on, value and all; CPython's `next()` raises one of its own.
* **Still stops by name,** each to be done with its case (`generator.throw(type, value, tb)` followed the same day, C-3-75): a `binascii.crc32` seed outside 32 bits (3.15's `integer value out of range`, which belongs to the unsigned converters wherever the runtime has them); an abstract AST node class instantiated; `Struct.__init__` with another format than `__new__`'s (a FutureWarning); the import system's two warnings for a module without `__spec__.loader`.
* **Cases and tests.** C-6-23 compiled and under eval2 (eval1 runs the program in frames of its own, so a warning's line is the evaluator's; 38 rows: each conversion from the places CPython's functions are reached, the warning's class, line and words, the same call under an error filter with what was called before it failed, and the warning printed once for a line by the filters a program starts with). `test_deprecated_number_answers.py`: 141 places a program takes a number from, each recorded and as an error. `test_replaced_exceptions.py`: 27 places with the chain of contexts, alone and inside a handler. `test_complex.py` lost its four stop rows.

**2026-11-19 — a generator's `throw(type, value, traceback)` (C-3-75).** Two and three arguments stopped by name at the DeprecationWarning CPython gives for them. The form is written out now, as `gen_throw`, `_gen_throw` and `gen_set_exception` have it, and warns through `warn_ex`.
* **What is thrown.** A class is made of the value as `PyErr_NormalizeException` makes it: no argument for None, a tuple's items as the arguments, anything else as the one argument, and the value itself when it is an instance of the class (the exception is then of the instance's class). When the making fails, what it raised is thrown in its place, with the traceback given if it has none of its own; a making that gives no exception is a TypeError with CPython's words. An instance takes no value.
* **The traceback** given becomes the exception's before the generator is resumed, so the generator's entry is added in front of it (`PyErr_Restore`); None is no traceback.
* **What fails in the caller:** a third argument that is no traceback, an instance with a value, what is no exception. The generator is not resumed and stays where it was.
* **RL-451, found by the case:** the last of those was raised inside the generator, for one argument too (`g.throw(5)` could be caught by the generator's own `except`). Fixed with the form.
* **Delegation.** A generator suspended in `yield from` passes the arguments on as given: to a generator directly, to another object's `throw` with as many as there were (`PyObject_CallFunctionObjArgs`); a GeneratorExit closes the inner one first.
* **RL-452, found by the sweep:** the RuntimeError of PEP 479 had an entry for the frame of the generator the StopIteration left. CPython's handler makes the error and re-raises it past the place where a frame adds its entry, so the traceback starts at the caller and only the StopIteration behind it names the generator. `gen_uncaught` throws the error as one the frame has already recorded. It showed in `traceback.extract_tb` and in the report of the error uncaught.
* **Cases and tests.** C-3-75 compiled and under eval2 (eval1's frames are its own): 26 rows with the warning's line and words, the warning as an error, a generator not started and one that is over, and the traceback given as the instance's own afterwards. `test_generators.py`: 8,960 calls (sixteen kinds of first argument, twelve of second, five of third, against ten states of the generator, each with what `next()` gives afterwards), the warning for each count of arguments, and the tracebacks of PEP 479's error in five shapes with its report.
* **Checked per step:** the case on its legs; 21 test modules of the area and the tests that hold a stop by name; lint, a cold mypy on the case, verify (424/424), the staging check, the six compiled targets, both evaluator corpora.

**2026-11-19 — an unsigned argument out of range, and an abstract AST node class (C-7-90, C-7-91).** Two more stops that stood where 3.15 warns.
* **A field of bits.** The clinic's `unsigned_int(bitwise=True)` and `unsigned_long(bitwise=True)` read the int with `PyLong_AsNativeBytes` and warn `integer value out of range` (a DeprecationWarning) when it needs more bytes than the field has, as an unsigned number or as a signed one, then take the low bits. So for 32 bits `2**32` and `-2**31 - 1` warn and `-1` does not. `os.memfd_create`'s flags and `signal.pthread_kill`'s thread id already did this; the start values of `zlib.crc32`, `zlib.adler32`, the two combine functions and `binascii.crc32` stopped by name for everything outside 0..2**32-1, `-1` included, which is the commonest start value there is. They warn and cut now. `os.statx`, `fcntl`, `select` and `mmap` have such arguments too and are not in the runtime; `binascii.crc_hqx` is not written yet.
* **Abstract node classes.** `ast.expr()` and the other twelve (AST itself among them) warn as `ast_type_init` does, before the arguments are looked at, and make the node. RL-453: the prelude knew the classes by name, without AST, so `ast.AST()` was silent; it asks the set of the classes themselves now, and a class derived from one is not abstract.
* **Left:** `struct.Struct` subclassed, and `Struct.__init__` with another format than `__new__`'s. 3.15 moved the work to `__new__` and keeps the old ways with a FutureWarning and three DeprecationWarnings, with an object that can be without a format in between; that is a step of its own. The import system's two warnings (`_bless_my_loader`) are reached only through `warn_explicit` with `module_globals`, which stops before them.
* **Cases and tests.** C-7-90 and C-7-91 compiled and under eval2 (a warning's line is eval1's own); C-7-90 verified in the image, since it makes a memory file and signals its own thread. `test_unsigned_arguments.py`: nine arguments against 34 values, each recorded and as an error, the thread id with what 64 bits drop; every node class of `ast` called with nothing. `test_system_modules.py` lost three stop rows.
* **Checked per step:** the cases on their legs; nine test modules of the area and the tests that hold a stop by name; lint, cold mypy on the cases, verify (428/428), the staging check, the six compiled targets, both evaluator corpora.

**2026-11-20 — reviews S22 and A22: what was fixed (RL-454 to RL-522).** The author asked for a review of `3234866..bc3f700` by a swarm of Sonnet reviewers and Codex's Astra at low effort. 79 findings, every one reproduced on both sides before a verdict; 62 are defects, three are the specification's (D-20, E-04), three are questions for the author. This entry is the round's first commit of fixes: 34 findings and one found on the way. The rest are in TODO.
* **Two crashes and a hang.** A signal handler could resize the bytearray a regular expression was matching (C-7-85 let handlers run inside the engine, and only `sub` and the scanner held the buffer): every entry point holds it now, so the handler gets CPython's BufferError (RL-496). The report of an exception cast a group's `exceptions` to a tuple unseen (RL-512) and let an exception out of itself, into `std::terminate` (RL-513). `list(range(2**63))` did not end: the length hint asked only objects of Python classes, and a range that long has no length, which is CPython's OverflowError (RL-470).
* **The runtime's report of an exception** is closer to `traceback` in four things and has CPython's fallback. It folds a repeated frame after three entries (an uncaught RecursionError was 1002 lines; RL-510), reads `sys.tracebacklimit` with the module's slice, quirk included (RL-511), prints a hand-made SyntaxError's file, text and carets (RL-515), writes each piece and an empty string and does not flush (RL-516), and reads the class's `__qualname__` and `__module__` as attributes (RL-519). When any of that raises, CPython's `_PyErr_Display` clears the error and prints with its C: the chain by the exceptions' own fields, tracebacks with the C's own reading of the limit, no group's box and no notes. `print_plain` is that printer. Two things it cannot do exactly stop by name: a report after `del sys.stderr` or to a stream that cannot be written (CPython dumps the object's address and reference count), and a SyntaxError that carries the parser's `_metadata`. The report of an exception that is only told of (the unraisable hook's) is dropped without a word when it cannot be written, as CPython drops it: a program whose reader has gone ends with status 120 and no more (`test_signals.py`, which the first version of this failed).
* **Generators.** The frame is marked executing while `throw()` makes the exception (RL-454); a failed lookup of the delegate's `throw` is the caller's (RL-455); the delegating generator is on the stack while the delegate is thrown into (RL-456); the subclass check asks the metaclass (RL-479); a class that answers with another class's instance is called once more, as `PyErr_Restore` does (RL-480); a `close` attribute that raises is reported and passed over (RL-481).
* **The compiler.** A wrong number of arguments is the caller's TypeError: the emitter binds the arguments before the frame guard exists, so the traceback has no entry for the function and keeps nothing of the call (RL-482). A comprehension in a nested block has its variables after the names the block bound before it (RL-487): the resolver takes the comprehension's place among a function's names from where its helper is called, which is where the comprehension stands, and no longer from where the helper is defined, which is before the outermost statement around it. **Tried first and taken back:** lowering the helper inside the block. It gave the same order and made the helper function on every turn of a loop; the author asked about that cost, and the resolver's way has none. Over the reviewer's ten probe files 43 of 331 functions differed from CPython's `co_varnames`, and none do now. RL-460 was a regression of RL-445: a class body that binds `x` and forwards the function's `x` to a method hid the function's cell.
* **Preludes.** The handlers moved for RL-448 answer a BaseException too, as the C does (RL-457). A close whose flush and close both fail gives the second exception the first as its context, not its cause (RL-472); `zip` and `map` with `strict` raise outside the handler (RL-473); a buffered object is closed once its buffer is freed, also by a close that failed (RL-522, gh-138720). `__file__` leaves the program's globals when its body is over (RL-501).
* **The harness** refuses a case with two `# deviation:` lines (RL-520), and case C-7-85 lost an operand that checked nothing (RL-465).
* **Left open:** 28 findings of low severity (TODO, "Open from reviews S22 and A22"), among them one that is a step of its own: the line of a traceback entry inside a statement of several lines (RL-494).
* **Tests.** `pipeline/tests/test_review_2026_11_19.py`: 39 programs, the findings' own or lighter ones, each compared on standard output, exit status and masked standard error.

**2026-11-20 — `PYTHONWARNINGS` is ignored, for now (the author's decision; RL-483, D-18, spec v0.21).** A reviewer found that a compiled program does not read the variable and that the specification's Environment paragraph does not name it. The author was given three ways: honoured everywhere (the option parser ported into the runtime, so that a program without `warnings` has it too; recommended), honoured in a program that holds `warnings`, or ignored and said so. Decided: ignored for now, the first to be looked at again (TODO, Later).
* **What a program sees:** the warning filters CPython has without the variable and without `-W`, and an empty `sys.warnoptions`, whatever the variable says. Nothing in the runtime changed; the paragraph, D-18's row and the register say it now.
* **An entry here was wrong:** the step of 2026-11-15 (C-7-86) said the variable was ignored under D-18, which did not name it then.
* **Held by** `pipeline/tests/test_start_up_environment.py`: two programs, one holding `warnings` and one not, under CPython without the variable and compiled with it at eleven values (an action, a category, a module, several, what is no option, nothing).
* **Found by sweeping the class** (one probe under 42 settings of CPython's start-up variables, each side against its own run without the variable), all three for the author: about twenty other variables are in the same state (RL-527); `PYTHONIOENCODING` is honoured by the runtime since C-7-30 although D-15 and decision 10 say it is ignored (RL-526); and the report of an uncaught exception has no colour at a terminal (RL-528).

**2026-11-20 — the other variables of CPython's start-up: three honoured, the rest ignored and named (the author's decision; RL-527, C-8-16, spec v0.22).** The sweep after `PYTHONWARNINGS` had found about twenty variables that CPython reads and a compiled program did not, none of them named in the Environment paragraph. The author was given three ways: name the class as ignored; that, and honour `PYTHONUNBUFFERED`, `PYTHONINTMAXSTRDIGITS` and `PYTHON_CPU_COUNT` now (recommended: they are what a deployment sets); leave the paragraph. Decided: the second.
* **The three, as CPython reads them.** `PYTHONUNBUFFERED` is a flag (`_Py_get_env_flag`): any text but a number below one. Standard output and error are then text streams over the raw file that write through, neither is line buffered, standard input keeps its buffer, and the C library's three streams are unbuffered. `PYTHONINTMAXSTRDIGITS` is 0 or at least 640 and is the limit the program starts with and `sys.flags.int_max_str_digits`. `PYTHON_CPU_COUNT` is `default` or at least 1 and is what `os.cpu_count()` says, and `os.process_cpu_count()` with it, since `os.py` asks `sys._get_cpu_count_config()` when it is imported. A variable set to nothing is not set. All are read once: what the program does to its environment later changes nothing.
* **A value CPython refuses** ends the program before its first line as CPython ends: `Fatal Python error: config_init_cpu_count: -X cpu_count=n option: n is missing or an invalid number, n must be greater than 0`, `Python runtime state: preinitialized` and an empty line on standard error, status 1. The words are the C's, its function's name among them: they are what the person who set the variable is told under CPython. A seed that cannot be taken is told of first, then the digits, then the count (measured; the case has the three pairs).
* **RL-529, found with it:** a `PYTHONHASHSEED` that CPython refuses (`abc`, `4294967296`, `7x`) was taken as some seed, and one set to nothing was the seed 0 while `sys.flags.hash_randomization` said 1. It is read with the others now.
* **RL-530, found by the case:** `sys.flags.int_max_str_digits` did not follow `sys.set_int_max_str_digits`. CPython puts a new flags object in `sys` each time (`_PySys_SetFlagObj`), so the one a program kept says what it said, and a deleted `sys.flags` is `RuntimeError: lost sys.flags` with the limit unchanged. A `sys.flags` that is another object stops by name: the C reads its memory as a flags object.
* **The rest are ignored,** and a program runs as CPython does without them. The paragraph says so in one sentence, with `PYTHONBREAKPOINT` (D-11) and `PYTHONCOERCECLOCALE` named as read, so that the sentence is true of what the runtime does; D-18's row names the twelve that change what a script sees. `PYTHONIOENCODING` is D-15's and still open (RL-526). Honouring more of them is on TODO's Later list.
* **How:** one record, `StartUp` (`docs/design.md`, the end of 10.4).
* **Cases and tests.** C-8-16: the program starts itself again (`os.posix_spawn(sys.executable, sys.orig_argv, ...)`, which is the interpreter with the case under CPython and the program itself compiled) under 53 settings with standard input at `/dev/null` and both outputs in one pipe, and prints what the second one saw: what is written past a stream comes first when the stream keeps its own, the conversions at each limit, the count as the variable's or the system's, the hashes under a fixed seed. It passes compiled and under both evaluators, and was verified in the image, also with fewer CPUs than the machine has. `pipeline/tests/test_start_up_environment.py`: 59 values of the four variables compiled against CPython with the same value, standard error to the letter; 31 settings of variables that are not read against CPython without them, where CPython with the variable must differ or the row shows nothing; four programs that use the streams (an uncaught exception after half a line, `os._exit` without a flush, `input()` and its prompt, bytes written under the text) with both outputs in one pipe, buffered and not; `sys.flags` when the limit is set.
* **Checked per step:** the case on its three legs, in the image and in the staging check; 20 test modules of the areas touched (the standard streams, `sys`, big ints, `posix`, signals, the runtime's reports, warnings without the module); lint, a cold mypy on the case, verify (430/430), `make slice`, the six compiled targets, both evaluator corpora (170 and 186, no regression).

**2026-11-20 — the byte-order mark and where a stream starts (RL-531).** Measuring `PYTHONIOENCODING=utf-16` for the author's next question showed standard output with a byte-order mark where CPython's has none. It is the text layer's, not the variable's: `textio.c` has functions of its own for nine codecs (`ascii`, `iso8859-1`, `utf-8`, and UTF-16 and UTF-32 with and without a byte order), picks one by the name the codec gives itself, and then never asks the codec's incremental encoder. For `utf-16` and `utf-32` the function writes the mark only at the start of a stream, and only a stream that can seek and is at position 0 is at its start; anywhere else it writes the machine's byte order without a mark, and a failure there names `utf-16-le`. A first write that fails ends the start too. The prelude asked the incremental encoder every time, which writes the mark at its first call whatever the stream is. It has the C's choice now (`_tw_encode`); the encoder is still made, reset and given its state as before, since `seek` and `tell` do that in the C as well.

**2026-11-20 — `PYTHONIOENCODING` is honoured; D-15 narrowed (the author's decision; RL-526, C-8-17, spec v0.23).** The specification had the variable as ignored since decision 10 ("UTF-8 always"). The runtime had read it since the standard streams became `_io` objects on 2026-11-07, an entry here said so, and the specification was not changed: a decision reopened without the author. Put to the author as two ways, honoured with D-15 narrowed (recommended: it is what a deployment sets, and what the harness sets for every case) or ignored as decided, with the runtime and the harness changed to that. Decided: honoured.
* **What a program sees:** the three standard streams with the encoding and errors the variable names, read as `config_init_stdio_encoding` reads it (either part may be left out; an encoding alone makes the errors `strict`; standard error's errors stay `backslashreplace`). UTF-8 mode stays on for everything else: `open()`, the file system, `encoding="locale"`.
* **Repaired with the decision.** `sys.stdout.encoding` was the variable's text and is now the name the codec has for itself (`iso8859-1` for `latin-1`, `utf-8` for `UTF8`), as `init_stdio_encoding` makes it. A name that is no encoding stopped with the words for a deleted `sys.stderr`, because the failed stream was reported through a `sys.stderr` that did not exist yet; it ends the program now as CPython ends: `Fatal Python error: init_stdio_encoding: failed to get the Python codec name of the stdio encoding`, `Python runtime state: core initialized`, `LookupError: unknown encoding: nonsense`, an empty line and the thread's line, status 1. A name whose bytes are not UTF-8 is `RuntimeError: cannot encode stdio_encoding` there, and errors that are no text end it in `init_sys_streams`, each as measured.
* **A codec outside the phase-1 set** (`cp1252`, `utf-7`) is one that is not installed (D-20): the registry does not find it, and the program ends as for a name that is no encoding. The author was told it would stop at its start with a message that names it; this is that, in CPython's words and with CPython's status, and it is what CPython itself prints when the codec's module is absent.
* **One residue, a stop by name:** a name that is not ASCII. 3.15's `encodings.normalize_encoding` warns of it (a DeprecationWarning nobody is shown) and so imports `warnings`; a program that holds the module agrees with CPython, one that does not stops at the import. D-27 is of the warnings the C gives, not of a library module's own `import warnings`.
* **Cases and tests.** C-8-17: the program starts itself again under 34 settings with a file of bytes as its standard input and prints the bytes the second one wrote, the thread's number and name cut out; compiled, both evaluators, the image. `test_the_encoding_of_the_standard_streams`: 43 values compiled against CPython on standard output's bytes, the status and standard error; eight codecs outside the set; the two names that are not ASCII, with and without `warnings`.

**2026-11-20 — the second flush at exit is the streams' own going (RL-532).** The runtime flushes the standard streams twice when a program ends, before and after the modules are cleared, and treated the two alike: a failure was reported and made the status 120. CPython's second `flush_std_files` finds `sys.stdout` None, since clearing `sys` is part of clearing the modules; what a finaliser printed leaves when the stream object is finalised, and a failure there is dropped. Measured: a module-level object whose `__del__` prints, standard output a pipe nobody reads, is status 0 and a silent standard error under CPython. The second flush here says nothing now and leaves the status alone.
* **How it showed.** RL-531's commit (`8b0bd62`) made `test_a_reader_that_has_gone` fail with the report printed twice, and I had not run the signals module with that commit. The report of the second failure had always been attempted; it was lost because standard error encoded through an object of `encodings.utf_8`, whose module was cleared by then. Since RL-531 the text layer encodes UTF-8 itself, as the C does, and the report came out. The wrong status was there before.
* **Checked for the two together** (`PYTHONIOENCODING` and the second flush): C-8-17 on its three legs, in the image and in the staging check; 15 test modules of the areas touched (the streams, codecs, signals, threads, weak references, `sys`, `posix`, the runtime's reports); the 47 cases of the compiled corpus that touch exit, finalisers or the standard streams; lint, a cold mypy on the case, verify (432/432), the six compiled targets, both evaluator corpora (171 and 187, no regression).

**2026-11-20 — the runtime's report has no colour (the author's decision; RL-528, D-28, spec v0.24).** CPython's `traceback` colours the report of an exception nothing caught when it may (`_colorize.can_colorize`: a terminal; `PYTHON_COLORS`, `NO_COLOR`, `FORCE_COLOR`, `TERM=dumb`), and D-28 said that everything above the last line was the same. Put to the author as two ways, plain always or coloured as CPython colours. Decided: plain always, which is what the report CPython prints without `traceback` is. Nothing in the runtime changes.
* **Held by** `test_the_runtimes_report_has_no_colour` (`pipeline/tests/test_runtime_reports.py`): standard error on a pseudo-terminal or in a pipe under twelve settings of the variables; the compiled report has no escape sequence in any and is CPython's plain report after the mask; CPython colours in seven.
* **Measured on the way:** `traceback.print_exc()` is plain under CPython at a terminal too (only `traceback._print_exception_bltin`, the uncaught report's entry, asks whether it may colour), and a compiled program agrees.

**2026-11-20 — what a built-in type keeps for itself has no name (the author's decision: A; RL-507, case C-3-76).** The prelude writes about 120 of CPython's C types in Python (122 classes with slots, 327 names) and each kept its state in `__slots__` of ordinary private names. A class that derived from one and used such a name for itself overwrote the state: `class Page(enumerate)` with `self._count = len(rows)` numbered its rows from 3. The author was given three ways (A: the state has no name in the object model; B: one reserved spelling for the 327 names; C: refuse the class at build) with measurements (no cost at run time for A; a scan of 29,638 classes of public code found one clash, trio's `_IdRef`, harmless by luck) and chose A.
* **What a program sees:** nothing but CPython's behaviour. A slot of such a type that is no member of CPython's type is no attribute of the object and nothing in the type's dict; a class that derives from the type uses any name for itself, as an attribute, a slot, a property, a method or a class attribute; the type's own work reads its field and asks no `__setattr__`, `__delattr__` or `__getattr__` of the class. No deviation and no word of the specification changes.
* **How:** `docs/design.md`, the paragraph before section 11. The model first (`RefClass(hidden=...)`, `Runtime.state_get`, `state_set`, `state_del`; its test against CPython's `enumerate`), the header from it (`hide_slots`, `state_index`; `make object-test` now compares the two on this, under the sanitizers), the runtime (`note_prelude_class` hides what the names table does not have; `rt::state_get` and its kin), the emitter (in the prelude's units, an attribute of a name a prelude class declares). The prelude's text is as it was, but for its own earlier ways round the same matter, which are this one now: stores through `object.__setattr__` are `_bella_state_set`, and the hand-written refusals of `_p_phcount`, `_ga_parameters` and Union's three names in `__setattr__` and `__delattr__` are gone (a class that derives from `partial` sets `_p_phcount` as CPython lets it).
* **Measured:** a million items through `enumerate` 1.65 s before and 1.19 s after, `islice(count())` 4.86 and 2.75, `deque` 15.6 and 13.2, `zip` 2.93 and 2.73: the read of a field is a lookup in a small table where it was a walk of the classes. An object's size is unchanged, and the hidden slots' descriptors leave their classes at the start.
* **The class, not the instance:** `pipeline/tests/test_state_without_names.py` asks about 100 kinds of object and every type the prelude-backed modules hold, for all 327 names, under CPython and compiled: the compiled program shows no name CPython does not and every one CPython does. It found two more of the kind (RL-534): the csv reader's and writer's C functions were private methods of the types, and `_string`'s two iterators were never presented as CPython's types.
* **Found on the way (RL-533):** a class that derives from a built-in type and overrides `__getattribute__` ran with the override never asked; the stop the specification names was `object.__new__`'s alone. It is asked wherever an instance is made now.

**2026-11-20 — review A23, and the members a built-in type shows (RL-535 to RL-562; case C-3-77).** The author asked for a review by Codex (`gpt-6.2-astra`, medium effort) of `bc3f700..7a8e3f2`, the day's change first. Its first run was stopped by its provider's filter while it read the field code; the second, asked in the same words but for the vocabulary, reported 28 findings, each run on both sides by the session before its verdict. Twenty-three are fixed, four are open and one is the author's.
* **The half RL-507 had left.** `7a8e3f2` took the name from a field CPython's type does not show and left a field it does show (`partial.func`, `GenericAlias.__origin__`, a stream's `_finalizing`) an ordinary slot, read and stored by its name by the type's own code. A class attribute `func = str` on a class deriving from `partial` then took over the call, a property `__origin__` kept a generic alias from being made, and a member read under a second name ended the program with an uncaught C++ exception. Now every slot of a presented type is a field of its own; one CPython shows keeps a descriptor that reads that field (`members` in the model, `Plan::State` in the header). The model first, as before; `make object-test` compares the two on it.
* **Read-only and bool members.** The prelude kept `partial`'s, GenericAlias's, Union's and Struct's members read-only by a `__setattr__` of its own, which `object.__setattr__` and the member's `__set__` went past. A member now says so itself (`__readonly__`, `__boolean__` in the class; `check_member_store`), the hand-written guards are gone, and a deriving class sets its own attribute of the name as CPython lets it.
* **The order a built-in type lets go of its fields** is the order its class declares them, which stands for its clear function's; `partial` and the two below are right by it. The other types' declarations have not been compared with their C one by one: TODO.
* **Older than the range, fixed on the way:** a class deriving from `_csv.Dialect` changed what the reader and writer did (they read the dialect's public names); `enumerate` and `zip` let go of the item just given (they keep and reuse their result tuple now, as the C does); `BytesIO` and `StringIO` state with and without a dict; a text stream's newline translation by the text's own `replace`, its pending writes holding a short ASCII text itself, an encoder that gives its text back, a codec name judged by its type; the second flush at exit asks a stream of `_io`'s alone; a directory as standard input ends the start with CPython's message; the types the bootstrap presents forget their Python origin, and no static type has a `__module__` entry (the sweep found fourteen).

**2026-11-20 — a finaliser at exit when `sys` still reaches its module's globals: exact, with the collector (the author's decision; RL-514).** The finding of review S22 said that a module-level object's `__del__` never runs under CPython once a function of `__main__` is stored in `sys` (as `sys.excepthook`, or under any name). Measured again with a finaliser that writes through `os.write`: CPython does run it, at the last collection of `finalize_modules`, after every module's globals are set to None and `sys`'s dict is cleared, because until then `sys` reaches the module's globals through the function. A finaliser that asks a module's global by then fails with nothing left to report it, which looked like "never"; one that holds what it needs prints on both sides. The compiled program's exit walk finalises it while the modules are whole. The author was given three ways (exact now, by a reachability pass of the exit's own; D-07 widened to say so; exact with the collector by trial deletion, already decided and queued, which needs the same reachability) and chose the third. Until the collector is in the header this is a known difference with no deviation, listed with the collector in TODO; its case comes with that step.

**2026-11-20 — the limit of digits and a main program's own literals (the author's decision: A; RL-555, case C-8-18, spec v0.25).** Since the same day a compiled program honours `PYTHONINTMAXSTRDIGITS`. CPython applies the limit to the program's own decimal literals too, because it compiles a main program at each start: a literal longer than the limit is the parser's `SyntaxError`, status 1, and nothing of the program runs. A compiled program converted its literals at the build and ran. For an imported module CPython's answer depends on its cached code: without it the import raises the `SyntaxError`, with it the module is imported. The author was given three ways (check the main program at its start; register that the build's limit counts; check every module at its import) and chose the first.
* **What a program sees:** a decimal integer literal of the main program that is longer than the limit given at launch, anywhere in its text, ends the program before its first statement as CPython ends it: `File "...", line N`, the line, `SyntaxError: Exceeds the limit (L digits) for integer string conversion: value has D digits; ... - Consider hexadecimal for huge integer literals to avoid decimal conversion limits.` The first such literal in the text is the one named, its digits counted without underscores. A literal of another base, a float, an imaginary number and a literal of zeros have no limit, as in CPython. A module the program imports keeps its literals: the behaviour CPython has with cached code.
* **Wording added to the specification's Environment paragraph (v0.25):** "The limit of digits holds for the main program's own decimal literals too: one longer than the limit ends the program before its first statement, with the `SyntaxError` CPython's compiler raises at each start (C-8-18); a module the program imports keeps its literals, as it does when CPython runs it from its cached code."
* **How:** `docs/design.md`, the paragraph on what the environment said at the start. A literal over the build's own limit is refused by the build, with CPython's words, as before.
* **Held by:** C-8-18 (the program starts itself again under ten limits; compiled leg; the two evaluators refuse a program's SyntaxError as a build does, so the case is not in their baselines); `test_a_literal_longer_than_the_limit_of_digits` (nine places of a text: an f-string, an annotation, a default, a match pattern, a continuation line, two on one line, a negated one, one with underscores) and `test_an_imported_modules_literal_is_as_from_its_cached_code`, in `pipeline/tests/test_start_up_environment.py`.
* **No question is left for the author** from the reviews so far.

**2026-11-20 — a cell for each declaration of a slot (RL-541 of review A23, case C-3-78; no decision of the author's was needed).** The model keyed a slot by its name, so a class that declares again a slot of its base's shared the base's cell; CPython gives each declaration a member and a cell. Seen by a program only through the base's own member (`D.x.__get__(obj)`, `super().x`, the member under another name), and at the end of an object, which lets go of two values where there was one. The model changed first (`RefClass.shadowed_fields`, `_cell_of`), then the header (`ClassObject::shadowed`, `slot_cell`); `python3 -m refmodel.selftest` on four seeds and `make object-test` hold, and the lockstep test of the state has a part for it.
* **What a program sees:** CPython's behaviour and no other; no deviation, no spec wording.
* **Found beside it (RL-563):** a member of a class that is no base of the object's class, kept by that class under a name, was read as a slot of the name; it is CPython's TypeError now, for a read, a store and a delete.
* **How:** `docs/design.md`, the paragraph "A slot a class declares again".
* **Held by:** C-3-78 on the compiled leg and both evaluators; `test_a_slot_declared_again_is_a_second_cell` in the model's tests; part 7 of `runtime/tests/object_state.py` and `.cpp`; the two programs RL-541 and RL-563 in `pipeline/tests/test_review_2026_11_20.py`.

**2026-11-20 — review A24 (Codex Astra, low effort, of `7a8e3f2..729cd7b`; the author asked for a cross-check of each step by this reviewer).** Eleven findings (RL-564 to RL-574), each run on both sides before its verdict: ten confirmed defects and one for the author.

* **Open:** where a weak reference's callback runs among the values a deriving class's object lets go of (RL-565), with the sweep of that order, the next step; a tuple keeps its hash (RL-574), a step with a case.
* **For the author (RL-572):** a SyntaxWarning of the main program's text, which CPython prints at each start because it compiles the program each time, and a compiled program never.
* **Closed with no change:** RL-552 and RL-553 of A23 (two `__sizeof__` values): the specification's table of what is declared unobservable names `sys.getsizeof` values, and `__sizeof__` is where they come from. The first verdict had called them open without reading that row.
* **Held by:** `pipeline/tests/test_review_2026_11_20_a24.py` (five programs, each wider than the report's), `test_the_line_echoed_for_a_literal_over_the_limit`, part 7 of the lockstep test of the state.

**2026-11-21 — a built-in type's dealloc has its own order (RL-565 of review A24 and the sweep review A23 left, RL-577; no decision of the author's was needed).** The model let an object go in one order whatever its type: the weak references, each class's slots, then the built-in base's fields and the dict. CPython's order depends on who has the list of weak references and the dict: a type written in C clears them in its own dealloc, after the slots and the dict of the classes that derive from it. The model changed first (`RefClass(release=...)`: the type's fields by name, `__weakref__` and `__dict__` at their places), then the header (`set_release`, `dealloc_base`); the selftest on three seeds and `make object-test` hold.
* **What a program sees:** CPython's order and no other: for `class Q(deque)` with slots, the slots' values go before a weak reference's callback runs, and the deque's items after; a `BytesIO`'s attributes go before its weak references; `map` lets go of its iterators before its function. No deviation, no spec wording.
* **The sweep:** 178 rows, each an object dropped under CPython and compiled, with values that record their deaths. 14 differed after the rule and are fixed (RL-577); `types.SimpleNamespace` takes no weak reference now (RL-508 of review S22).
* **Also fixed:** an empty slot's error names the member under whatever name it was looked up (RL-576, found by comparing the outputs of the reviewer's own probes).
* **How:** `docs/design.md`, the paragraph "The order in which a built-in type lets go of what it holds".
* **Held by:** `pipeline/tests/test_release_order.py`; part 8 of the lockstep test of the state; `test_a_built_in_types_dealloc_has_its_own_order` in the model's tests; three programs in `test_review_2026_11_20_a24.py`.

**2026-11-21 — review A25 (Codex Astra, low effort, of `729cd7b..30ad3a9`: the cross-check of the release-order step and of A24's fixes).** Twenty-two findings (RL-578 to RL-599), each program run on both sides before its verdict; all confirmed, most of them older than the range and reached through it.

**2026-11-21 — the compiler's warnings of a main program's text, at each start (the author's decision: A, with a switch; RL-572, case C-8-19, D-29, spec v0.26).** CPython compiles a main program at each start, and its compiler warns each time of what it finds doubtful in the text: `"\d"`, `x is 1`, an assertion of a tuple, a literal called or subscripted. A compiled program gave none; the build printed them, some four times. The author was given three ways (the executable gives the main program's warnings at its start; a deviation under which only the build reports them; the build refuses such a program) and chose the first, "with an option to disable printing that completely".
* **What a program sees:** the `SyntaxWarning`s CPython gives for its main text, the same lines in the same order (the tokenizer's and the parser's in the order of the text, then the code generator's), each with the file, the line and the line's text, on standard error before the first statement, at every start. Its first statement finds what CPython's finds: `warnings` and `linecache` imported for them, the program's lines in `linecache.cache`, no registry among its globals. With standard error closed nothing is written and `linecache` is not imported. A literal past the limit of digits (C-8-18) ends the start after the warnings CPython gives until its parser meets the literal, and before all of the code generator's. A module the program imports gives none: the behaviour CPython has with cached code.
* **The switch (D-29):** `--syntax-warnings=off` on the build's command line (`BELLA_SYNTAX_WARNINGS=off` for the harnesses; `build(..., syntax_warnings=False)`). Such a program never gives them and holds no `warnings` it did not ask for; nothing else changes, and the limit of digits still ends its start. Independent of the fidelity switch: a lean build warns unless it is built with this one too.
* **A program whose text warns holds `warnings`.** CPython imports the module for the first warning shown. Under D-27 a program that imports nothing reaching `warnings` would have had the C's fallback and no module in `sys.modules`; the build knows that this program needs it at every start, so it is compiled in as if the text had imported it. D-27 is as it was for every other program.
* **The build's own output:** the host's compiler warned at every parse and compile the build did, several times a text, and under `--run` on the program's own standard error. That is silenced in `build()`. A build that does not run the program says each warning once, the main program's and those of the program's other modules: where an imported module's warnings are seen, since a compiled program never gives them.
* **Wording added to the specification (v0.26).** The Environment paragraph: "The same compile warns: what CPython's compiler finds doubtful in the main program's text (an escape sequence that is none, a number that runs into a keyword, `is` with a literal, an assertion of a tuple, a literal called or subscripted) is a `SyntaxWarning` on standard error at each start, before the program's first statement, and a compiled program gives the same ones in the same order (C-8-19); a module the program imports gives none, as when CPython runs it from its cached code. A build with `--syntax-warnings=off` gives none at all (D-29)." The register's row D-29 and its note. The harness sentence of section 10: "the file a warning's line names reduced to its base name as a traceback line's is".
* **The harness (`mask_stderr`):** a warning's line that names a file by its whole path is recorded with the base name; its line number, its words and the source line under it are compared. Needed because the case's own text warns, at the start the harness watches, and CPython names the file by its absolute path; no recorded expectation had such a line before.
* **How:** `docs/design.md`, the paragraph "The compiler's warnings at each start". The list is CPython's own, asked of the host at the build, so no rule of the compiler's is written a second time; `return`, `break` and `continue` out of a `finally` block are refused by the build as before (D-10) and never reach it. Filters that make such a warning an error cannot be there at a start while `PYTHONWARNINGS` is not read (D-18); the start stops by name if it meets that, and the step that honours the variable has it on its list.
* **Held by:** C-8-19 on the compiled leg (the two evaluators give the parser's warnings through the host and not the code generator's, so the case is not in their baselines, as C-8-18 is not); four tests in `pipeline/tests/test_start_up_environment.py`: 57 warnings of every kind in one text and ten texts with a coding cookie, a mark of the byte order, the three line ends, a tab, a form feed, a long line and no last line end, each started twice and compared byte for byte; two texts under ten limits of digits; an imported module, with CPython's first and later starts and the build's own note; the switch, by the function, the option and the environment. `test_a_warnings_line_names_its_file_by_the_base_name` for the harness, and the corpus tool refuses to write the compiled baseline from a run with the switch.

**2026-11-21 — a bounded `lru_cache` lets go of its keys and results (RL-600; no decision of the author's was needed).** The prelude kept a bounded cache's links in a ring, each link holding its neighbours, so the ring held itself when the wrapper went: no key and no result of a bounded cache was ever finalised. CPython's links hold borrowed pointers, and the wrapper's end and `cache_clear()` take the ring apart and let the links go oldest first.
* **What a program sees:** CPython's order at the wrapper's end: the weak references' callbacks, the function, the wrapper's own dict, then each link from the oldest, its key before its result; the same for `cache_clear()`. Nothing else of the cache changes (what is kept, what goes when it is full, the counts).
* **How:** a link has an id and names its neighbours by id; the wrapper's last field has the links under their ids, in a dict kept in the ring's order, which is the one reference the ring has to each (`runtime/prelude/_functools.py`, the comment over `_Link`). The wrapper says its dealloc order in `__release__`, the links last. A ring of the runtime's own making, so not one of the cycles of a program's that wait for the collector (RL-222).
* **Held by:** the program RL-600 in `pipeline/tests/test_review_2026_11_21.py` (105 lines of output, the same under CPython); checked with the functools tests, the two sweeps (the order of release, the state without names), four cases that use the cache, and the six compiled targets.

**2026-11-21 — review A26 (Codex Astra, low effort, of `369abde`: the cross-check of the step above).** Eleven findings (RL-601 to RL-611), each run on both sides before its verdict.

The name of the program is the one CPython has, the current directory and the path as given with nothing normalised: `__file__`, the code objects' name and the file a warning names; through a link to a directory the normalised name was another file, whose line stood under the warning (RL-606, older than the step). A program that is one binary with and without the switch keeps it while either kind's last build names it (RL-609). The harness's mask takes the cases' directory off a warning's path and compares every other path as written (RL-608). Where it is another after a warning than before it and the text has a literal of more than 640 digits, the start stops: `bella: not implemented: the limit of digits changed while the compiler's warnings of the main program's text were given, ...`. Exact is in TODO.

A text in an encoding outside the phase-1 codecs has no source line under its warning, since `linecache` cannot decode the file: D-20, no change (RL-613).

**2026-11-21 — a tuple keeps its hash (RL-574 of review A24, case C-6-24; no decision of the author's was needed).** CPython keeps a tuple's hash in the object since 3.14 (`ob_hash`); a compiled program computed it again each time, which a program sees when an element's `__hash__` counts its calls: a second `hash(t)`, and every lookup of a tuple key after the first.
* **What a program sees:** the elements are asked once, however often the tuple is hashed or looked up, alone or inside another tuple, also for a class over tuple and a named tuple; a hash that could not be computed (an element's `__hash__` raised) is not kept, and the next call asks every element again; what was kept is the first answer, whatever an element would say later. The iterators that use their result tuple again forget the hash with the old elements.
* **How:** `Tuple::hash` in the header, read and written by `tuple_hash` (`runtime/src/core.cpp`), forgotten in `_tuple_set` (`runtime/src/imports.cpp`), the one place that changes a tuple that exists. The model has no tuple of its own (its tuples are the host's), so the header is the place.
* **Held by:** C-6-24 on all three legs. Checked: the 24 cases of section 6 compiled, the tests about tuples, hashes, iterators and dicts, the sweep of the order of release, lint, verify (444 of 444), the staging check, the six compiled targets.

* **Fixed, six:** a bounded `lru_cache` whose store fails after the oldest link was taken out lets go of the link with the new result, then the old key, then the old result, as the C does (RL-614, older than RL-600's step); `maxsize` is an index by its type's slot, where the prelude had asked the metaclass (RL-616, older); the warnings the runtime gives itself call the `_warnings` prelude's functions as they were when the module was made, so a program that puts its own function under `_warnings.warn_explicit` while the first of the compiler's warnings is shown does not take the second (RL-615; also the tokenizer's, the import system's and an unclosed file's); the harness's mask takes any class name for a warning's category and needs a file name after the cases' directory (RL-618, RL-620); the program that reaches the pop of the oldest link is in the tests, which RL-600's row did not reach (RL-619).
* **Open, one:** the line under a refused literal's SyntaxError is the build's; CPython reads it from the file at that moment (RL-617; low).
* **The class behind RL-616 (RL-621, open, the next step):** 39 tests on 37 lines in 23 preludes ask `hasattr(type(x), name)` where the C reads a slot of the type. One was run on both sides; the others are the same expression. They get `_type_lookup` and a sweep of their own.
* **The reviewer's saved outputs, compared pairwise:** nothing beyond the report (source lines under traceback frames, D-06; one stop that names itself).
