**2026-11-23 — the exit is `finalize_modules`' sequence (H4, RL-514, C-9-13).** The author decided RL-514 on 2026-11-20: exact, with the collector. The exit walk is gone. In its place, `finalize_at_exit` (`runtime/src/call.cpp`) takes the program apart in CPython's steps, each read from the 3.15.2 source (`Python/pylifecycle.c`, `Python/pystate.c`, `Objects/moduleobject.c`, `Python/gc.c`), and a finaliser sees the step it runs in:

1. `PyGC_Collect`, a collection with everything whole, unless the collector is disabled. Bella had no collection at exit, so a cycle left at exit was never finalised.
2. `_PyImport_FiniExternal` sets `sys.path_importer_cache` and `sys.path_hooks` to None.
3. `finalize_modules`, in order:
   * the special names go and the standard streams are put back;
   * each module of `sys.modules` is replaced by None in its order, and a module nothing else holds goes, its namespace with it if nothing else holds that;
   * `sys.modules` is emptied;
   * the builtins get back the copy taken before the program (no `open`; `__spec__`, `__loader__` and `__package__` None);
   * a collection;
   * the modules still alive, last imported first, have their names set to None, those with one underscore first (`_PyModule_ClearDict`'s two passes);
   * sys's and the builtins' names;
   * a last collection.
4. `finalize_interp_clear` releases the context and the codec registry, then runs the interpreter's own last collection. After it, `gc`'s lists and the warning filters are released; no collection meets their cycles any more.

**Why a trace.** The program's modules and classes are immortal here (D-19, C-3-12), so the cycle collector cannot see through a module's namespace or a class's methods. What a step finds dead is therefore found by a trace in CPython's view:
* a module object holds its namespace;
* a function, a frame, a generator and a traceback entry hold their module's namespace, not the module;
* a class holds its attributes, bases and type;
* every object holds its class.

The trace uses the collector's own traverses, in a mode that also names immortal objects (`detail::tracing_everything`, read in `walked()`). No collection runs and no count is touched while it is on.

**The roots** are what the interpreter holds:
* sys and the builtins, and the builtins' copy;
* the runtime's own modules, together with the hidden namespaces their preludes run in (`hidden_modules()`, which `forget_module` now keeps). Their state is where the codec registry, the warning filters and the context live, as interpreter state is in CPython;
* the intrinsics.

**An exit collection** (`collect_at_exit`, `_PyGC_CollectNoFail`) runs gc.c's sequence over two sets together: the cyclic garbage in the collector's buffer, found by trial deletion as always, and what the module objects reach and the roots do not. It runs whatever `gc.enable` said, and calls no `gc.callbacks`. An object a finaliser makes reachable from the roots again, with what it reaches, is the resurrection check's.

**Module objects die by count.** CPython frees one when its count drops, and the trace stands in for that count. References to each module object are counted over all that lives, and the holders of each namespace with them. A module whose count is nought goes. Its namespace goes too if only the module held it, released entry by entry as `dict_dealloc` releases it. A module value whose count reaches nought on the way dies there and then, before the next entry. `pipeline/tests/test_compiled_imports.py`'s order test caught that: `mb`'s binding of `ma` lets go of `ma` before `mb`'s own globals. A namespace whose last holder dies later goes then. The count is taken again after each namespace that goes.

**sys's names in CPython's places.** `_PySys_SetPreliminaryStderr` places `stderr` and `__stderr__` right after `modules`, and `init_sys_streams` puts the other four at the end, each dunder name first. Wiping sys in that order makes the standard error None before the standard output is let go of, so what the output's close says goes nowhere. `test_signals.py`'s reader-that-has-gone caught it: compiled reported the broken pipe a second time.

**The runtime's own modules.**
* A built-in module's prelude runs in a namespace of its own (`fill_in_python`), so the module the program sees is wiped exactly as CPython wipes a C module's dict, and the prelude's code keeps working.
* The import system's two modules, whose code is also the runtime's, keep their namespace. The program sees a copy with its names None (`Module::program_view`, read by the module's attribute paths and `__dict__`).
* Before sys and the builtins are wiped, every module of the runtime's own gets a copy of the builtins for its code.
* `builtin(name)`, how the runtime's code calls a builtin by name, reads the start-up copy and not the live builtins. The C calls the C function, and after the wipe the builtins are None. `bytes.hex`'s `len` goes through it too, instead of the live dict.

**What is not exact, known and listed:**
* The order within one collection (D-04).
* What only a dead class holds is finalised but not freed, since classes are immortal (C-3-12).
* The state of a C module that CPython frees with its module stays alive here as a root, for example `_csv`'s dialects. No case reaches it.
* While modules are wiped, a module reference released inside a container that dies in the cascade is not counted until the next trace.

The `_abc` caches still hold strong references, the rest of H4 (TODO).

**The case.** C-9-13 has seventeen rows, each a restart of the program with one thing set up:
* a replaced stream;
* a cycle, with the collector on and off;
* a module with no function;
* a global, a class attribute, a resurrected object;
* the restored builtins;
* a module kept from sys;
* weak references with a callback, of the program's and not;
* sys.excepthook (RL-514's row);
* a C module's attribute read at the end;
* the codec registry, a context variable, a warning filter, `gc.callbacks`.

All seventeen agree compiled. eval1 and eval2 do not model the exit; the case is in neither baseline. Three rows I first wrote assigned to the builtins, which E-06 excludes, and were taken out before recording.

**Notes.**
* RL-514's verdict was corrected: the finaliser runs after sys's and the builtins' names are cleared, but with its module's names whole (`__main__` died when sys.modules let go of it, so its namespace was never wiped).
* A question for the author: C-9-13's `alive` row checks an order inside one module's wiping (one underscore first), which D-07 leaves unspecified. D-07 could be narrowed to "within one collection" (D-04), since the exit is now exact.

**2026-11-24 — review A43's fixes, and `_abc` holds weak references (the rest of H4; RL-923 to RL-936, C-7-19, C-9-13).** Review A43 (codex) of the exit step found fourteen differences, all confirmed (review log). Eleven are fixed in the exit's design:

* **Steps where CPython has them.**
  * `sys.is_finalizing()` is true from the end of the pre-finalization calls (threads, atexit), as `_PyInterpreterState_SetFinalizing`, no longer from the start of the modules' removal (RL-927). The import system's own test (`shutting_down()`) is unchanged.
  * `_PyGC_DumpShutdownStats` runs after the collection that follows the builtins' restoring: a `gc.garbage` with something in it, unless `DEBUG_SAVEALL` put it there, is a ResourceWarning of file `gc`, line 0 (RL-923). With `DEBUG_UNCOLLECTABLE`, the list's repr follows through `sys_write_stderr`, which is `PySys_WriteStderr`: 1000 bytes, then `... truncated`, or the C stream when sys.stderr cannot be written.
  * `_Py_Finalize`'s second `flush_std_files` is inside `finalize_at_exit`, right after `finalize_modules`' last collection, on what sys has then (RL-925). That is None unless a late finaliser put a stream there; a failure is status 120, as the first flush's is. The old flush after the interpreter's state, of `_io` streams only and with no status (RL-532, RL-556), is gone. It read the wiped sys, so it did nothing, except where it was wrong.
* **What the runtime's own namespaces held of the program's.**
  * The import system's two namespaces are copied as the program finds them (`namespaces_at_start`). At their wiping, each value that is not the start's is let go of in `_PyModule_ClearDict`'s two passes, and the start's value is put back for the runtime's code (RL-926). The program still sees the copy with its names None.
  * gc's two lists are let go of by the gc prelude's namespace too, the garbage first, as `_PyGC_Fini` lets go of them (RL-928).
* **Module objects by their count.**
  * Each sys.modules entry is one of a module's references, so an alias keeps a module until it is replaced too (RL-936). The count is taken again after a key that is not an exact str, whose hash may run anything.
  * The modules to wipe are listed as their entries are met, a module a finaliser adds on the way included (RL-935).
  * A gone module's namespace goes as soon as only its module holds it, in whatever step that comes (RL-930). `release_pending` checks after each value a wiping or a release lets go of, and after each collection; before, it went at the next trace only. It takes a new count only when a namespace's own count fell to its module's and the last count found no function, frame or generator holding it. Those hold the module, not the namespace, so a namespace they held still waits for the next trace, and the check costs no trace per entry.
  * A module a finaliser makes while sys.modules is emptied gets a count of its own before its entry decides (RL-935's probe showed it): the last count did not know it.
* **What a step runs or raises.**
  * The wiping sets a key that is not an exact str through `dict_set`, which hashes it, as `PyDict_SetItem` does (RL-931).
  * A failure while removing modules, clearing a namespace or restoring the builtins is reported in CPython's words and the next entry follows (RL-934); it ended the process before.
* `bytes.hex` and `bytearray.hex` check their keywords before converting `bytes_per_sep` (RL-924).

**Not fixed here.**
* RL-929 and RL-932 are of classes being immortal, which D-19's summary does not allow: "functions, classes and every other definition-time object are reference-counted like any other". C-3-12 is the case that waits for classes as counted objects; both findings wait with it (TODO).
  * RL-929: what a finaliser's new object holds of a dead class is not kept through the collection.
  * RL-932: a stream that only a dead class's method holds is never finalised. The DECISIONS entry of 2026-11-23 said "finalised but not freed"; a class that no module reaches is not even found dead.
* RL-933, a module whose body raised keeps its globals until the exit, is a question for the author: whether D-19's immortal module objects cover what their namespaces hold.

**`_abc`.** The registry and the two caches hold weak references with a callback, `_destroy`, as `_add_to_weak_set` makes them, so an ABC holds nothing it registered or cached. C-9-13's new row `abc` shows it: an ABC sys keeps no longer keeps a registered class's attribute past the collection after sys.modules is emptied. `_get_dump` returns shallow copies of the three sets, so its stop is gone. Resets and the negative cache's invalidation clear the sets in place.

A look-up makes no reference for a class. CPython looks up with the class's basic weak reference, which its bases' lists of subclasses hold for as long as the class lives, so its hash is computed once and kept, and each entry of that hash is compared by its target's `__eq__`. The runtime keeps each class's hash once computed and compares the targets (`set_contains_by`, set_lookkey with a comparison of the caller's). So a metaclass's `__hash__` and `__eq__` run as CPython runs them, and an `isinstance` against an ABC allocates nothing.

The cost of a look-up:
* A metaclass that keeps `object`'s two methods (`type`, which cannot change, or another by its version) needs no kept hash, and the class itself is a hit without a call.
* A loop of 3,000,000 `isinstance` checks against `Sized` and `Mapping` takes 2.5% longer than with the strong sets (7.4 s against 7.2 s; CPython's is 1.1 s). That is the weak record's look-up per hit; the first version, without the metaclass test kept, was 8% slower. For what is not a class, the reference is made as CPython makes it.

`_weakref` is made when `_abc` first needs its type, if the program has not imported it. That is as `_imp.create_builtin` makes it, so sys.modules has it only once imported (D-30).

The class's basic weak reference itself is not there: `weakref.getweakrefcount(C)` is one less than CPython's, `weakref.ref(C)` is a new object when none is held, and a program's hash of one is not the one `_abc` keeps (TODO). C-7-19's rows count references as differences for that reason.

**2026-11-24 — a class is a counted object (C-3-12; D-19; what was left of 3c's H2; RL-929, RL-932).** D-19's summary says "functions, classes and every other definition-time object are reference-counted like any other"; the runtime's classes were immortal until now. They are counted, the model first (`refmodel/objects.py`, `RefClass`), then the header from it (`object.hpp`), as CPython 3.15.2's heap types are:

* **Who holds a class.** Its creator (type_new's new reference, which `type_new` now hands on instead of taking another), each of its objects (`instance_made`, PyType_GenericAlloc; let go of after the object's memory, in `free_object`, as subtype_dealloc's last step), its subclasses (their MRO and bases), its member descriptors (descr_new's `d_type`), a `__classcell__` cell, a mappingproxy of its dict, and its metaclass's count of its own when the metaclass is the program's.
* **What a class holds.** Its dict, its bases (`bases`, `bases_tuple`), its MRO with itself first (`mro`, `mro_tuple`) and a metaclass of the program's. Since the MRO holds the class, a class never dies by its count alone: a collection finds it (`detail::visit_class`, type_traverse), clears it (`detail::class_clear`, type_clear: the class modified, its dict let go of in its order, then tp_mro) and frees it once its objects are gone (`detail::class_dealloc`, type_dealloc: out of its bases' subclasses and the live classes, its weak references cleared, then the rest). An object's traverse visits its class where subtype_traverse does: after the managed dict, or before a built-in base's own fields.
* **One difference in timing, unobservable.** type_clear lets go of the whole of tp_mro. Here it lets go of the MRO's count of the class itself, which is the cycle, and the others at the dealloc (`RefClass.mro_held`): the class's last object's end walks the MRO's layout (CPython walks tp_base's chain, which type_dealloc lets go of), and tp_bases holds every class of the MRO until then in either, so no class dies at another moment.
* **Immortal still.** A built-in type, and a class the runtime's own code makes: in a prelude's namespace, the bootstrap's, a built-in module's hidden one, or where no frame runs (`runtime_namespace`). Those stand for CPython's C types, and the runtime keeps some by their address (`types().memoryview`, `_weakref.ReferenceType`, sys.flags' type). `note_prelude_class` makes a class immortal too.
* **The address-keyed tables forget a dead class** (`detail::class_forgotten`): an assigned `__name__` or `__qualname__` kept as given, and `_abc`'s kept hash of the class's basic reference.
* **A stop.** A metaclass of the program's with `__del__` would run it at each of its classes' deaths (slot_tp_finalize on the class): not written, so `type_new` stops by name before the class exists. It never ran before either.
* **The exit.** A class dies in the exit's collections once nothing holds it, and what it holds with it: a stream only a dead class's method held is finalised and flushed (RL-932, eight rows). The trace reads a class through its traverse (`reach`), CPython's view: tp_dict's values, tp_bases, tp_mro.
* **What a finaliser makes reachable again, at the exit** (RL-929). The exit's collection checked it by a trace from the interpreter's roots, which a new object a finaliser makes does not reach, so the class it refers to was cleared under it (its method's defaults gone, its `__del__` not found). It is gc.c's reckoning by counts now (`deduce_unreachable` with the module objects found dead, which hold nothing from outside: D-19 gives them no count), and what the roots reach lives on as before. `fresh/bare` had agreed only because the class never died; it agrees by the same reckoning.

**A known gap.** A mappingproxy of a class's dict (`C.__dict__`, a class body's `__classdictcell__`) holds the class here; CPython's holds the class's dict only. A class whose own dict names it (a `__dict__` or `__weakref__` getset, a member, a method's `__class__` cell: most classes) is kept by the proxy in either. One whose dict does not (a subclass that adds neither a dict nor slots, of a class that has them) dies in CPython while the proxy is held, its dict emptied by type_clear, and lives on here until the proxy goes (TODO).

**The lockstep binding** collects at each world's end: the model's worlds each have a heap of their own, and a cycle a world did not collect now keeps its classes, which before let go of their attributes unconditionally.

**Cost.** One count more per object made and let go of (the class's, as CPython's), a test of `cleared` in a class's lookup, and a class among the candidates of each collection after one of its objects dies. Measured against `de36608` over loops of 3,000,000 (the median of five runs) and by callgrind over 200,000 (instructions without the start's):
* making and dropping an instance takes 12.3 s against 12.5 s (0.7% fewer instructions);
* the same with `__slots__`, 8.1 s against 8.0 s (0.2% fewer);
* a method call on one instance, 3.48 s against 3.46 s (0.3% more, about 16 instructions a call).

CPython's are 0.60, 0.55 and 0.19 s. The first build was 3% slower on the method call, and not for the test: `ops.cpp` is at GCC's limit for a unit's growth by inlining (`--param inline-unit-growth`, 2,062 calls refused at `de36608`, 2,081 after), and the header's new code made GCC refuse `ClassObject::lookup` in `plan_generic` (twice) and the two descriptor tests, four calls a method call. So `lookup` is `[[gnu::always_inline]]` (sixteen call sites); the class's paths that run only in a collection or at a class's death are out of line (`class_clear`, `class_dealloc`, `release_class_dict`, `ClassObject::dispose`, `visit_class`); and `free_object` lets go of the class with a plain call, a guard only on its buffered path, whose compaction can throw.

**2026-11-24 — a mappingproxy of a class's dict holds the dict, not the class (C-3-82; the gap C-3-12's entry left; no decision of the author's was needed).** CPython's `type.__dict__` is a mappingproxy over `tp_dict` (`type_dict`), and so is the cell an annotation scope reads a class's names from (`type_new_set_classdictcell` stores `tp_dict` itself; RL-489 keeps a proxy here). So the proxy keeps the dict and not the class: a class whose dict names nothing of it (no method's `__class__` cell, no slot, no `__dict__` or `__weakref__` descriptor of its own) dies in a collection while its proxy is held, and the proxy shows the dict as `type_clear` left it, empty. The runtime's proxy held the class and read its attributes. Now a class's proxy is a proxy over the class's dict as an object (`ClassObject::dict_view`, made at the first asking and kept in step with the attributes), which `release_class_dict` empties in place when the class dies; the proxy reads it as CPython's reads an exact dict (`proxy_exact_dict`: `[]`, `in`, `get`, `len` and `mapping_get` without a method call). An immortal class's proxy (a built-in type's, one the runtime's own code made) still reads the class's attributes live through a pointer it does not count (`Proxy::of`, `class_proxy`): such a class never dies, and the runtime writes a built-in type's attributes in place as it goes (`no_instances_yet`, the types' set-ups), around `set_attr`, which a view would miss; the first version read every class through its view, and C-3-56 and seven tests of three modules that read built-in types' dicts failed (`test_descriptors`, `test_missing_names`, `test_functools`). A prelude's class is counted while its class statement runs and made immortal after (`note_prelude_class`), so its `__classdictcell__` (an annotated method gives a class one) made its view before the runtime presented the type; an immortal class's view is therefore brought in step with its attributes when it is asked for (`class_dict_of`, in place, so that its holders see it), which only an iteration of a built-in type's dict does. The model's `_release_dict` empties its view in place too, with a test of its own (`test_a_class_dict_outlives_its_class_c_3_82`). C-3-82 passes compiled: a class dropped, kept by an object, by a subclass, a weak reference's callback, a proxy over a dict that names nothing of its class and one that does, an iterator and views taken before the class's death (the iterator's next is CPython's `RuntimeError`), and an annotation scope reading a class-level name after its class died (`NameError`). It passes under eval2 too; under eval1 the `annotated` row's class lives until its annotate function goes, so it joins the compiled and eval2 baselines only.

**Cost**, measured against the build before (the median of five runs):
* reading through `C.__dict__` (`get`, `[]`, `in`, `len`, 1,000,000 times) is 9% faster (2.00 s against 2.20 s), the dict read in place;
* a class attribute stored after the class's `__dict__` was taken costs 3% more (0.79 s against 0.77 s for 1,000,000): the view exists from the first `__dict__` now, where before a proxy made it only when iterated;
* making a class with an annotation, in its body or in a method's signature, costs 10% more (3.19 s against 2.89 s for 100,000): its `__classdictcell__`'s proxy makes the view at the class's creation.

Most of the last two is the collector's: the view is one more container per class, listed and traversed in each collection, as CPython's `tp_dict` is in CPython's. The view is made at its final size (`class_dict_of`), which took two points off. A variant without the view would have to visit the same values from the proxy to keep CPython's reachability, so it would cost the collector the same: there is no form of this fix without the cost.

**2026-11-24 — review A44's fixes (RL-937 to RL-942).** Review A44 (codex) of classes as counted objects and of A43's fixes found six differences, all confirmed (review log); each is fixed as CPython 3.15.2's source has it, and `pipeline/tests/test_review_2026_11_24_a44.py` holds the reviewer's rows with their controls:
* **What a metaclass's `mro()` sees** (RL-940, RL-941; the model first: `RefClass(mro_hook=True)`). `type_ready` calls `mro_internal` with `tp_mro` NULL and adds the class to its bases' subclasses only after it, so the hook sees `__mro__` None, a look-up on the class that finds nothing and a class its bases do not list; a class the hook fails for holds no MRO, so its creator's letting go frees it at once; and the hook may give an order C3 has none of. A class whose metaclass is not `type` now waits for its MRO (`ClassSpec::mro_hook`, `ClassObject::set_mro`), its layout read in C3's order or, where C3 has none, the bases' orders (`layout_order`; CPython reads the layout along `tp_base`, the same fields wherever C3 has an order, since one base alone may have a layout). Meanwhile `is_subclass` walks `tp_base`'s chain (`type_is_subtype_base_chain`), and `type.mro()` raises CPython's message where C3 has no order (`set_mro_error`), which before only `type_new` did. `mro_invoke` keeps the tuple it made of the result as `__mro__` (the very object when it was a tuple), and a tuple read before goes.
* **`_abc`'s kept hash** (RL-938). The class's basic weak reference keeps its hash from the first look-up in a set that is not empty, whatever the metaclass's `__hash__` becomes; the runtime kept one only under a metaclass of the program's. The hash is the class's own field now (`ClassObject::reference_hash`), filled on either path, which replaces the table by address and its forgetting.
* **What a class holds**: the names of its `__slots__` as the program gave them, where one is an object of a str subclass's (`ht_slots`, `ClassObject::slot_names`, let go of at the dealloc after the class's names: RL-939); and under `DEBUG_SAVEALL` its dict and its MRO's tuple join `gc.garbage` with it, made if the program never asked for them (RL-937).
* **The exit's first reckoning** (RL-942). The exit's collections put everything a lost module reached among the unreachable by a trace, before any count; an assigned class name, which `type_traverse` does not visit (`ht_name`), counts as held from outside in CPython, and what it reaches lives. The first marking is now reckoned by counts as RL-929 made the second: trial deletion's result and what the lost modules reach, the modules subtracted as dead holders (`collect_at_exit`). Two things had to be CPython's for that: what is reachable reaches what CPython's view adds (a function, a frame or a traceback entry its module's namespace, which here has no count and is subtracted nowhere: `deduce_unreachable(..., cpython_view)`), and the trace's class names the objects the runtime has of its dict and its tuples, whose counts would otherwise look like holds from outside (the first try kept 1,912 objects of the library's modules alive). And the two tables of given names are never destroyed: CPython frees nothing alive at its end, and the tables' C++ teardown ran a name's finaliser after the interpreter was gone. No other static table of the runtime's holds an object a program made.

Cost: none measured. Five alternating runs of one program against `6ed9635` (minimums): 3 million turns of `isinstance(x, Sized) + isinstance(d, Mapping)` 13.00 s against 13.31 s (the hash a field of the class's instead of a table by address), of `isinstance` with a plain class 3.21 s against 3.24 s (`is_subclass`'s new branch is one test of an empty MRO), 30 000 classes made by `ABCMeta(...)` 2.20 s against 2.21 s.

**2026-11-24 — Question 32 answered by the author: B (what closes increment 1c).** D-7 made phase 1 the easy three, each stage closed by its target agreeing with its recording, and 1c "the rest of the manifest" (28 modules, `targets/closure-summary.json`), with no criterion of its own: six of them are reached by the tricky three, 22 by no target. Measured the day it was put, with one program a module printing each public name's type, module and callability: 21 of the 28 agree with CPython, `secrets` differs as D-20 decides, `asyncio` and `socket` stop at their import (their shims are not written), `unittest` and `unittest.async_case` stop through `asyncio`, and `marshal` and `zipimport` stop at their import (Question 33). The options were A (every manifest module imports compiled and its names agree, except where a decision says otherwise; the corpus passes compiled; the six targets still agree), B (A, and at least one case passing compiled for each \[P1\] row of the spec that 1c's modules belong to) and C (A, and a seventh target program). The author's words: "Q32: 1c closes with B". So 1c's gate is: the sweep of the manifest's modules agrees but where a deviation, an exclusion or \[P2+\] decides otherwise; every \[P1\] row 1c touches has a case passing compiled (missing on the day: t-strings, `secrets`, `uuid`, `unittest`'s runner and loader, `logging`'s handlers and formatters); the compiled corpus passes; the six targets agree; and the claim is made after a full run. The sweep becomes a test with the step that closes 1c.

**2026-11-24 — Question 33 answered by the author: A (`marshal`; D-31, spec v0.30).** `marshal` is a core module of the manifest that a compiled program could not import, and nothing in the closure imports it but CPython's own `importlib._bootstrap_external`, which the runtime's import system replaces. At version 3 and above `dumps` flags an object it may meet again by its reference count (`w_ref`: `_PyObject_IsUniquelyReferenced`, interned strs always), so CPython's bytes depend on who else holds a value: measured on 3.15.2, a list a global holds is flagged (`db…`), a temporary or a function's local is not (`5b…`; 3.15 borrows a local's reference), an int inside a literal list is (the code's constants hold it), and an outside holder renumbers the back-references after it, at equal length and with equal values loaded. The options were A (a port with the flags by the runtime's own counts, a deviation), B (a port with the flags by a rule over the value alone, a deviation that differs more often), C (`dumps` at version 3 and above stopping by name) and D (`marshal` excluded). The author's words: "A". So D-31 (`docs/deviations.toml`, spec section 11) and the spec's row (v0.30): `marshal` is \[P1\] but for code objects, which stop by name (E-02); `loads`, `load`, and `dumps` at version 2 and below are exact. The question in TODO had said `marshal.dumps((1, 'a'))` flags the tuple and that the version is 5; measured, the tuple is not flagged and the version is 6.

**2026-11-24 — Question 34 answered by the author: A (`zipimport`; spec v0.31).** `zipimport` is CPython's importer for modules in zip archives, Python frozen into the interpreter, which CPython imports at its start and puts on `sys.path_hooks`; a compiled program could not import it, and nothing in the closure does. It has two halves: reading an archive (plain Python over `_io`, `time`, `zlib` and the import system's helpers, which the runtime's own `bootstrap_external.py` has) and making code (`get_code`, `exec_module`, `load_module`: `compile` of a source, E-01, or a `.pyc`'s code object through `marshal`, E-02). An archive on `sys.path` is imported from under CPython and not here under any option (E-04; the closed world resolves imports at build time). The options were A (CPython's module compiled with the program, the code-making half stopping by name), B (a stub whose every call stops) and C (`zipimport` added to E-04). The author's words: "A". So `zipimport` is \[P1\] for reading an archive, after `marshal` (Question 33), which it imports at its top.

**2026-11-24 — Question 35 answered by the author: A (`__sizeof__`, `sys.getsizeof`, `__basicsize__` and `__itemsize__`).** CPython's `sys.getsizeof(x)` calls `type(x).__sizeof__` (`_PySys_GetSizeOf`: a TypeError when the type has none, a ValueError when it is negative) and adds what lies before the object (`_PyType_PreHeaderSize`: the collector's header, and for a heap type the managed dict and weak-reference slots); its `__sizeof__` is its own C struct (`tp_basicsize`, and `tp_itemsize` per item) plus what the object owns. Measured the day it was put: CPython gives 56, 88, 43 and 40 for `[]`, `[1, 2, 3]`, `"ab"` and `10**30`, where the compiled program gave 64 for each (the runtime had no `object.__sizeof__`, so `sys.getsizeof` returned a flat 64 for every object whose type had none, `runtime/src/call.cpp`); the `__sizeof__` of 163 built-in types stopped by name, and so did `__basicsize__` and `__itemsize__`; and a program's own `__sizeof__` returning 100 gave 132 under CPython and 116 compiled (the runtime added 16, CPython 32). The spec's section 2 already lists "`sys.getsizeof` values" as declared unobservable, and RL-552 and RL-553 (review A23) were closed on that reading; the question in TODO had offered the runtime's own sizes only as a registered deviation, which missed it. The options were A (the runtime's own sizes: `object.__sizeof__` and a size per kind where storage varies give the bytes the compiled object occupies, `sys.getsizeof` adds the runtime's header before the object and the flat 64 goes, `__basicsize__` and `__itemsize__` give the runtime's struct sizes), B (CPython's figures from a table of its 3.15 layouts, with models of a list's over-allocation and a dict's table sizes) and C (all four stop by name, and the flat 64 goes). The author's words: "A". So no deviation: the numbers are the compiled program's own, the relations a program may rely on hold (a longer list is not smaller, a program's `__sizeof__` is what `getsizeof` starts from), and the errors are CPython's (a negative size is a ValueError; a type whose `__sizeof__` is not callable raises as CPython's does). The spec's row names `sys.getsizeof` values; `__sizeof__`, `__basicsize__` and `__itemsize__` are read as covered by it, being the numbers `getsizeof` is made from (the row's words are the author's: put with Question 37's wordings). Not decided by this: RL-592, whether the default pickling state of an instance of a C type with fields raises, which CPython decides from `tp_basicsize` and which is observable; it needs CPython's facts about which types have fields beyond `object`'s, as a table inside the runtime, whatever number `__basicsize__` shows.

**2026-11-24 — Question 36 answered by the author: B (the program's module objects are counted; RL-933; D-19).** D-19 says "module objects are immortal; functions, classes and every other definition-time object are reference-counted like any other", on the premise (spec section 11) that "a module can leave `sys.modules` only through E-04, which is excluded". The premise is false in two ways: the import system takes a module out of `sys.modules` when its body raises (`_load_unlocked`'s `del sys.modules[spec.name]`, CPython's text, which the runtime's `bootstrap.py` runs), and a module the program makes with `types.ModuleType` was never in it. Measured the day it was put (CPython 3.15.2 against the compiled program at `fbe5df4`): a failed import's globals, with no cycle, are dropped by CPython when the `except` clause ends (the traceback held the module's frame and its globals), with a class defined in the module (its methods' `__globals__` make a cycle) at `gc.collect()`, and compiled both at the exit; a `ModuleType` dropped by `del m` dies at once under CPython, its weak reference dead, and compiled at the exit, its weak reference alive; a function kept from a failed import reads its own namespace after a successful re-import in both. So finalisers, weak-reference callbacks and an unclosed file's ResourceWarning of such a module come at the exit, and a program that retries a failing import or makes `ModuleType` objects in a loop grows without bound. The options were A (keep, D-19's wording corrected to say so), B (the program's module objects counted, as C-3-12 counted classes, the runtime's own modules immortal still; D-19 narrowed) and C (A now, B after 1c). The author's words: "B seems valid". So B, next after this round of questions and before the 1c ports (TODO): the model first, then the header; the exit's reckoning for uncounted modules replaced by the counts; the cost measured as the class step's was. One residue is known before the step: compiled code reaches its globals through the module object (`fn->module`), so a function, a frame or a traceback entry keeps the module object alive where CPython's keeps only its dict, seen only by a weak reference to the module or a `ModuleType` subclass's `__del__`; it is registered under a narrowed D-19 with the step. D-19's words and the spec's sentence change with the step, their wording put to the author then.

**2026-11-24 — Question 29 answered by the author: A (a starred tuple reaches the callee as itself; RL-626).** CPython's `f(*t)` with an exact tuple hands `t` itself to `PyObject_Call` (`_DO_CALL_FUNCTION_EX`), so a callable written in C that takes its arguments as a tuple is given the caller's object, and three keep it: an exception's `args` (`BaseException_new`: `self->args = Py_NewRef(args)`), the untyped `lru_cache`'s key with no keywords (`lru_cache_make_key`'s short path), and `operator.itemgetter`'s items. A static exception type whose `tp_new` and `tp_init` are BaseException's is given `BaseException_vectorcall` at start (`_PyExc_InitTypes`), which makes a new tuple, so `Exception(*t)`, `KeyError(*t)` and `OSError(*t)` do not keep `t` while `StopIteration(*t)` and every exception class of the program's do. The runtime's `call_ex` copied the tuple's items at every starred call, so no callee saw `t`. Measured on 2026-11-21 and again the day it was put, 30 rows: `MyError(*t).args is t`, `StopIteration`'s, `self.args is args` after `super().__init__(*args)`, `e.__init__(*t)`, the exception's reduce, `itemgetter`'s reduce and `partial(MyError)(*t)` True under CPython and False compiled; a prehashed key of two items given twice to an untyped cache 0 `__hash__` calls under CPython and 4 compiled (one item: 0 and 2; `maxsize=2`: 0 and 4), since the copy has lost the hash the tuple keeps (RL-574); typed or keyword caches, the built-in exception classes with the vectorcall, a function written in Python, two stars, a list and a tuple subclass agree. Unlike D-03 (identity true more often than in CPython), this is identity true less often, and the cache's row is how often a program's own `__hash__` runs. The options were A (exact: `call_ex` passes the tuple's own items and notes the tuple for the call, the keepers take it when their arguments are exactly its items with no keywords, and a built-in exception class makes a new tuple when its `__new__` and `__init__` are BaseException's, read from the runtime's class dicts) and B (a deviation beside D-03 with a lint rule for `.args is`, naming the cache's extra `__hash__` calls). The author's words: "A". So exact, case first (TODO); a call without a star does not change, and the starred call loses a copy.

**2026-11-24 — Question 30 answered by the author: A (a call hands its temporaries over; RL-695).** CPython's call of a Python function moves each argument from the caller's stack into the callee's frame (`_PyEvalFramePushAndInit`: "Consumes references to func, locals and all the args"), so a parameter given a temporary is its only reference. The compiled call kept the argument in a temporary of the caller until the call returned (`t9 = rt::invoke(t7.get(), {t8.get()}); t8 = Ref{};`), the parameter a second reference, so a callee that lets go of its parameter early (`del x`, `x = None`, `x = transform(x)`) did not finalise it: the caller's temporary did, after the return. Section 9 promises the CPython moment ("a program may rely on an object being finalised when its last reference goes away, exactly as it may under CPython"), and no deviation covered it (D-04 is cycles, D-07 module globals at the exit). Measured on 2026-11-21 and again the day it was put, 13 shapes, 8 differing: a parameter deleted or rebound, two in turn (compiled both at the return, the second first), `*args` deleted, `**kwds` cleared, a method's argument, `f(g(X()))`, a weak reference in the callee alive after `del x`; agreeing: a caller's variable, an instance's `__call__` (CPython keeps a tuple until the return), a lambda passing it on, `X().n` and `isinstance(X(), int)`. The options were A (exact: the compiler marks the temporaries and moves them into the call, a Python function's binding takes the caller's reference, the caller lets go only of what was not taken, a built-in takes nothing, a failed binding releases in the frame's order), B (a deviation of lifetime beside D-04, with a lint rule for a parameter deleted or rebound in a function called with a temporary) and C (A for direct calls' positional and keyword arguments, the starred forms as they were). The author's words: "lets go for A". So exact (TODO), the starred forms with Question 29's `call_ex` change; the cost measured, expected neutral (one count up and down less per moved argument against a test of the owned mark).

**2026-11-24 — Question 31 answered by the author: A (`TextIOWrapper.tell` after a seek; D-32, spec v0.32; RL-756).** To find a position, `tell` decodes again part of the bytes it last read, from a snapshot, and estimates how many from the last chunk's ratio of bytes to characters (`skip_bytes = b2cratio * chars_to_skip`). After a `seek` the snapshot is only what the seek fed back in while the ratio is still the old chunk's, so the estimate can exceed the snapshot. CPython's C asserts it cannot (`Modules/_io/textio.c:2949`, `assert(skip_bytes <= PyBytes_GET_SIZE(next_input))`); a release build goes on and hands the decoder `skip_bytes` bytes from the snapshot, first the NUL that ends every bytes object. The prelude caps the estimate at the snapshot (RL-347, 2026-11-13). Measured the day it was put, with a decoder of two bytes at a time, a three-byte chunk and `t.read(1); t.seek(t.tell()); t.read(1); t.tell()` over `b"a\r\nb"`: CPython's last `tell` calls `decode(b'a\r\x00', False)`, `getstate()` and `setstate((b'', 3))` before `decode(b'a\r', False)`, the compiled program only the last, and both return 55340232221128654850; CPython's own `_pyio` raises AssertionError at the same point (`Lib/_pyio.py:2444`), and with `-O` makes the compiled program's calls. The options were A (a deviation: `tell` never hands a decoder bytes that are not the stream's), B (the one NUL copied where the estimate is one byte too long, longer estimates capped and registered) and C (open until upstream settles it). The author's words: "A please, and let's add a note to ourselves to report this issue upstream to cpython team". So D-32 (`docs/deviations.toml`, spec section 11, v0.32), no lint rule (a stale ratio cannot be seen in the source), nothing to implement; and a TODO item (Housekeeping) to report it upstream: the tracker searched and the main branch checked first, the reproduction reduced, the issue text drafted and shown to the author, and filed from the author's account only on the author's go-ahead.

**2026-11-24 — Question 38 answered by the author: B (what D-17's recursion limit counts; spec v0.34; RL-639, RL-883).** CPython counts two entries of the recursion limit for a class's call that enters a Python `__init__` once the site is specialised (`CALL_ALLOC_AND_ENTER_INIT` pushes a frame of its own beside `__init__`'s) and one for a site's first calls, before it specialises; its import system is Python, and its frames count, several a fresh import. A compiled program counted one entry a call, and the runtime's import system, a prelude, none. And the spec's Resources sentence said CPython reaches the stack's bound "later or not at all", where a recursion whose every level goes through C (`lru_cache`, `partial`) reaches it earlier on CPython's 8 MiB native stack. Measured the day it was put (`ulimit -s` 8192): through `Made()`, whose `__init__` calls `Made()`, under a limit of 60 run twice, CPython 30 then 29 and compiled 58 and 58; under 150, 74 and 148; a plain function under 60, 58 on both; an `lru_cache` recursion 20 000 deep under a limit of 100 000, RecursionError under CPython and 20000 compiled. The options were A (words only: the counter's one entry a call, and the constructor, a site's first calls and the import frames named) and B (two entries for a class's call that enters a Python `__init__`, agreeing from a site's third call; words for the rest). The author's words: "B". So D-17's summary and section 11's row say what the counter counts and where CPython counts otherwise, and section 8 says CPython reaches the stack's bound "at another depth, or not at all" (v0.34); the count of two is a step (TODO), with the recursion-count test's `__init__` row compared from a site's third call. Copying CPython's warm-up was not offered: it follows its adaptive interpreter's counters per instruction.

**2026-11-24 — Question 39 answered by the author: B (D-07 kept and sharpened; spec v0.35).** D-07 said the "finalisation order of module globals at exit" is unspecified. Since RL-514 ("exact, with the collector") the exit is CPython 3.15.2's own sequence (H4: `_Py_Finalize`, `finalize_modules`' steps, `_PyModule_ClearDict`'s two passes), and C-9-13's rows agree compiled, one of them (`alive`) checking the order inside a module's wiping that D-07 leaves free. The options were A (D-07 retired, the exit's order promised as CPython's) and B (D-07 kept, the case's order rows taken out). Recommended was A; the author asked whether B would keep optimisations open that A blocks ("if the order does not matter (programs don't care as cpython does not promise) going for A would block otherwise valid opportunities in future"), and it does: CPython's language reference does not guarantee that `__del__` runs for objects alive at exit and warns that a finaliser's globals may be deleted or None, the order has changed between CPython versions, and a faster exit (finalising what needs it without the module-by-module sequence) would be ruled out by A. What must stay CPython's is which objects are finalised: a file left open is flushed and closed during the exit's wiping, and programs rely on it. So B, sharpened; the author's words: "yes, B as listed above". Section 9: which of the objects still alive at exit are finalised is CPython's, the order among them and the step of the exit at which each finaliser runs (what it still finds of its module's globals, `sys` and the builtins) are unspecified; section 2's row and section 11's say the same, and D-07's note says the runtime follows CPython 3.15.2's order today and that a change relaxing it gets an entry here (v0.35). C-9-13 is split (TODO): the rows' promise (which finalisers run, which never do, a weak reference's callback called) stays the case, recorded again from CPython; their order and steps move to a design test that holds the runtime's current choice. The split is shown to the author before anything moves.

Done the same day, the author's words on the split shown: "if there is no simpler way, please do". The simpler way taken: the finalisers are as they were, and only the case's parent changes, printing each row's lines reduced to their tags and sorted, or all of them in order when BELLA_CASE_STEPS is set; so the design test, `pipeline/tests/test_exit_steps.py`, is the same case run with the switch, nothing copied. One line was added so the `hook` row's asker shows its finalising, which before showed only through a `print` that fails at that step (`asker: finalised`, written with `os.write`). The case was recorded again with CPython 3.15.2 (its steps output is the old record and that line), passes under CPython from btrfs and tmpfs and compiled, and the design test passes.

**2026-11-25 — The program's module objects are counted (Question 36: B; RL-933; C-9-14).** Done as the decision said, the model first (`refmodel/objects.py`: `RefModule`, `RefFunction`, `hold_globals`, `release_globals`; `test_a_module_is_a_counted_object`, CPython's story beside the model's; the oracle's worlds make modules, define functions in them, store into their namespaces, drop and weakly refer to them, and put them in the interpreter's table, in `refmodel.selftest` on eight seeds and as rules of the stateful test), then the header from it. A module of the program's (an import's, a `types.ModuleType`'s, `__main__`) is counted from its making; the runtime's own (sys, builtins, CPython's C modules, the preludes' and hidden namespaces) stay immortal.
* **No function keeps its module.** The question foresaw a residue: compiled code reaches its globals through the module object (`fn->module`), so a function, frame or traceback entry would keep the module object where CPython's keeps only its dict. It does not arise: what runs in a module (a function, a frame object, a traceback entry) holds the module's namespace, as CPython's holds `func_globals`, and finds it through the module object without being one of its references. The module's count is CPython's; `holders` counts what holds its namespace as its globals; the module dies when CPython's does (`module_dealloc`: its weak references cleared, its namespace let go of) and what is left of it, its holders' way to their globals, goes with the last of them, dead to every reference. A function the collector clears lets go of its globals (`func_clear`), and a frame object of them first (`frame_dealloc`'s `f_funcobj`). `__main__` is held by the start while the program runs, as `_PyRun_SimpleFileObject` holds it.
* **The exit goes by the counts.** The trace in CPython's view that stood in for the uncounted modules (`ExitView`, its reckoning of each module's referrers and of what holds each namespace, `module_goes`, `settle`, `release_pending`; the collector's `dead` and `cpython_view`, and the header's `tracing_everything`) is gone: each step of `finalize_modules` lets go of what CPython's does and what dies goes then. `finalize_remove_modules` keeps each module weakly as CPython does: a serial number per counted module tells which are alive at `finalize_modules_clear_weaklist`, and the module's basic weak reference is made too once the program has loaded `_weakref` (before that nothing the program runs can count a module's weak references).
* **The collector does not walk a rooted namespace.** Measured first without it, a loop that made cycles (a collection every 2,000 candidates) took 6.3% more instructions: every function among a collection's candidates walked the program's whole module graph through its globals, where the walk had stopped at an immortal module. A namespace whose module `sys.modules` holds under its own name is reachable from outside, and so is all it reaches, so the collector does not walk it from the module or from what holds it as its globals: trial deletion finds the same groups in the same order (what it leaves out is held from outside). The look-up runs no code of the program's (an exact str, compared as `unicode_eq` compares; a same-hash key of another kind ends it). In the model first (`RefModule.rooted`), checked by the selftest.
* **Cost**, against `6f0f149`, by callgrind over 200,000 turns with the start and the exit taken out, and the median of five over 3,000,000: a function made and dropped, 0.6% more instructions (2% more time: its namespace's count taken and let go of, as CPython's `func_globals`; the common case inline); a bound method made and dropped, 0.5% fewer; the loop of cycles, 0.6% fewer; a module's value loaded in a loop (`os.path.join`), unchanged; the start and the exit of a program that imports json, argparse and collections, 51% fewer instructions (0.22 s to 0.12 s), the exit's trace being gone.
* **What is left.** A module whose body raises dies when the import that ran it ends, where CPython's lives until the exception goes: CPython's import system is Python (`_bootstrap`), and the failed module's frame object, which the traceback holds, keeps those frames as its `f_back`, so `_load_unlocked`'s `module` keeps the module. The runtime's import system runs as the preludes do, without frames of its own (`Code::shown`), so nothing keeps their values once they end. Seen only through a weak reference to the module object (a `ModuleType` subclass, the other way, stops by name); the namespace dies when CPython's does. C-9-14's rows `failed caught` and `failed class caught` show it, so the case waits for the author before it joins the compiled baseline (Question 43, with D-19's words). RL-933's own row (`exit/failed` of review A43) agrees now and joins the A43 test.

**2026-11-25 — review A45's fixes (RL-943, RL-945, RL-947); two findings for the author (RL-944, RL-946).** Review A45 (codex) of the program's module objects counted and the steps batched with it found five differences, all confirmed (review log). Three are fixed as CPython 3.15.2's source has it, and `pipeline/tests/test_review_2026_11_25_a45.py` holds the reviewer's rows with controls:
* **A text's locals are its top-level frame's** (RL-945, of Question 36's step). CPython's function takes its globals and builtins, never the locals the text that made it ran with; those are the text's top-level frame's `f_locals`, kept by its frame object when the frame ends while held (`take_ownership`). `hold_counted_globals` took a staged text's locals for every holder, so `dataclass`'s `__create_fn__`, which only the locals of `_create_fn`'s text hold, lived as long as the methods it made and went to `gc.garbage` with them under `DEBUG_SAVEALL`. The model's function holds the namespace alone, so the header had gone beyond the model, which needs no change: a holder now takes the globals and builtins only (its inline path tests the builtins alone), the text's record lets go of its locals with its namespace, the collector visits them from the record only, and the text's top-level frame object takes them as its `f_locals` when the frame ends while held, where traceback's suggestion reads them once the record has let go.
* **A module's name** (RL-943). `module_init_dict` keeps `md_name` only for an exact str, so a str subclass's object given as the name is held by the namespace alone and goes when `__name__` is replaced; the runtime kept it as the module's name too. Now only an exact str is.
* **`dir()` of a module** (RL-947). `module_dir` returns the namespace's keys (`PyDict_Keys`), hashing nothing; `module_dir_m` put them into a new dict to merge a native module's names with CPython's table, which ran a str subclass's `__hash__`. The keys are listed as they are now, and a native module's names of CPython's that are not written yet follow them, found missing by their text.

Two are the author's, put after Question 43: a class's dict reached through the proxy's reflected `|` (`mappingproxy_or` hands the reflected operand the class's dict itself) and written, which a look-up on the class does not see because the class's attributes are its own store and the dict a view of them (C-3-82; RL-944: an exclusion beside E-05, or writes to the view taken into the store); and a module's `__builtins__` rebound, which CPython's functions defined after take as their builtins and the runtime does not honour at all (RL-946: E-06's words widened, or honoured).
